Java — Concept Notes

Interview Q&A

The Java language from the ground up to senior level: OOP, core APIs, functional Java, concurrency and the JVM, in easy words and full depth. Written for Java 21; every code example has been compiled and run. Print this page for a PDF.

Part 1 — Java Basics

1. What Java Is: JDK, JRE & JVM

In plain words

Java is two things at once: a programming language (the code you type) and a platform (the software that runs that code). You write .java files, a compiler turns them into bytecode — instructions for an imaginary computer — and a program called the JVM (Java Virtual Machine) runs that bytecode on the real computer.

Three names come up all the time. The JVM is the engine that runs bytecode. The JRE (Java Runtime Environment) is the JVM plus the standard library — everything you need to run Java programs. The JDK (Java Development Kit) is the JRE plus developer tools such as the compiler javac — everything you need to write Java programs.

Think of a game console. The bytecode is the game disc: the same disc works on every console of that model. The JVM is the console itself — a different circuit board in each country, but it plays the same disc. The JRE is the console plus the cables and controllers you need to actually play. The JDK is the studio kit game makers use to build and test new discs.

JDK vs JRE vs JVM

JVMJREJDK
What it isAn abstract machine (a spec) plus its implementation that executes bytecodeJVM + standard class libraryJRE + compiler and developer tools
Main jobLoad, verify and execute .class files; manage memory (GC); run threadsProvide everything a program needs at run timeCompile, package, debug, document, profile
Contains javac?NoNoYes
Platform-specific?Yes — one build per OS/CPUYesYes
Who needs itEvery machine that runs JavaServers/users that only run apps (historically)Developers and build servers
Modern detail: since Java 11, Oracle and most vendors no longer ship a separate JRE download. You install a JDK, and to ship a small runtime you build a custom one with jlink (Java 9 module system) containing only the modules your app uses. "JRE" is still a useful concept in interviews.

Bytecode and "write once, run anywhere"

C or C++ compile straight to machine code for one CPU and OS; to run on another platform you recompile. Java adds a middle step: javac produces bytecode, a compact, platform-neutral instruction set stored in .class files. Each platform has its own JVM that understands that same bytecode. So the same .class or .jar file runs on Windows, macOS and Linux without recompiling — "write once, run anywhere" (WORA).

+--> JVM for Windows x64 --> Windows machine code Hello.java --javac--> Hello.class --+--> JVM for macOS ARM64 --> Apple silicon machine code (source) (bytecode, +--> JVM for Linux x64 --> Linux machine code same file) compile ONCE one JVM per platform, bytecode unchanged
  • Bytecode is platform independent; the JVM is not. This is the most common trick question in this area.
  • Bytecode is not only for Java. Kotlin, Scala, Groovy and Clojure also compile to .class files, so they run on the same JVM and can call Java libraries.
  • WORA has limits: native code (JNI), OS-specific file paths (\ vs /) and line endings can still make a program behave differently. Use Path.of(...) and System.lineSeparator().

Is Java compiled or interpreted?

Both. Source is compiled ahead of time to bytecode by javac. At run time the JVM first interprets bytecode (reads and executes it instruction by instruction), and the JIT (Just-In-Time) compiler turns frequently-run "hot" methods into optimised native machine code. That is why long-running Java servers get faster after a warm-up period. Section 2 walks through this pipeline in detail.

What kind of language is Java?

PropertyMeaning
Statically and strongly typedEvery variable has a type checked at compile time; no silent conversion from String to int.
Object-oriented (not "pure")Everything lives in classes, but the 8 primitives (int, double, ...) are not objects, and static members belong to no object.
Garbage collectedYou never call free. The JVM finds unreachable objects and reclaims their memory.
No pointer arithmeticReferences point to objects, but you cannot do ptr + 4. This removes a whole class of memory bugs.
MultithreadedThreads, locks and a memory model are built into the language and JVM. Java 21 added cheap virtual threads.
Safe by designArray bounds are checked, bytecode is verified before running, casts are checked at run time.

Versions you should know

Since Java 10 (2018) a new version ships every six months. Every few releases is an LTS (Long-Term Support) version that companies actually run in production.

VersionYearHeadline features
8 (LTS)2014Lambdas, Streams, Optional, java.time, default methods in interfaces
9 / 102017–18Module system, jshell, List.of (9); var for local variables (10)
11 (LTS)2018Single-file launch java Hello.java, HttpClient, String.strip/isBlank/repeat/lines
14 – 162020–21Switch expressions (14), text blocks (15), records and instanceof patterns (16)
17 (LTS)2021Sealed classes; the common "modern Java" baseline
21 (LTS)2023Virtual threads, pattern matching for switch, record patterns, sequenced collections
25 (LTS)2025Compact source files and instance main methods finalised, scoped values

Distributions and JVM implementations

  • OpenJDK is the open-source reference code. Oracle JDK, Eclipse Temurin, Amazon Corretto, Azul Zulu and Microsoft Build of OpenJDK are all builds of it — functionally almost identical; they differ in licence and support.
  • HotSpot is the standard JVM inside those builds. Alternatives: OpenJ9 (low memory footprint) and GraalVM, whose native image compiles an app ahead of time into a standalone binary that starts in milliseconds (no JIT warm-up, but less peak optimisation).
// Check what you are running on
public class S1Runtime {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(Runtime.version().feature());          // 21
        System.out.println(System.getProperty("java.vm.name"));   // Java HotSpot(TM) 64-Bit Server VM
    }
}
// From a terminal:  java -version   and   javac -version

The JVM is a specification

"The JVM" means two things. The JVM Specification is a document that defines the bytecode instruction set, the .class file format, how classes are loaded and linked, and what memory areas must exist. A JVM implementation (HotSpot, OpenJ9, GraalVM) is a real program that follows that document. The spec says what must happen — for example "unreachable objects may be reclaimed" — but not how, so garbage collectors and JIT compilers differ between implementations and even between versions of HotSpot.

The Java Language Specification (JLS) is a separate document that defines the language itself (syntax, types, overload rules). javac follows the JLS; the JVM follows the JVM spec. Other JVM languages skip the JLS entirely and only target the JVM spec.

Common misconceptions

MythReality
"Java is slow because it is interpreted"Hot code is JIT-compiled to optimised native code; steady-state speed is close to C++ for most server workloads. Start-up and warm-up are the real costs.
"The JVM is platform independent"The bytecode is; each JVM is a native program built for one OS and CPU.
"You need a JRE to run and a JDK to build"True historically; today you install a JDK or ship a jlink runtime image.
"Garbage collection means no memory leaks"Objects that are still reachable (a static map that only grows, unremoved listeners) are never collected.
"Java passes objects by reference"Java is always pass-by-value; for objects the value copied is the reference (section 7).

Trying things quickly: jshell (Java 9)

$ jshell
jshell> int x = 7 / 2;
x ==> 3
jshell> "java".toUpperCase()
$2 ==> "JAVA"
jshell> List.of(3, 1, 2).stream().sorted().toList()
$3 ==> [1, 2, 3]
jshell> /exit

jshell is a REPL (read-eval-print loop) shipped with the JDK: no class, no main, no semicolon needed for expressions, and common packages like java.util are imported for you. Ideal for checking an edge case during interview prep.

Interview angle: "Explain JDK, JRE, JVM" — answer with the nesting (JDK ⊃ JRE ⊃ JVM) and one sentence each. Follow-ups: "Is Java platform independent?" (the bytecode is; the JVM is platform-specific). "Is Java pure OO?" (no — primitives and statics). "Compiled or interpreted?" (both: javac to bytecode, then interpreter plus JIT). "Why is the first request to a Java service slow?" (class loading and JIT warm-up).

2. How a Java Program Runs

In plain words

Running Java is a relay race with four runners. 1) javac compiles your .java source into .class files full of bytecode. 2) You start the JVM with java, and a class loader finds and loads the classes it needs. 3) A verifier checks the bytecode is safe. 4) The execution engine runs it — first with an interpreter, then by compiling hot code to native machine code with the JIT compiler.

A recipe written in a universal shorthand (bytecode). Any kitchen in the world (a JVM) can follow it. The chef first reads it line by line (interpreting). After cooking the same dish a hundred times, the chef memorises it and cooks it from memory much faster (JIT compilation).

Step 1 — compile with javac

public class Hello {
    static int add(int a, int b) {
        return a + b;
    }
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println("Hello, " + (args.length > 0 ? args[0] : "world"));
        System.out.println(add(2, 3));
    }
}

// $ javac Hello.java        -> creates Hello.class
// $ java Hello              -> Hello, world   then 5
// $ java Hello Ana          -> Hello, Ana     then 5
  • javac takes a file name (Hello.java); java takes a class name (Hello). java Hello.class fails with "Could not find or load main class Hello.class".
  • A public class must live in a file with the same name. Each class — including nested ones — becomes its own file: App.java with a nested Helper produces App.class and App$Helper.class.
  • The compiler checks types, resolves overloads, folds constants, and reports errors — but does little optimisation. The heavy optimisation happens later in the JIT.

You can look at bytecode with javap -c. The JVM is a stack machine: instructions push values onto an operand stack and pop them off.

$ javap -c Hello
  static int add(int, int);
    Code:
       0: iload_0        // push local variable 0 (a)
       1: iload_1        // push local variable 1 (b)
       2: iadd           // pop both, push a + b
       3: ireturn        // return the int on top of the stack
  ...
      16: invokedynamic #15,  0   // InvokeDynamic #0:makeConcatWithConstants  (string +, Java 9+)
      29: invokestatic  #25       // Method add:(II)I

Packages, classpath and JARs

// src/com/acme/App.java  starts with:  package com.acme;
$ javac -d out src/com/acme/App.java      // -d puts classes in out/com/acme/
$ java -cp out com.acme.App               // -cp = classpath; use the FULLY qualified name
App running from package com.acme

$ jar --create --file app.jar --main-class com.acme.App -C out .
$ java -jar app.jar                        // manifest says which class has main
App running from package com.acme

The classpath is the list of folders and JARs where the class loader looks for classes. Using the simple name (java -cp out App) fails with ClassNotFoundException: App.

Step 2 — class loading (loading, linking, initialisation)

  1. Loading — a class loader reads the bytes of the .class file and creates a Class object in memory (class metadata goes into Metaspace, which replaced PermGen in Java 8).
  2. Linking — verify (bytecode is well-formed and type-safe: no stack overflow tricks, no jumping into the middle of an instruction); prepare (allocate static fields with default values); resolve (turn symbolic names like java/lang/System into real references, often lazily).
  3. Initialisation — run static initialisers and assign static field values. This happens on first active use of the class, not at start-up.
Class loaderLoadsgetClassLoader() prints
BootstrapCore JDK classes: java.lang.String, java.util.*null (it is native code, not a Java object)
Platform (Java 9+; was "extension")Other JDK modules, e.g. java.sqlplatform
Application (system)Your classes and JARs on the classpathapp

Loaders use parent delegation: before loading a class itself, a loader asks its parent first. So a class named java.lang.String on your classpath can never replace the real one — the bootstrap loader always wins. This is a security feature.

public class S2Loaders {
    static { System.out.println("S2Loaders initialised"); }
    static class Lazy {
        static { System.out.println("Lazy initialised"); }
        static int X = 1;
    }
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(String.class.getClassLoader());                          // null
        System.out.println(java.sql.Connection.class.getClassLoader().getName());   // platform
        System.out.println(S2Loaders.class.getClassLoader().getName());             // app
        System.out.println("before Lazy");
        System.out.println(Lazy.X);   // first use: prints "Lazy initialised", then 1
    }
}
// Output order: S2Loaders initialised, null, platform, app, before Lazy, Lazy initialised, 1

Step 3 — execution: interpreter plus JIT

The interpreter starts instantly but is slow per instruction. The JVM counts how often each method is called and each loop runs. When a method becomes hot, HotSpot compiles it to native code using tiered compilation:

tier 0 interpreter -- every method starts here, collects profiling data tier 3 C1 (client) -- quick compile with profiling (after ~ thousands of calls) tier 4 C2 (server) -- slow, aggressive compile using the profile: inlining, escape analysis, loop unrolling, dead-code removal deopt back to tier 0 -- if an assumption breaks (e.g. a new subclass is loaded)
$ java -XX:+PrintCompilation JitDemo | grep JitDemo::
     53    7       2       JitDemo::square (4 bytes)
     53    8       4       JitDemo::square (4 bytes)          // promoted to C2
     53    7       2       JitDemo::square (4 bytes)   made not entrant   // old version retired
     57    9 %     3       JitDemo::main @ 4 (32 bytes)       // % = on-stack replacement of a hot loop
// Timings and ids differ on every run.

Because the JIT uses real run-time data (which branches are taken, which class a call site actually sees), it can sometimes beat ahead-of-time compiled code at peak speed. The price is warm-up: the first requests to a fresh JVM are slower. OSR (on-stack replacement) lets a long loop already running in main switch to compiled code mid-loop.

JVM run-time memory areas (overview)

AreaPer thread or sharedHolds
HeapSharedAll objects and arrays; managed by the garbage collector
StackOne per threadA frame per method call: local variables, parameters, operand stack. Too deep = StackOverflowError
MetaspaceShared (native memory)Class metadata, method bytecode (Java 8+; replaced PermGen)
PC registerOne per threadAddress of the bytecode instruction being executed
Native method stackOne per threadFrames for native (C/C++) code called via JNI

The main method

public static void main(String[] args)
  • public — the launcher calls it from outside the class. static — it runs before any object exists. void — the exit code is set with System.exit(n), not returned. String[] args — command-line arguments (never null; empty array if none).
  • Also legal: String... args, final String[] args, static public. You may overload main, but the launcher only calls the String[] version.
  • Wrong signature (say static void main()) compiles fine but fails at launch: "Error: Main method not found in class NoMain, please define the main method as: public static void main(String[] args)".

Single-file launch and simpler main

// Java 11 (JEP 330): run a source file directly -- compiled in memory, no .class written
$ java Hello.java Bob
Hello, Bob
5

// Java 21 PREVIEW (JEP 445): unnamed class + instance main.  File Simple.java:
void main() {
    System.out.println("No class, no static, no String[] args");
}
$ java --enable-preview --source 21 Simple.java
No class, no static, no String[] args

In Java 21 the simpler main needs --enable-preview; it was finalised in Java 25 as "compact source files and instance main methods". Java 22 extended source launch to programs with several files. For quick experiments, use jshell (section 1).

Errors you will meet at this stage

ErrorMeaningUsual fix
ClassNotFoundExceptionCode asked for a class by name (launcher, Class.forName) and it is not on the classpathFix -cp, use the fully qualified name
NoClassDefFoundErrorClass existed at compile time but is missing at run time, or its static initialiser failed earlierAdd the missing JAR; check the first exception in the log
UnsupportedClassVersionErrorClass compiled for a newer Java than the JVM running it (Java 21 = class-file major version 65)Upgrade the runtime or compile with --release 17
Interview angle: "Walk me through what happens when you run java Hello." Say: JVM starts, bootstrap loader loads core classes, application loader loads Hello (parent delegation), it is verified, prepared and initialised, the launcher calls main on the main thread, code is interpreted then hot methods are JIT-compiled, objects go on the heap and the GC reclaims them. Bonus points: lazy class loading, tiered C1/C2, and why warm-up matters for benchmarks (use JMH, not System.nanoTime in a loop).

3. Types, Variables & Wrappers

In plain words

Every Java variable has a fixed type. There are two families. Primitive types (8 of them: byte, short, int, long, float, double, char, boolean) store the value itself. Reference types (classes, interfaces, arrays, String) store a reference — an arrow to an object that lives on the heap.

Each primitive has a wrapper class (int → Integer) so it can be used where an object is required, like in a List. Java converts between them automatically: autoboxing (primitive → wrapper) and unboxing (wrapper → primitive).

A primitive is cash in your pocket: the value is right there. A reference is a cloakroom ticket: the ticket is small, and it tells you where the real coat (the object) is hanging. Two tickets can point to the same coat.

The 8 primitive types

TypeSizeRangeDefault (fields)WrapperLiteral
byte8 bit-128 to 1270Byte(byte) 10
short16 bit-32,768 to 32,7670Short(short) 10
int32 bitabout ±2.1 billion (231 − 1 = 2,147,483,647)0Integer42, 0xFF, 0b1010, 1_000_000
long64 bitabout ±9.2 × 10180LLong10_000_000_000L
float32 bitabout ±3.4 × 1038, ~7 significant digits0.0fFloat3.14f
double64 bitabout ±1.8 × 10308, ~15–16 significant digits0.0Double3.14, 1e-9
char16 bit0 to 65,535 (unsigned; one UTF-16 code unit)'\u0000'Character'A', '\n'
booleannot specified (JVM-dependent)true / falsefalseBooleantrue
  • Sizes are fixed on every platform — an int is always 32 bits, unlike C. All integer types are signed except char.
  • Defaults apply only to fields and array elements. A local variable has no default: reading it before assigning is a compile error — variable x might not have been initialized.
  • An integer literal is an int by default: long big = 3_000_000_000; fails with "integer number too large" — add L. A decimal literal is a double: float f = 3.14; fails with "possible lossy conversion from double to float" — add f.
  • char is a number: 'A' + 1 is 66, and (char) ('A' + 1) is 'B'.

Primitive vs reference

Primitive (int x = 5)Reference (Integer y = 5, String s)
Variable holdsThe value itselfA reference (address) to an object on the heap
Can be null?NoYes
== comparesValuesIdentity (same object?) — use equals for content
Memory4 bytes for intReference (4–8 bytes) + object (~16 bytes for an Integer)
Generics / collectionsNot allowed: List<int> is illegalAllowed: List<Integer>

Wrappers, autoboxing and unboxing (Java 5)

Wrappers exist because generics and collections only hold objects, because sometimes you need "no value" (null), and to host helper methods like Integer.parseInt, Integer.MAX_VALUE, Character.isDigit. Wrappers are immutable. The compiler inserts the conversion calls for you:

Integer boxed = 42;          // compiler writes: Integer.valueOf(42)      (autoboxing)
int back = boxed;            // compiler writes: boxed.intValue()         (unboxing)
List<Integer> list = new ArrayList<>();
list.add(7);                 // boxes 7
int first = list.get(0);     // unboxes

Integer.parseInt("123") + 1; // 124  String -> int
Integer.toBinaryString(10);  // "1010"
// new Integer(5) is deprecated (Java 9) and marked for removal (Java 16): use Integer.valueOf(5)

The Integer cache (-128 to 127)

Integer.valueOf — and therefore autoboxing — returns a cached, shared object for values from -128 to 127. Outside that range it creates a new object each time. So == on two Integers "works" for small numbers and silently breaks for larger ones.

Integer a = 127, b = 127, x = 128, y = 128;
System.out.println(a == b);          // true   same cached object
System.out.println(x == y);          // false  two objects
System.out.println(x.equals(y));     // true   compare values with equals

Long l1 = 127L, l2 = 127L;
System.out.println(l1 == l2);        // true   Long, Short, Byte also cache -128..127
Integer mixed = 1000; int prim = 1000;
System.out.println(mixed == prim);   // true   one side is primitive, so the other is UNBOXED
System.out.println(Long.valueOf(1).equals(1));  // false  1 boxes to Integer, not Long

Map<String, Integer> m = new HashMap<>();
int v = m.get("missing");            // NullPointerException: unboxing null
  • Character caches 0–127, Boolean has just TRUE/FALSE. Float/Double have no cache. The Integer upper bound can be raised with -XX:AutoBoxCacheMax=<n> — never rely on it.
  • Rule: compare wrappers with equals (or unbox first). Beware a null wrapper being unboxed in arithmetic, if (flag), or a ternary.
  • Boxing in a hot loop (Long sum = 0L; sum += i;) creates millions of objects. Use primitives for arithmetic.

Widening and narrowing conversions

Widening (automatic, no cast): byte -> short -> int -> long -> float -> double char -^ Narrowing (the other direction) needs an explicit cast: int i = (int) 3.99;
int i = 100;  long wl = i;  double wd = wl;     // 100  100.0   widening, always safe in range
(int) 3.99            // 3            cast TRUNCATES toward zero (no rounding)
(int) -3.99           // -3
Math.round(3.99)      // 4            use Math.round / floor / ceil to round
(byte) 300            // 44           keeps the low 8 bits: 300 - 256
(int) 1e20            // 2147483647   double->int clamps to MAX_VALUE
(long) Double.NaN     // 0

// Widening can still LOSE PRECISION: long -> float and int -> float
long precise = 123456789123456789L;
float lossy = precise;          // compiles without a cast!
(long) lossy                    // 123456790519087104   digits lost
float ff = 16_777_217;          // 2^24 + 1
(int) ff                        // 16777216

// Arithmetic on byte/short/char is done in int
byte p = 10, q = 20;
int r = p + q;                  // OK
// byte r2 = p + q;             // compile error: possible lossy conversion from int to byte
byte r2 = (byte) (p + q);       // 30

Overflow — silent wrap-around

int max = Integer.MAX_VALUE;
max + 1                                // -2147483648  wraps to MIN_VALUE, no exception
Math.abs(Integer.MIN_VALUE)            // -2147483648  still negative!
long wrong = 1_000_000 * 1_000_000;    // -727379968   int*int overflowed BEFORE widening
long right = 1_000_000L * 1_000_000;   // 1000000000000
Math.multiplyExact(1_000_000, 1_000_000);  // throws ArithmeticException: integer overflow
// also addExact, subtractExact, toIntExact; BigInteger for arbitrary size

Floating point is approximate

0.1 + 0.2                  // 0.30000000000000004  (binary cannot store 0.1 exactly)
0.1 + 0.2 == 0.3           // false -- compare with Math.abs(a - b) < 1e-9
new BigDecimal("0.1").add(new BigDecimal("0.2"))   // 0.3  use BigDecimal (from String!) for money
1.0 / 0                    // Infinity   (no exception for doubles)
0.0 / 0                    // NaN
Double.NaN == Double.NaN   // false -- use Double.isNaN(x)
7 / 0                      // ArithmeticException: / by zero   (integers DO throw)

var — local type inference (Java 10)

var names = new ArrayList<String>();   // type is ArrayList<String>, fixed at compile time
var count = 0;                          // int
// var x;            // error: cannot infer without an initialiser
// var n = null;     // error
// Only for local variables (and for-loop / lambda params) -- not fields, not method params or returns.

var is not dynamic typing: the compiler infers one fixed type. Use it when the right-hand side makes the type obvious; avoid it when it hides the type (var result = service.process();).

Interview angle: "What are the primitive types and their sizes?" (the table). "Why does Integer a = 128, b = 128; a == b print false?" (the -128..127 cache; == compares references). "What is autoboxing and what can go wrong?" (NPE on unboxing null, identity comparisons, hidden allocations). "What does (byte) 200 give?" (-56: keep the low 8 bits). Best practice: use primitives for maths, wrappers only for collections or nullable values, long for large counts and money in cents or BigDecimal.

4. Operators & Control Flow

In plain words

Operators are the symbols that compute values: + - * / % for maths, == < > for comparisons, && || ! for logic. Control flow decides which lines run and how often: if/else, switch, and the loops for, while, do-while.

Most of it looks like C, but interviews love the small rules: integer division throws away the fraction, i++ and ++i return different values, && can skip its right side, and modern Java (14+) has a much safer switch.

Operators are the buttons on a calculator; control flow is the set of road signs ("if the road is closed, turn left; repeat until you reach the town"). Knowing the exact rules of each button prevents a wrong turn.

Arithmetic: integer division and remainder

7 / 2              // 3     int / int = int; the fraction is dropped (truncated toward zero)
-7 / 2             // -3    toward zero, not -4
7 / 2.0            // 3.5   one double operand makes the whole division double
double avg = 7 / 2;  // 3.0 -- the division happened in int BEFORE assignment
7 % 3              // 1
-7 % 3             // -1    result takes the sign of the LEFT operand
Math.floorMod(-7, 3) // 2   always non-negative for a positive divisor (use for wrap-around indexes)
7 / 0              // ArithmeticException: / by zero
7.0 / 0            // Infinity

Fix the "average" bug by making one operand a double first: (double) sum / count. Writing (double) (sum / count) is too late — the truncation already happened.

Increment: pre vs post

int i = 5; int a = i++; // POST: use the old value, then add 1 -> a = 5, i = 6 int b = ++i; // PRE : add 1 first, then use the value -> i = 7, b = 7 int j = 5; j = j++; // j stays 5! j++ yields 5, bumps j to 6, then the assignment writes 5 back int k = 1; int res = k++ + ++k; // 1 + 3 = 4, and k ends as 3 (evaluated strictly left to right)

Java guarantees left-to-right evaluation of operands, so these results are defined (unlike in C). Still, never write this in real code — use i++ alone on its own line or in a for header.

Compound assignment hides a cast

short sh = 10;
sh += 5;          // OK: means sh = (short) (sh + 5)
// sh = sh + 5;   // compile error: possible lossy conversion from int to short
byte by = 127;
by += 1;          // -128 -- the hidden cast lets it overflow silently

Logical operators and short-circuiting

&& and || short-circuit: if the left side already decides the answer, the right side is not evaluated. & and | on booleans always evaluate both sides.

static boolean check(String label, boolean result) {
    System.out.println("  evaluated " + label);
    return result;
}
boolean r1 = check("left", false) && check("right", true);   // prints only: evaluated left
boolean r2 = check("left", false) &  check("right", true);   // prints: evaluated left, evaluated right

String s = null;
if (s != null && s.length() > 3) { ... }   // safe: length() never runs when s is null

Bitwise and shift operators

6 & 3     // 2    AND   110 & 011 = 010
6 | 3     // 7    OR    110 | 011 = 111
6 ^ 3     // 5    XOR   110 ^ 011 = 101
~6        // -7   NOT   flips every bit (two's complement: ~x == -x - 1)
1 << 4    // 16   shift left  = multiply by 2^4
-16 >> 2  // -4   arithmetic right shift keeps the sign bit
-16 >>> 28 // 15  logical right shift fills with zeros

Precedence you actually need

Group (high to low)Operators
Postfix, unaryx++ x--, ++x --x + - ! ~ (cast)
Multiplicative, additive* / %, then + -
Shift, relational<< >> >>>, then < > <= >= instanceof
Equality== !=
Bitwise, then logical&, ^, |, then &&, then ||
Ternary, assignment (right to left)? :, then = += -= ...

String + is evaluated left to right too: 1 + 2 + "3" + 4 + 5 is "3345" — numbers add until the first String appears, then everything is concatenation. When in doubt, add brackets.

The ternary operator

String grade = score >= 90 ? "A" : score >= 70 ? "B" : "C";   // score 72 -> "B"

// Trap 1: unboxing null
Integer nullable = null;
int v = true ? nullable : 0;       // NullPointerException -- the result type is int, so nullable is unboxed
// Trap 2: numeric promotion
System.out.println(true ? 1 : 2.0);   // 1.0 -- both branches promoted to double

switch statement (classic) — watch the fall-through

int day = 2; String name;
switch (day) {
    case 1: name = "Mon";
    case 2: name = "Tue";          // no break: falls through to the next case!
    case 3: name = "Wed"; break;
    default: name = "?";
}
System.out.println(name);          // Wed

Classic switch works on byte, short, char, int (and their wrappers), String (Java 7, compared with equals) and enum. It cannot switch on long, float, double or boolean.

switch expression (Java 14)

String dayName = switch (day) {
    case 1, 7 -> "Weekend-ish";      // several labels, comma-separated
    case 2 -> "Tue";                 // arrow: no fall-through, no break needed
    default -> {
        String tmp = "Day " + day;
        yield tmp;                   // yield returns a value from a block
    }
};                                   // it is an expression, so it ends with ;
Old case X: statementNew case X -> / switch expression
Fall-throughYes, unless you breakNo — only the matching branch runs
Produces a valueNo (assign inside cases)Yes, with -> or yield
ExhaustiveNot requiredRequired for expressions (default, all enum constants, or all sealed subtypes)
Multiple labelsStacked case 1: case 7:case 1, 7 ->

Pattern matching (Java 16 – 21)

instanceof patterns (Java 16) test and cast in one step; the new variable is only in scope where the test is true:

Object o = "hello";
if (o instanceof String str && str.length() > 3) {
    System.out.println(str.toUpperCase());      // HELLO -- no manual (String) cast
}

Pattern matching for switch (preview in 17–20, final in Java 21) lets you switch on type, add when guards, handle case null, and destructure records with record patterns (also final in Java 21). With a sealed hierarchy the compiler knows every subtype, so no default is needed.

sealed interface Shape permits Circle, Square, Rect {}
record Circle(double r) implements Shape {}
record Square(double side) implements Shape {}
record Rect(double w, double h) implements Shape {}

static double area(Shape s) {
    return switch (s) {                           // exhaustive: all 3 permitted types covered
        case Circle c -> Math.PI * c.r() * c.r();
        case Square sq -> sq.side() * sq.side();
        case Rect(double w, double h) -> w * h;   // record pattern: pulls out the components
    };
}

static String describe(Object o) {
    return switch (o) {
        case null -> "null!";                      // without this, a null throws NPE
        case Integer i when i > 100 -> "big int " + i;   // guard; more specific case first
        case Integer i -> "int " + i;
        case String str -> "string of length " + str.length();
        default -> "something else";
    };
}
// area: 3.14, 4.00, 6.00     describe(500) = "big int 500", describe(2.5) = "something else"

Unnamed patterns _ were a preview in Java 21 (final in Java 22): if (o instanceof Point(int x, _)) ignores components you do not need. In Java 21 they need --enable-preview --source 21. Pattern matching on primitive types is a later preview (Java 23+).

Loops, break and continue

for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) { ... }       // classic
for (var x : new int[]{1, 2, 3}) { ... } // enhanced for: arrays and any Iterable; no index
while (cond) { ... }                      // may run 0 times
do { n++; } while (n < 0);               // runs at least once -- n becomes 1

outer:                                    // label: break out of BOTH loops at once
for (int r = 0; r < 3; r++) {
    for (int c = 0; c < 3; c++) {
        if (r * c == 2) { System.out.println("found at " + r + "," + c); break outer; }
    }
}
// found at 1,2     (continue outer; would skip to the next r instead)
  • Modifying a List while looping over it with enhanced for throws ConcurrentModificationException — use an Iterator and it.remove(), or removeIf.
  • Comparing floats: 0.1f == 0.1 is false (the float is widened to a slightly different double).
Interview angle: "What does i = i++ leave in i?" (unchanged). "Difference between & and &&?" (short-circuit; also bitwise on ints). "Why is sh += 1 legal but sh = sh + 1 not?" (hidden cast). "Switch statement vs switch expression?" (arrows, no fall-through, yields a value, exhaustiveness). Senior follow-up: "How do sealed types and pattern matching replace the visitor pattern?" — the compiler checks the switch covers every subtype, so adding a new subtype becomes a compile error everywhere it is not handled.

5. Strings

In plain words

A String is a sequence of characters, and in Java it is an object that can never change after it is created (immutable). Methods like toUpperCase() or replace() do not edit the string — they return a brand-new one.

Because strings never change, Java can safely share them. String literals (text in double quotes in your code) are stored once in a special area called the String pool, and every identical literal points to that one copy. When you need to build text piece by piece, use StringBuilder, which is the mutable version.

A printed book is immutable: to "fix a typo" you print a new edition. A library keeps one copy of each edition on the shelf and lends everyone the same one (the pool). A notebook, on the other hand, you can scribble in and erase (StringBuilder).

Immutability in practice

String s = "java";
s.toUpperCase();          // creates "JAVA" and throws it away
System.out.println(s);    // java
s = s.toUpperCase();      // re-point s at the new object
System.out.println(s);    // JAVA

The variable s can be re-pointed; the object "java" never changes. String is a final class (no subclass can add mutability), its internal array is private, and no method modifies it.

Why immutable?

  • Pooling — sharing one copy is only safe if nobody can change it.
  • Security — file paths, URLs, class names and passwords checked once cannot be altered afterwards by another reference.
  • Thread safety — can be shared across threads with no locking.
  • Hash caching — hashCode() is computed once and cached, which makes strings fast HashMap keys.

Inside, since Java 9 (compact strings) a String stores a byte[] plus a "coder" flag: 1 byte per character when all characters are Latin-1, 2 bytes (UTF-16) otherwise. That roughly halves memory for typical English text.

The String pool, == and equals

String a = "hello";
String b = "hello";
String c = new String("hello");
String d = c.intern();
a == b          // true   same pooled object
a == c          // false  c is a separate heap object
a.equals(c)     // true   same characters
a == d          // true   intern() returns the pooled copy

// Compile-time constants are folded into one literal; runtime concatenation is not
String hel = "hel";
final String helFinal = "hel";
a == "hel" + "lo"          // true   folded to "hello" by javac
a == hel + "lo"            // false  built at run time -> new object
a == helFinal + "lo"       // true   final + literal is a constant expression
a == (hel + "lo").intern() // true
  • Always compare content with equals. == compares references and only "works" by accident with literals.
  • Null-safe comparison: "x".equals(maybeNull) (literal first) or Objects.equals(a, b). equalsIgnoreCase ignores case; compareTo orders alphabetically (by UTF-16 code).
  • Since Java 7 the pool lives in the normal heap (it was in PermGen before), so pooled strings can be garbage collected.
  • intern() can reduce memory when you have millions of duplicate strings, but it costs a hash lookup; modern GCs (G1) can also do string deduplication automatically (-XX:+UseStringDeduplication).
  • new String("hello") may create two objects — the pooled literal (if not already there) and the new heap copy. A classic trivia question.

String vs StringBuilder vs StringBuffer

StringStringBuilder (Java 5)StringBuffer (Java 1.0)
Mutable?NoYesYes
Thread-safe?Yes (immutable)NoYes — every method synchronized
SpeedEach change copies the whole stringFastestSlower (locking)
Use forValues, keys, constantsBuilding text in a method or loop (default choice)Legacy code; almost never needed today
// Bad: O(n^2) -- each += copies everything built so far
String acc = "";
for (int i = 0; i < 20_000; i++) acc += "x";

// Good: amortised O(1) append
StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder();
for (int i = 0; i < 20_000; i++) sb.append("x");
String result = sb.toString();

StringBuilder b2 = new StringBuilder("ab");
b2.append('c').append(1).insert(0, "[").append("]");   // [abc1]  (methods chain)
b2.length();                                            // 6
b2.reverse();                                           // ]1cba[  (in place)
// also: deleteCharAt, setCharAt, setLength, replace, indexOf

A single-line "a" + x + "b" is fine: since Java 9 the compiler emits one invokedynamic call (StringConcatFactory) that sizes the result once. The problem is only += inside a loop. Note that StringBuilder does not override equals — compare with sb1.toString().equals(...) or sb1.compareTo(sb2) (Java 11).

Text blocks (Java 15)

String json = """
    {
      "name": "Ana",
      "age": 30
    }
    """;          // indentation up to the closing """ is stripped; ends with a newline
String oneLine = """
    one \
    line""";      // backslash joins lines; closing """ on the same line = no trailing newline
// oneLine is "one line"

Text blocks are ordinary Strings (same pool, same methods) — just easier to write multi-line JSON, SQL and HTML without \n and escaped quotes.

Common methods

MethodExample with w = "Hello, World"Result
length(), charAt(i)w.length(), w.charAt(4)12, 'o'
indexOf / lastIndexOfw.indexOf('o'), w.lastIndexOf('o'), w.indexOf("xyz")4, 8, -1
substring(begin[, end])w.substring(7), w.substring(0, 5)"World", "Hello" (end exclusive)
contains, startsWith, endsWithw.contains("World"), w.endsWith("!")true, false
replace, toLowerCasew.replace('l', 'L')"HeLLo, WorLd"
trim() / strip() (11)" Hi ".strip()"Hi"; strip also removes Unicode whitespace
isEmpty() / isBlank() (11)" ".isEmpty(), " ".isBlank()false, true
repeat(n) (11), lines() (11)"ab".repeat(3), "a\nb\nc".lines().count()"ababab", 3
split(regex)"a,b,,c,,".split(",")[a, b, , c] (trailing empties dropped)
String.join, String.valueOfString.join("-", List.of("x","y","z"))"x-y-z"
formatted (15), String.format"%s is %d".formatted("Ana", 30)"Ana is 30"
toCharArray(), chars()"abc".chars().sum()294 (97 + 98 + 99)

Pitfalls

  • split takes a regex: "a.b.c".split(".") returns an empty array because . matches every character. Use split("\\.") → [a, b, c].
  • substring, replace, trim return new strings — forgetting to assign the result is a silent bug.
  • Different strings can share a hash: "Aa".hashCode() and "BB".hashCode() are both 2112. Equal hash never means equal strings.
  • length() counts UTF-16 code units, not visible characters — an emoji or some CJK characters take 2. Use codePointCount when that matters.
  • Do not keep passwords in a String (it lingers in memory until GC and may be pooled) — APIs use char[] so you can wipe it.
Interview angle: "Why is String immutable?" (pool, security, thread safety, cached hash). "== vs equals?" (identity vs content, with the pool example). "How many objects does new String("hello") create?" (up to two). "StringBuilder vs StringBuffer?" (same API; Buffer is synchronized and slower; use Builder). "Is + in a loop bad?" (yes, O(n²) copying; one-line concatenation is fine). Best practice: equals for comparison, StringBuilder in loops, isBlank/strip for input cleaning, text blocks for multi-line literals.

6. Arrays

In plain words

An array is a fixed-size row of slots that all hold the same type, numbered from 0. You choose the size when you create it and it can never grow or shrink. Reading or writing slot i is instant (O(1)) because the JVM computes its address directly.

In Java an array is an object on the heap: the variable holds a reference to it, it has a length field, and every slot starts with a default value (0, false, or null). A "2D array" is really an array of references to other arrays.

A row of numbered post-office boxes built into a wall. You can open box 7 directly without checking boxes 0–6, but you cannot add an eighth box to the wall later — you would need to build a new, bigger wall and move the letters.

Declaring and creating

int[] nums = new int[3];          // [0, 0, 0]           size fixed at 3
int[] primes = {2, 3, 5, 7};      // initializer (only in a declaration)
int[] more = new int[]{1, 2};     // anonymous array: usable anywhere, e.g. as an argument
int legacy[] = new int[2];        // C-style; legal but avoid -- keep [] with the type
String[] strs = new String[2];    // [null, null]  -- no String objects created yet!

primes.length                     // 4    a FIELD (no parentheses); String uses length()
primes[4]                         // ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException: Index 4 out of bounds for length 4
new int[0]                        // legal empty array; return this instead of null
System.out.println(primes);       // [I@5ca881b5  -- type code + hash, NOT the contents
Arrays.toString(primes)           // [2, 3, 5, 7]
Element typeDefault value
byte, short, int, long0
float, double0.0
char'\u0000' (the character with code 0)
booleanfalse
Any reference (String, Integer, int[], ...)null

Arrays are checked on every access: an invalid index always throws, it never reads random memory as in C. The size can be a run-time value (new int[n]) but is limited to about Integer.MAX_VALUE because indexes are int.

2D and jagged arrays

int[][] grid = new int[2][3];          // 2 rows x 3 columns, all 0
grid[1][2] = 9;
Arrays.deepToString(grid)              // [[0, 0, 0], [0, 0, 9]]

int[][] jagged = new int[3][];         // only the outer array; rows are null for now
jagged[0] = new int[]{1};
jagged[1] = new int[]{1, 2};
jagged[2] = new int[]{1, 2, 3};
Arrays.deepToString(jagged)            // [[1], [1, 2], [1, 2, 3]]
jagged[2].length                       // 3 -- each row has its own length

for (int[] row : jagged) {             // always use row.length, never assume a rectangle
    for (int v : row) { ... }
}

Because rows are separate objects, iterating row by row (grid[r][c] with c in the inner loop) is cache-friendly; iterating column-first jumps between objects and is noticeably slower on large grids.

The Arrays utility class

int[] data = {5, 2, 9, 1};
Arrays.sort(data);                        // [1, 2, 5, 9]   dual-pivot quicksort for primitives
Arrays.binarySearch(data, 5)              // 2              array MUST be sorted
Arrays.binarySearch(data, 3)              // -3             not found: -(insertionPoint) - 1
Arrays.copyOf(data, 6)                    // [1, 2, 5, 9, 0, 0]   pads with defaults (or truncates)
Arrays.copyOfRange(data, 1, 3)            // [2, 5]         end exclusive
Arrays.fill(filled, 7)                    // [7, 7, 7, 7]
Arrays.setAll(sq, i -> i * i)             // [0, 1, 4, 9, 16]
Arrays.stream(data).sum()                 // 17
Arrays.compare(new int[]{1, 2}, new int[]{1, 3})          // -1  lexicographic (Java 9)
Arrays.mismatch(new int[]{1, 2, 3}, new int[]{1, 9, 3})   // 1   first differing index (Java 9)

Integer[] boxed = {3, 1, 2};
Arrays.sort(boxed, Comparator.reverseOrder());   // [3, 2, 1]  comparator needs OBJECTS, not int[]

List<Integer> view = Arrays.asList(boxed);       // fixed-size LIST VIEW backed by the array
boxed[0] = 100;  // view is now [100, 2, 1]   -- writes show through both ways
view.add(4);     // UnsupportedOperationException
Arrays.asList(new int[]{1, 2}).size()            // 1 -- a List<int[]> holding one array!

Comparing and copying

int[] x = {1, 2}, y = {1, 2};
x == y                  // false  different objects
x.equals(y)             // false  arrays do NOT override equals (inherits identity from Object)
Arrays.equals(x, y)     // true   compares elements
int[][] g1 = {{1}}, g2 = {{1}};
Arrays.equals(g1, g2)       // false  compares the row REFERENCES
Arrays.deepEquals(g1, g2)   // true   recurses into rows

int[] orig = {1, 2, 3};
int[] alias = orig;  alias[0] = 99;     // same object: orig is now [99, 2, 3]
int[] cloned = orig.clone(); cloned[1] = 42;   // new array: orig unchanged, cloned is [99, 42, 3]

int[][] deep = {{1, 2}, {3, 4}};
int[][] shallow = deep.clone();         // copies the outer array only -- rows are SHARED
shallow[0][0] = 100;                    // deep[0][0] is now 100 too!

int[] dst = new int[5];
System.arraycopy(orig, 0, dst, 1, 3);   // [0, 99, 2, 3, 0]   (src, srcPos, dest, destPos, length)

For a true deep copy of a 2D array, clone each row: int[][] copy = Arrays.stream(deep).map(int[]::clone).toArray(int[][]::new);

Array covariance and ArrayStoreException

Arrays are covariant: because String is a subtype of Object, String[] is treated as a subtype of Object[]. That compiles, but it opens a hole that the JVM must plug at run time — every store into a reference array is type-checked.

Object[] objs = new String[2];   // compiles: covariance
objs[0] = "fine";
objs[1] = 42;                    // compiles, but at run time: ArrayStoreException: java.lang.Integer

// Generics are INVARIANT, so the same mistake is caught at compile time:
// List<Object> lo = new ArrayList<String>();   // compile error

This is one reason you cannot create a generic array (new T[10] or new List<String>[10] are compile errors): generics are erased at run time, so the JVM could not do this store check. Prefer List<T> over T[] in APIs.

Array vs ArrayList

Array int[] / String[]ArrayList<E>
SizeFixed at creationGrows automatically (about 1.5x when full)
PrimitivesYes — int[] stores raw intsNo — boxes to Integer (more memory, slower)
Type safetyRun-time store check (covariant)Compile-time (invariant generics)
Lengtharr.lengthlist.size()
equals / toStringIdentity / [I@hash — use Arrays.*Element-wise, readable
Best forKnown size, performance-critical numeric code, DP tablesEverything else in business code

Pitfalls

  • Arrays as HashMap/HashSet keys use identity: set.contains(new int[]{1}) is false even after adding new int[]{1}. Use List.of(...) or a record as the key.
  • new String[3] gives three nulls — calling a method on an element throws NullPointerException.
  • Off-by-one: the last index is length - 1; loop with i < arr.length.
  • Returning an internal array exposes it to modification. Return arr.clone() or an unmodifiable list.
Interview angle: "Default value of an array element?" (0, false, null). "How do you compare two arrays?" (Arrays.equals, deepEquals for nested). "Is clone() deep or shallow?" (shallow for nested arrays). "What is ArrayStoreException and why can't generics have it?" (array covariance with a run-time check vs invariant, erased generics). "Array or ArrayList?" (fixed size and primitives vs flexibility and the collections API).

7. Methods & Pass-by-Value

In plain words

A method is a named block of code that takes inputs (parameters), does some work, and may return one value. Several methods in a class can share a name if their parameter lists differ — that is overloading. A varargs parameter (int... nums) accepts any number of arguments.

When you call a method, Java always copies the value of each argument into the parameter — this is pass-by-value, with no exceptions. For a primitive the copied value is the number itself. For an object, the copied value is the reference (the arrow), not the object. So the method can change the object the arrow points to, but it can never make the caller's variable point somewhere else.

You give a friend a photocopy of your house address. They can go to the house and repaint the door (mutate the object) — you will see the new colour. But if they scribble a different address on their photocopy, your own copy of the address is unchanged.

Anatomy of a method

//  modifiers     return type  name   parameters              exceptions
    public static int          max   (int first, int... rest) throws IllegalStateException {
        int m = first;
        for (int r : rest) m = Math.max(m, r);
        return m;                // max(3) = 3, max(3, 9, 4) = 9
    }
  • The signature is the name plus the parameter types in order: max(int, int[]). Return type, parameter names, modifiers and throws are not part of it.
  • static methods belong to the class (Math.max); instance methods need an object and can use this.
  • A method returns at most one value. To return several, return a record (Java 16): record MinMax(int min, int max) {}.

Overloading rules

Overloaded methods must differ in the number, types or order of parameters. A different return type alone is not enough:

static int  m(int x) { return 1; }
static long m(int x) { return 2; }   // compile error: method m(int) is already defined

The compiler picks the overload at compile time using the declared (static) types of the arguments, in three phases. It stops at the first phase that finds a match, and within a phase chooses the most specific method:

Phase 1: exact match or WIDENING only (int -> long -> float -> double, String -> Object) Phase 2: allow BOXING / UNBOXING (int -> Integer, Integer -> int) Phase 3: allow VARARGS (int -> int...) => widening beats boxing, boxing beats varargs
static String f(int x)     { return "int"; }
static String f(long x)    { return "long"; }
static String f(Integer x) { return "Integer"; }
static String f(Object x)  { return "Object"; }
static String f(int... x)  { return "varargs"; }

f(1)                   // int       exact
f(1L)                  // long      exact
f((byte) 1)            // int       widening byte -> int (closest)
f('a')                 // int       char widens to int
f(Integer.valueOf(1))  // Integer   exact
f("s")                 // Object    String widens to Object
f()  /  f(1, 2)        // varargs   only varargs accepts 0 or 2 args

static String g(long x)    { return "long"; }
static String g(Integer x) { return "Integer"; }
g(5)                   // long      phase 1 (widening) wins before boxing is even tried

static String h(Object o)  { return "Object"; }
static String h(String s)  { return "String"; }
h(null)                // String    both match; String is more specific

static void m(Integer x) {}
static void m(String x) {}
m(null);               // compile error: reference to m is ambiguous
Overloading vs overriding: overloading = same name, different parameters, chosen at compile time (static binding). Overriding = a subclass redefines a method with the same signature, chosen at run time by the object's actual class (dynamic dispatch) — covered in the OOP part.

Varargs (Java 5)

static String v(int... x) { return "varargs " + x.length; }
v()                  // varargs 0   -- x is an empty array, never null
v(1, 2, 3)           // varargs 3
v(new int[]{7})      // varargs 1   -- you can pass an array directly

String.format("%s-%s", "a", "b");   // format(String, Object...) -- the classic varargs API
  • A varargs parameter is just an array: int... x is int[] x inside the method.
  • Only one per method and it must be last. To require at least one value, use (int first, int... rest).
  • Each call allocates a new array — avoid in very hot paths (that is why List.of has fixed-arity overloads up to 10 elements).
  • Generic varargs (List<String>...) trigger "possible heap pollution" warnings; @SafeVarargs suppresses them when the method only reads the array.

Java is ALWAYS pass-by-value

static class Dog { String name; Dog(String name) { this.name = name; } }

static void bump(int n)      { n = n + 1; }                 // changes the local copy only
static void rename(Dog d)    { d.name = "Max"; }            // follows the copied reference: mutates the object
static void replace(Dog d)   { d = new Dog("Rex"); d.name = "Buddy"; }  // re-points the LOCAL copy only
static void swap(Dog a, Dog b) { Dog t = a; a = b; b = t; }

int n = 5;           bump(n);       // n is still 5
Dog dog = new Dog("Rex");
rename(dog);                        // dog.name is "Max"
replace(dog);                       // dog.name is STILL "Max"
Dog a = new Dog("A"), b = new Dog("B");
swap(a, b);                         // a.name = "A", b.name = "B" -- nothing swapped

More cases that follow from the same single rule:

static void addItem(List<String> list) {
    list.add("new");              // mutates the caller's list  -- visible
    list = new ArrayList<>();     // re-points the local copy   -- invisible
    list.add("lost");
}
List<String> items = new ArrayList<>(List.of("x"));
addItem(items);                   // items is [x, new]

static void mutate(int[] a) { a[0] = 100; a = new int[]{-1, -1}; }
int[] arr = {1, 2};  mutate(arr); // arr is [100, 2]

static void upper(String s)  { s = s.toUpperCase(); }
String s = "hi";     upper(s);    // still "hi"  -- String is immutable, so it can only be re-pointed

static void incBox(Integer i) { i++; }   // i++ means i = Integer.valueOf(i + 1): a NEW object
Integer boxed = 10;  incBox(boxed);      // still 10
Inside the method you...Caller sees the change?Why
Assign a new value to a primitive parameterNoThe parameter is a copy of the number
Call a mutating method / set a field on an object parameterYesThe copied reference points to the same object
Assign a new object to the parameterNoOnly the local copy of the reference changes
"Change" a String or wrapper parameterNoThey are immutable; every "change" makes a new object

To give a result back, return it (s = upper(s)) rather than trying to modify a parameter. That is also cleaner design.

The call stack and recursion

Each call pushes a stack frame holding its parameters and local variables; return pops it. Recursion works because each call gets its own frame — factorial(5) builds 5 frames and returns 120. Recursion with no base case (or too deep) fills the thread's stack and throws StackOverflowError (an Error, not an Exception) — typically after thousands to tens of thousands of frames; the default stack size can be raised with -Xss. Java does not do tail-call optimisation, so deep recursion (say 105 levels) should be rewritten as a loop.

static int factorial(int n) { return n <= 1 ? 1 : n * factorial(n - 1); }   // factorial(5) = 120
static void recurse() { recurse(); }   // StackOverflowError
Interview angle: "Is Java pass-by-reference?" Answer: "No. Java is always pass-by-value. For objects the value that is copied is the reference, so a method can mutate the object but cannot make the caller's variable point to a different object — a swap(a, b) method cannot work." Then show the swap example. Other favourites: "Can you overload by return type?" (no), "Which overload does f(5) call with f(long) and f(Integer)?" (long: widening before boxing), "What is m(null) with m(String) and m(Integer)?" (ambiguous — compile error).
Part 2 — Object-Oriented Programming

8. Classes & Objects

In plain words

A class is a blueprint: it says what data a thing holds (fields) and what it can do (methods). An object is one real thing built from that blueprint, living in memory with its own copy of the fields. new builds an object; a constructor is the code that sets it up.

Your variable does not hold the object itself. It holds a reference — an arrow pointing to the object on the heap. Two variables can point at the same object.

A class is the architect's drawing of a house; objects are the actual houses on the street. Each house has its own paint colour (field values), and your variable is the house's address written on a piece of paper — copy the paper and you still have one house.

Anatomy of a class

public class Pizza {
    // fields (state) -- every object gets its own copy
    private final String size;
    private int toppings;

    // static field -- ONE copy shared by the whole class
    private static int made = 0;

    // constructor -- same name as the class, NO return type
    public Pizza(String size) {
        this.size = size;       // 'this' = the object being built
        made++;
    }

    // instance method -- works on one object's state
    public void addTopping() { toppings++; }

    // static method -- belongs to the class, no 'this'
    public static int totalMade() { return made; }
}

Pizza p = new Pizza("large");   // declare a reference, create the object, link them

What new actually does

new Pizza("large") 1. Is class Pizza loaded and initialised? no -> load it, run static initialisers (only ONCE) 2. Allocate memory on the HEAP for the object (header + all instance fields) 3. Set every field to its default: 0, 0.0, false, '\u0000', null 4. Run the constructor chain: super(...) first, then field initialisers and instance blocks, then the rest of the constructor body 5. Return the REFERENCE, which is stored in variable p (on the stack)

Default values: fields yes, local variables no

TypeField defaultLocal variable
int, long, short, byte0No default. Reading it before assigning is a compile error ("variable might not have been initialized").
double, float0.0
booleanfalse
char'\u0000'
any reference (String, arrays, objects)null

Constructors

  • A constructor has the class's name and no return type. Writing public void Pizza() creates an ordinary method that happens to share the name — a classic trick question.
  • If you write no constructor, the compiler adds a public no-arg default constructor. The moment you write any constructor, that free one disappears — so new Pizza() stops compiling after you add Pizza(String).
  • Constructors are not inherited and cannot be static, final or abstract. They can be private (singletons, factory methods, utility classes).

Constructor chaining with this(...) and super(...)

this(...) calls another constructor of the same class; super(...) calls a constructor of the parent. Chaining keeps all the real set-up in one place instead of copy-pasting it into every overload.

class Pizza {
    private final String size, crust;
    private final int toppings;

    Pizza()                          { this("medium"); }              // -> next one
    Pizza(String size)               { this(size, "thin"); }
    Pizza(String size, String crust) { this(size, crust, 0); }
    Pizza(String size, String crust, int toppings) {                  // the one "real" constructor
        this.size = size; this.crust = crust; this.toppings = toppings;
    }
    @Override public String toString() { return size + "/" + crust + "/" + toppings; }
}

class Animal {
    final String name;
    Animal(String name) { this.name = name; }
}
class Dog extends Animal {
    Dog() { super("Rex"); }   // REQUIRED: Animal has no no-arg constructor
}

new Pizza();          // medium/thin/0
new Pizza("large");   // large/thin/0
new Dog().name;       // Rex
  • this(...) or super(...) must be the first statement, and you can have only one of them. (Java 25 relaxes this a little with "flexible constructor bodies", but in Java 21 it is a hard rule.)
  • If you write neither, the compiler inserts super() — which fails to compile if the parent has no no-arg constructor. That is the error "constructor Animal in class Animal cannot be applied to given types".
  • A cycle (A() calls this(1) which calls this()) is a compile error: "recursive constructor invocation".

Initialization order (verified)

This is the most-asked "what does this print?" question about classes. Here is a parent and child, each with a static field, a static block, an instance field, an instance block, and a constructor:

public class InitOrder {
    static class Parent {
        static String ps = log("1. Parent static field");
        static { log("2. Parent static block"); }
        String pf = log("5. Parent instance field");
        { log("6. Parent instance block"); }
        Parent() { log("7. Parent constructor"); }
    }
    static class Child extends Parent {
        static String cs = log("3. Child static field");
        static { log("4. Child static block"); }
        String cf = log("8. Child instance field");
        { log("9. Child instance block"); }
        Child() {
            super();                       // implicit if omitted
            log("10. Child constructor");
        }
    }
    static String log(String s) { System.out.println(s); return s; }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println("--- first new Child() ---");
        new Child();
        System.out.println("--- second new Child() ---");
        new Child();
    }
}
--- first new Child() ---
1. Parent static field
2. Parent static block
3. Child static field
4. Child static block
5. Parent instance field
6. Parent instance block
7. Parent constructor
8. Child instance field
9. Child instance block
10. Child constructor
--- second new Child() ---
5. Parent instance field
6. Parent instance block
7. Parent constructor
8. Child instance field
9. Child instance block
10. Child constructor
ONCE per class (first use): Parent statics -> Child statics (top to bottom, in source order) EVERY new Child(): Parent fields + blocks -> Parent ctor body -> Child fields + blocks -> Child ctor body

The rules behind it:

  • Static initialisers run once, when the class is first initialised (first new, first static method call, or first access to a non-constant static field). Parent before child.
  • Field initialisers and instance blocks are copied by the compiler into every constructor, right after the super(...) call. That is why they run after the parent constructor but before the rest of the child constructor.
  • Within one level, field initialisers and blocks run in the order they appear in the source.
  • A constructor that starts with this(...) does not re-run the field initialisers — only the constructor that calls super(...) does.

Common pitfalls

  • Comparing objects with == checks identity. new Pizza() == new Pizza() is false even with equal fields; use equals (section 13).
  • Calling an overridable method from a constructor lets a subclass run code before its own fields are set — you will see null (verified demo in section 10).
  • Letting this "escape" from a constructor (registering it as a listener, starting a thread with it) exposes a half-built object to other code.
Interview angle: expect "what is the difference between a class and an object?", "what happens when you write new?", "when does the default constructor exist?" and a print-the-output puzzle on static/instance blocks. Answer the puzzle with the two-line rule: statics once, parent first; then for each object, parent fields/blocks/constructor, then child fields/blocks/constructor.

9. Encapsulation & Access Modifiers

In plain words

Encapsulation means an object keeps its data private and lets the outside world touch it only through methods it chooses. The object guards its own rules (a balance never goes negative, an email is always valid) instead of trusting every caller to behave.

Access modifiers (private, package-private, protected, public) are the locks that make this possible — they decide who can see each class, field, method and constructor.

A cash machine. You cannot open the box and grab notes; you press buttons (methods), and the machine checks your PIN and your balance before anything moves. The machine can change its insides tomorrow and your buttons still work.

The four access levels

ModifierSame classSame packageSubclass in another packageEveryone elseTypical use
privateyesnononoFields, helper methods
(none) package-privateyesyesnonoInternals shared inside one package; tests in the same package
protectedyesyesyes, through inheritance onlynoHooks meant for subclasses
publicyesyesyesyesThe API

Rules people forget

  • A top-level class can only be public or package-private. Only nested classes can be private or protected. One public top-level class per file, and the file name must match it.
  • private is per class, not per object. Inside Point, you can read other.x of another Point — that is how equals works. An outer class and its nested classes can also read each other's private members.
  • protected includes package access. It is wider than package-private, not narrower — a common wrong answer.
  • Interface members are public by default (methods are public abstract, fields are public static final).

The protected subtlety (verified with javac)

A subclass in a different package can use a protected member only through its own type (this, or a reference to itself or its subclasses) — not through a reference of the parent type.

// file com/shop/model/Account.java
package com.shop.model;
public class Account {
    public    String owner = "amy";   // everyone
    protected int    level = 1;       // same package + subclasses
              double rate  = 0.05;    // package-private
    private   long   balance = 100;   // this class only
    public long getBalance() { return balance; }
}

// file com/shop/web/Premium.java  -- a DIFFERENT package
package com.shop.web;
import com.shop.model.Account;
public class Premium extends Account {
    int myLevel()            { return level; }     // OK: inherited, via 'this'
    int viaSelf(Premium p)   { return p.level; }   // OK: reference is a Premium
    int viaParent(Account a) { return a.level; }   // ERROR
    double r()               { return rate; }      // ERROR
}
Premium.java:8: error: level has protected access in Account
Premium.java:9: error: rate is not public in Account; cannot be accessed from outside package

Why? A random Account could be some other company's subclass. Protected means "you may use the parts you inherited", not "you may poke into every Account". Meanwhile a class in com.shop.model (same package) can read a.level and a.rate freely.

Getters, setters and why encapsulation matters

Making fields private and adding a getter and setter for each one is not encapsulation by itself — it is a public field with extra steps. Real encapsulation means the class exposes behaviour and protects its invariants (rules that must always be true).

// Leaky: private field, but the getter hands out the internal list
class LeakyCart {
    private final List<String> items = new ArrayList<>();
    public List<String> getItems() { return items; }
}
LeakyCart cart = new LeakyCart();
cart.getItems().add("hacked");        // caller changed private state
cart.getItems();                      // [hacked]

// Encapsulated: validation inside, read-only view out
class BankAccount {
    private long balanceCents;
    private final List<String> history = new ArrayList<>();

    public long getBalanceCents() { return balanceCents; }       // read: fine

    public void deposit(long cents) {                            // no setBalance()!
        if (cents <= 0) throw new IllegalArgumentException("deposit must be positive");
        balanceCents += cents;
        history.add("+" + cents);
    }
    public void withdraw(long cents) {
        if (cents > balanceCents) throw new IllegalStateException("insufficient funds");
        balanceCents -= cents;
        history.add("-" + cents);
    }
    public List<String> getHistory() { return Collections.unmodifiableList(history); }
}
acc.deposit(500); acc.withdraw(200);
acc.getBalanceCents() + " " + acc.getHistory();   // 300 [+500, -200]
acc.withdraw(10_000);                             // IllegalStateException: insufficient funds
acc.getHistory().add("+1000000");                 // UnsupportedOperationException
BenefitWhat it buys you
InvariantsValidation lives in one place; the object can never be in an illegal state.
Freedom to changeStore cents instead of a double, add caching, switch to a database — callers never notice.
Thread safetyIf all access goes through methods, you can add locking in one place.
DebuggingA breakpoint in one setter catches every change; a public field can be changed from anywhere.

Best practice

  • Make every field private (and final if it never changes). Widen only when you have a reason.
  • Prefer intention-revealing methods (withdraw, markShipped) over raw setters. "Tell, don't ask."
  • Never return an internal mutable collection or array. Return an unmodifiable view, a copy, or List.copyOf.
  • For pure data carriers, a record (section 15) gives private final fields and accessors for free.
Interview angle: "What is the difference between protected and default access?" (protected adds subclasses in other packages, and only through inheritance). "Is a class with getters and setters for every field encapsulated?" (not really — it still leaks its structure and has no rules). "Can a top-level class be private?" (no).

10. Inheritance

In plain words

Inheritance lets one class (the subclass or child) take all the fields and methods of another (the superclass or parent) with extends, then add new ones or replace (override) some. It models an IS-A relationship: a Dog is an Animal.

Java allows a class to extend only one class, but implement many interfaces. Every class ultimately extends java.lang.Object.

A new car model built on last year's platform. The engine, wheels and seats come along for free; the designers add a sunroof and replace the headlights. But if the platform's engineers quietly change the chassis, every model built on it is affected.

extends, super and overriding

class Animal {
    protected String name;
    Animal(String name) { this.name = name; }
    String sound()    { return "..."; }
    String describe() { return name + " says " + sound(); }
    Animal reproduce() { return new Animal(name + " Jr"); }
    void save() throws IOException { }
    final String kingdom() { return "Animalia"; }       // cannot be overridden
}

class Dog extends Animal {
    Dog(String name) { super(name); }                    // parent constructor first
    @Override String sound() { return "Woof"; }
    @Override String describe() { return super.describe() + "!"; }   // reuse parent code
    @Override Dog reproduce() { return new Dog(name + " Jr"); }      // covariant return
    @Override public void save() throws FileNotFoundException { }    // wider access, narrower exception
}

Animal a = new Dog("Rex");
a.describe();                       // "Rex says Woof!"  -- parent code calls the CHILD's sound()
Dog pup = new Dog("Rex").reproduce();  // no cast needed thanks to the covariant return
pup.name + " " + pup.kingdom();     // "Rex Jr Animalia"

super.method() calls the parent's version; super(...) calls the parent's constructor. super.super.x() does not exist.

Method overriding rules

RuleAllowedNot allowed
Name and parameter typesExactly the same (otherwise it is an overload, not an override)—
Return typeSame, or a subtype (covariant return, Java 5)A different or wider type; for primitives it must be identical
AccessSame or wider (protected → public)Narrower (public → package-private)
Checked exceptionsSame, a subclass, or none; any unchecked exceptionsNew or broader checked exceptions
Modifiers on parent—final methods; static methods are hidden, not overridden; private methods are not inherited at all

The reasoning is the Liskov substitution principle: code written against Animal must keep working when handed a Dog. A narrower access or a new checked exception would break that caller. Here is what javac says when you break the rules:

class Dog extends Animal {
    String sound() { return "Woof"; }    // parent's is public
    void save() throws Exception { }     // parent throws IOException
    String kingdom() { return "x"; }     // parent's is final
}
final class Money { }
class Coins extends Money { }
error: sound() in Dog cannot override sound() in Animal
  attempting to assign weaker access privileges; was public
error: save() in Dog cannot override save() in Animal
  overridden method does not throw Exception
error: kingdom() in Dog cannot override kingdom() in Animal
  overridden method is final
error: cannot inherit from final Money

Always write @Override. If you misspell the name or get a parameter type wrong (equals(Point p) instead of equals(Object o)), the annotation turns a silent overload into a compile error.

final classes and methods

  • A final method cannot be overridden: use it when the parent's logic must not change (security checks, template-method skeletons).
  • A final class cannot be extended: String, Integer and all wrappers, LocalDate, every record. This guarantees nobody can create a "String" subclass that mutates or lies about its contents.
  • Java 17 adds a middle ground: sealed classes list exactly which subclasses are allowed (section 12).

Why no multiple class inheritance: the diamond problem

A { void hello() } / \ B C both override hello() differently \ / D D extends B, C ? new D().hello() -- B's or C's? and does D hold ONE copy of A's fields or TWO?

Java refuses class D extends B, C to avoid this ambiguity about both behaviour and state. Interfaces had no state and no code, so implementing many was always safe. Java 8 default methods brought code into interfaces, so the diamond came back for methods (never for fields). The resolution rules are:

  1. Class wins. A method in the class or any superclass beats any interface default.
  2. More specific interface wins. If B extends A and both have a default, B's is used.
  3. Otherwise you must override it, and you may pick one with X.super.method().
interface Swimmer { default String move() { return "swim"; } }
interface Flyer   { default String move() { return "fly"; } }

class Duck implements Swimmer, Flyer {
    @Override public String move() {               // required: otherwise compile error
        return Swimmer.super.move() + " + " + Flyer.super.move();
    }
}
new Duck().move();   // "swim + fly"
// Without the override:
// error: types Swimmer and Flyer are incompatible;
//   class Duck inherits unrelated defaults for move() from types Swimmer and Flyer

Pitfall: overridable method called from a constructor

class Base {
    Base() { System.out.println("Base ctor sees: " + label()); }
    String label() { return "base"; }
}
class Derived extends Base {
    private String tag = "derived";          // assigned AFTER Base() finishes
    @Override String label() { return tag; }
}
new Derived();   // prints "Base ctor sees: null"

Dynamic dispatch works even inside the parent's constructor, so Derived.label() runs before tag is initialised (see the order in section 8). Rule: constructors should call only private, final or static methods.

Composition over inheritance

Inheritance couples you to the parent's implementation, not just its contract. The famous example from Effective Java: count how many elements were ever added to a set.

class CountingSet<E> extends HashSet<E> {
    int added = 0;
    @Override public boolean add(E e) { added++; return super.add(e); }
    @Override public boolean addAll(Collection<? extends E> c) {
        added += c.size(); return super.addAll(c);
    }
}
s.addAll(List.of("a", "b", "c"));
s.added;   // 6, not 3 -- HashSet.addAll() internally calls add() for each element

class CountingSet2<E> {                           // composition: HAS-A set
    private final Set<E> inner = new HashSet<>();
    int added = 0;
    boolean add(E e) { added++; return inner.add(e); }
    boolean addAll(Collection<? extends E> c) { added += c.size(); return inner.addAll(c); }
}
s2.added;  // 3

This is the fragile base class problem. Use inheritance for a true IS-A relationship where the parent was designed to be extended; otherwise wrap an object and delegate to it.

Interview angle: "Why doesn't Java support multiple inheritance?" (diamond problem for state and behaviour; interfaces give multiple types without it). "What happens if two interfaces have the same default method?" (compile error until you override; use X.super.m()). "Can you override a static or private method?" (no — static is hidden, private is invisible). "Can an overriding method throw a broader exception?" (not a checked one).

11. Polymorphism

In plain words

Polymorphism ("many forms") means one piece of code can work with many types. You write shape.draw() once, and a circle draws a circle, a square draws a square.

Java has two kinds. Compile-time polymorphism is overloading: several methods with the same name but different parameters; the compiler picks one by looking at the argument types. Runtime polymorphism is overriding: the JVM looks at the actual object at run time and calls its version — this is dynamic dispatch.

A universal remote with a "power" button. You press the same button; the TV, the speaker and the projector each turn on in their own way. The remote does not need to know which device it is pointing at.

Overloading vs overriding

OverloadingOverriding
Also calledCompile-time / static polymorphismRuntime / dynamic polymorphism
WhereSame class (or inherited)Subclass redefines a parent method
SignatureSame name, different parametersSame name and parameters
Return typeAnythingSame or covariant
Decided byCompiler, using the declared type of the argumentsJVM, using the runtime type of the object
Works with static / private / finalYesNo

One demo, all the rules (verified)

class Shape {
    String name = "shape";                          // field
    static String kind() { return "Shape.kind"; }   // static
    String draw() { return "drawing a shape"; }     // instance
}
class Circle extends Shape {
    String name = "circle";                         // HIDES Shape.name (a second field)
    static String kind() { return "Circle.kind"; }  // HIDES Shape.kind()
    @Override String draw() { return "drawing a circle"; }
    double radius() { return 1.0; }
}
class Square extends Shape {
    @Override String draw() { return "drawing a square"; }
}

static String greet(Shape s)  { return "greet(Shape)"; }
static String greet(Circle c) { return "greet(Circle)"; }

Shape s = new Circle();        // upcast: implicit, always safe
s.draw();                      // "drawing a circle"  -- runtime type decides
s.name;                        // "shape"             -- fields: declared type decides
((Circle) s).name;             // "circle"
s.kind();                      // "Shape.kind"        -- static: declared type decides
greet(s);                      // "greet(Shape)"      -- overload chosen at compile time
