  • Only instance methods are polymorphic. Fields and static methods are resolved from the declared type of the variable. A subclass field with the same name is a second, separate field (field hiding) — avoid it.
  • Static methods are hidden, not overridden. Calling a static method through an instance compiles but javac -Xlint warns: "static method should be qualified by type name, Shape, instead of by an expression".
  • Overload choice is fixed at compile time: greet(s) picks greet(Shape) even though s holds a Circle. Overloading does not dispatch on runtime type.

How dynamic dispatch works inside

HotSpot calls this the vtable (virtual method table); interfaces use a similar itable. The JIT compiler often removes the lookup entirely: if only one class is ever seen at a call site, it inlines that method directly ("monomorphic inline cache"), so polymorphism is effectively free in hot code.

Overload resolution order

When several overloads could match, the compiler tries in three phases and stops at the first that works:

  1. Exact match or widening of primitives (int → long), no boxing.
  2. Boxing / unboxing (int → Integer).
  3. Varargs.
static String pick(long x)    { return "long (widening)"; }
static String pick(Integer x) { return "Integer (boxing)"; }
static String pick(int... x)  { return "varargs"; }
pick(5);   // "long (widening)" -- widening beats boxing beats varargs

If two overloads are equally good (for example f(String) and f(Integer) called with null), it is a compile error: "reference to f is ambiguous".

Upcasting, downcasting and pattern matching

Shape[] shapes = { new Circle(), new Square(), new Shape() };
for (Shape sh : shapes) sh.draw();   // circle, square, shape -- one loop, many forms

Shape sq = new Square();
Circle c = (Circle) sq;              // compiles, but throws ClassCastException at run time

// Java 16+: instanceof with a pattern variable -- test and cast in one step
if (s instanceof Circle c && c.radius() > 0) {
    System.out.println("radius " + c.radius());   // radius 1.0
}

// Java 21: pattern matching for switch
String out = switch (sh) {
    case Circle c -> "circle r=" + c.radius();
    case Square q -> "square";
    default       -> "plain shape";
};
// circle r=1.0, square, plain shape
  • Upcast (child to parent) is automatic and always safe; you only lose access to child-only methods.
  • Downcast (parent to child) needs an explicit cast and is checked at run time. Casting between unrelated classes ((String) someInteger) is a compile error.
  • Order matters in a pattern switch: a case Shape s placed before case Circle c is a compile error ("this case label is dominated by a preceding case label").
  • A long instanceof chain is often a sign that the behaviour belongs in an overridden method. Pattern switches shine over sealed hierarchies and records (section 12, 15), where the compiler checks you covered every case.
Interview angle: the classic puzzle is Parent p = new Child(); p.field; p.staticMethod(); p.method(); — answer: parent's field, parent's static method, child's instance method. Then "is overloading polymorphism?" (yes, compile-time; overriding is runtime). And "how does the JVM find the right method?" (object → class → vtable slot, inlined by the JIT when only one type is seen).

12. Abstract Classes vs Interfaces

In plain words

An abstract class is a half-finished class: it can hold fields, constructors and working methods, but also leaves some methods abstract (no body) for subclasses to fill in. You cannot new it. A class can extend only one.

An interface is a contract: a list of things a type promises it can do. Since Java 8 it can also carry some ready-made code (default and static methods), but it has no instance state. A class can implement many.

An abstract class is a franchise kit: the kitchen, the recipes and the logo come built in, and you add the local menu. An interface is a certification badge like "wheelchair accessible" — any building can earn it, whatever else it is, and a building can hold many badges.

Side by side

FeatureAbstract classInterface
Instantiate with newNoNo (but lambdas / anonymous classes can implement it)
How many per classExtend oneImplement many
Instance fields (state)Yes, any kindNo — only public static final constants
ConstructorsYes (called via super(...))No
Method bodiesAny methoddefault and static (Java 8), private (Java 9)
Abstract methodsMust say abstractImplicitly public abstract
Member accessAny modifierPublic by default; private allowed for helpers (Java 9)
Restrict who extendssealed ... permits (Java 17)sealed ... permits (Java 17)
RelationshipIS-A, shared identity and codeCAN-DO, a capability
Lambda targetNoYes, if exactly one abstract method (functional interface)

Abstract class: shared state and a template method

abstract class Report {
    private final String title;                    // state lives here
    protected Report(String title) { this.title = title; }

    public final String render() {                 // template method: the fixed skeleton
        return "== " + title + " ==\n" + body() + "\n-- end --";
    }
    protected abstract String body();              // the step subclasses fill in
}

class SalesReport extends Report {
    SalesReport() { super("Sales"); }
    @Override protected String body() { return "total: 42"; }
}

System.out.println(new SalesReport().render());
// == Sales ==
// total: 42
// -- end --

A class with even one abstract method must itself be abstract. An abstract class may have zero abstract methods — that just stops people instantiating it. An abstract method cannot be private, static or final (each would make overriding impossible).

Interface: Java 8 default and static, Java 9 private

interface Discount {
    double apply(double price);                    // abstract

    default double applyTwice(double price) {      // Java 8: implementers inherit this
        return round(apply(apply(price)));
    }
    static Discount percent(double p) {            // Java 8: factory, called as Discount.percent(..)
        return price -> price * (1 - p / 100);
    }
    private double round(double v) {               // Java 9: helper hidden from implementers
        return Math.round(v * 100) / 100.0;
    }
    int MAX_PERCENT = 90;                          // implicitly public static final
}

Discount tenOff = Discount.percent(10);
tenOff.apply(200);         // 180.0
tenOff.applyTwice(99.99);  // 80.99
Discount.MAX_PERCENT;      // 90

Why default methods exist: Java 8 wanted to add stream() and forEach() to Collection. Adding an abstract method would have broken every existing implementation in the world; a default method gives them a working version for free. Static interface methods are not inherited by implementing classes — you must call Discount.percent(...).

Java 17: sealed interfaces

sealed interface Payment permits Card, Upi, Cash {}
record Card(String number) implements Payment {}
record Upi(String vpa)     implements Payment {}
record Cash()              implements Payment {}

static String fee(Payment p) {
    return switch (p) {                            // exhaustive: no default needed (Java 21)
        case Card c -> "2% card fee";
        case Upi u  -> "no fee for " + u.vpa();
        case Cash c -> "no fee";
    };
}
// 2% card fee / no fee for amy@bank / no fee

Every permitted subtype must be final (records are), sealed, or non-sealed. Because the compiler knows the full list, a pattern switch needs no default; add a fourth payment type and every switch that forgot it stops compiling. This is Java's version of an algebraic data type.

Which one to pick

  • Default to an interface. It defines a type without locking callers into a hierarchy, and a class can mix in several (Comparable, Serializable, AutoCloseable).
  • Use an abstract class when subclasses genuinely share state or constructor logic, or you need non-public members, or a template method with a fixed skeleton (AbstractList, HttpServlet).
  • A common combination: an interface for the type plus an abstract "skeletal implementation" (List + AbstractList). Callers depend on the interface; implementers extend the skeleton to save work.
  • Use sealed when the set of subtypes is closed and known (payment kinds, AST nodes, results like Success / Failure).
Interview angle: "Now that interfaces have default methods, what is the difference from an abstract class?" — interfaces still have no instance state and no constructors, and a class can implement many. "Why were default methods added?" — to evolve interfaces like Collection without breaking existing implementations. "Can an interface have a private method?" — yes, since Java 9. "What is a functional interface?" — exactly one abstract method, so a lambda can implement it (@FunctionalInterface makes the compiler check).

13. The Object Class: equals, hashCode, toString

In plain words

Every class in Java secretly extends java.lang.Object, so every object has a few built-in methods. The important ones: equals (are two objects "the same" in meaning?), hashCode (a number used to find the object quickly in hash tables) and toString (a text version for printing and logs).

The defaults compare and hash by identity (the memory object), which is rarely what you want for value-like classes. If you override equals, you must override hashCode too, or HashMap and HashSet silently break.

A library. equals decides whether two books are the same title; hashCode is the shelf number. If two copies of the same title get different shelf numbers, the librarian looks on the wrong shelf and says "we don't have it" while it sits two aisles away.

Methods every object has

MethodDefault behaviourOverride?
equals(Object)this == o (identity)For value classes
hashCode()Identity-based number (not the memory address in modern JVMs)Always together with equals
toString()ClassName@hexHashAlmost always
getClass()The runtime Class objectCannot (final)
clone()Protected; shallow field copy if the class implements CloneableRarely; prefer copy constructors
wait(), notify(), notifyAll()Thread coordination on the object's monitorCannot (final)
finalize()Called before GC, maybe neverNever — deprecated since Java 9, for removal since 18

The equals contract

  • Reflexive: x.equals(x) is true.
  • Symmetric: x.equals(y) if and only if y.equals(x).
  • Transitive: if x.equals(y) and y.equals(z), then x.equals(z).
  • Consistent: the answer does not change unless the objects change.
  • Non-null: x.equals(null) is false (never throws).

The hashCode contract

  • If a.equals(b), then a.hashCode() == b.hashCode(). This is the one that matters.
  • Equal hash codes do not require equal objects (collisions are allowed, just slower).
  • The value must stay the same while the fields used in equals do not change.

Broken HashSet: equals without hashCode (verified)

class BadPoint {
    final int x, y;
    BadPoint(int x, int y) { this.x = x; this.y = y; }
    @Override public boolean equals(Object o) {
        return o instanceof BadPoint p && x == p.x && y == p.y;
    }
    // no hashCode()!
}
Set<BadPoint> bad = new HashSet<>();
bad.add(new BadPoint(1, 2));
bad.add(new BadPoint(1, 2));
new BadPoint(1, 2).equals(new BadPoint(1, 2));   // true
bad.size();                                      // 2   -- a "duplicate" got in
bad.contains(new BadPoint(1, 2));                // false -- can't find an equal element

A correct equals and hashCode

final class Point {
    private final int x, y;
    Point(int x, int y) { this.x = x; this.y = y; }

    @Override public boolean equals(Object o) {
        if (this == o) return true;                    // fast path
        if (!(o instanceof Point p)) return false;     // also handles null
        return x == p.x && y == p.y;
    }
    @Override public int hashCode() { return Objects.hash(x, y); }
    @Override public String toString() { return "Point(" + x + ", " + y + ")"; }
}
good.add(new Point(1, 2)); good.add(new Point(1, 2));
good.size();                       // 1
good.contains(new Point(1, 2));    // true
good;                              // [Point(1, 2)]
  • The parameter must be Object. equals(Point p) is an overload; collections call equals(Object) and never see it. @Override catches this.
  • Compare double fields with Double.compare, objects with Objects.equals(a, b) (null-safe), arrays with Arrays.equals.
  • instanceof vs getClass(): with instanceof, a subclass that adds a field (ColorPoint) breaks symmetry — point.equals(colorPoint) is true but not the reverse. Either make the class final (as above) or use getClass() != o.getClass(), which treats subclasses as never equal.
  • Records (section 15) generate a correct equals, hashCode and toString from their components.

Mutable keys: the other way to lose an entry

Set<Tag> tags = new HashSet<>();
Tag t = new Tag("java");            // equals/hashCode use 'name'
tags.add(t);
t.name = "kotlin";                  // mutate AFTER inserting
tags.contains(t);                   // false -- looks in the bucket for "kotlin"
tags.size();                        // 1     -- the entry is stuck in the "java" bucket

Never change a field that equals/hashCode use while the object is a key in a map or an element of a set. The safest fix: make keys immutable (String, records, enums).

toString

The default prints ObjectMethods$BadPoint@ followed by a hex hash — useless in a log. Override it for any class you will ever print or debug. Keep it cheap, never throw from it, and never include secrets (passwords, tokens), because logs end up everywhere.

clone: shallow vs deep

class Team implements Cloneable {
    String name;
    List<String> members = new ArrayList<>();
    @Override public Team clone() {
        try { return (Team) super.clone(); }          // copies fields one by one
        catch (CloneNotSupportedException e) { throw new AssertionError(e); }
    }
    Team deepCopy() {                                 // copy-constructor style
        Team t = new Team(name);
        t.members = new ArrayList<>(members);        // NEW list with the same elements
        return t;
    }
}
a.members.add("amy");
Team shallow = a.clone(), deep = a.deepCopy();
shallow.members.add("bob");
a.members + " " + shallow.members + " " + deep.members;   // [amy, bob] [amy, bob] [amy]
a.members == shallow.members;                             // true -- one shared list
shallow clone: a.members ──┐ ├──> [amy, bob] one list, two owners shallow.members ┘ deep copy: a.members ─────────> [amy, bob] deep.members ──────> [amy] separate list

Cloneable is widely considered a broken design: it is a marker interface with no clone method, Object.clone skips constructors, and it does not work with final fields that need a fresh copy. Prefer a copy constructor (new Team(other)) or a static factory (Team.copyOf(other)). Arrays are the exception: array.clone() is fine (and shallow).

Interview angle: "What happens if you override equals but not hashCode?" — equal objects land in different buckets, so HashSet keeps duplicates and contains/get fail. "Can two unequal objects have the same hash code?" — yes, that is a collision. "What is the difference between == and equals?" — identity vs logical equality. "Shallow vs deep copy?" — shared vs copied inner objects.

14. Nested, Inner & Anonymous Classes

In plain words

Java lets you write a class inside another class, or even inside a method. There are four kinds: a static nested class (just a class stored inside another for organisation), an inner class (each object is tied to one object of the outer class), a local class (declared inside a method) and an anonymous class (a one-off class with no name, written right where it is used).

The big practical difference: an inner (non-static) class secretly holds a reference to its outer object. That is handy, but it can keep a large outer object alive in memory long after you are done with it.

A static nested class is a separate booklet stored in the same folder. An inner class is a sticky note glued to one specific page — wherever the note goes, it drags the page along with it.

The four kinds

KindDeclaredNeeds an outer object?Can accessTypical use
Static nestedIn a class, with staticNoOuter's static members (including private)Builders, Map.Entry-style helpers, holder idiom
Inner (member)In a class, no staticYes: outer.new Inner()Everything in the outer objectIterators over the outer collection
LocalInside a method or blockYes, if the method is an instance methodOuter members + effectively final localsA helper used by one method only
AnonymousInline with new Type() { ... }Yes, in an instance contextSame as localOne-off implementations; mostly replaced by lambdas

All four in one program (verified)

public class Nested {
    private int outerValue = 10;
    private static int counter = 0;

    static class StaticNested {                  // no link to a Nested object
        int read() { return counter; }           // only static members
    }
    class Inner {                                // tied to one Nested object
        int outerValue = 20;
        int read() { return outerValue + Nested.this.outerValue; }   // 20 + 10
    }

    Runnable localAndAnonymous(String prefix) {
        int base = 5;                            // effectively final
        class Local {                            // visible only inside this method
            String show() { return prefix + (base + outerValue); }
        }
        return new Runnable() {                  // anonymous class
            @Override public void run() {
                System.out.println(new Local().show() + ", anon class = " + this.getClass().getName());
            }
        };
    }
    Runnable lambda() {
        return () -> System.out.println("lambda this is outer? " + (this.getClass() == Nested.class));
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Nested outer = new Nested();
        StaticNested sn = new Nested.StaticNested();     // no outer instance needed
        Nested.Inner in = outer.new Inner();             // needs an outer instance
        System.out.println(sn.read() + " " + in.read()); // 0 30
        outer.localAndAnonymous("sum=").run();           // sum=15, anon class = Nested$1
        outer.lambda().run();                            // lambda this is outer? true
    }
}
  • Nested.this.outerValue reaches the outer field when the inner class shadows the name.
  • Anonymous classes compile to Outer$1, Outer$2 …; nested ones to Outer$Inner.
  • Inside an anonymous class, this is the anonymous object. Inside a lambda, this is the enclosing object — a lambda is not a class of its own and does not introduce a new scope.
  • Local and anonymous classes (and lambdas) can use a local variable only if it is effectively final (never reassigned). Adding base++ gives "local variables referenced from an inner class must be final or effectively final". The value is copied into the object, so allowing changes would create two versions that drift apart.
  • Since Java 16, inner classes may declare static members, and you can declare local records, enums and interfaces.

The hidden reference and memory leaks

The compiler gives every inner class that uses its outer object a hidden field, this$0, pointing to that object. Reflection shows it:

for (Field f : Inner.class.getDeclaredFields())
    System.out.println("Inner field: " + f.getName() + (f.isSynthetic() ? " (synthetic)" : ""));
// Inner field: outerValue
// Inner field: this$0 (synthetic)      <-- reference to the outer object

class Unused { int x; }                  // an inner class that never touches Outer
// Unused fields: [x]                    -- javac 18+ drops this$0 when it is not used
StaticNested.class.getDeclaredFields().length;   // 0 -- no outer reference at all
  • Rule of thumb (Effective Java): if a nested class does not need the outer instance, make it static. It is smaller, cannot leak, and can be created without an outer object.
  • Anonymous classes created in instance methods can capture this too. A lambda captures this only if its body actually uses an instance member.
  • Inner-class objects are also a serialization trap: serializing one drags in the outer object.

Where each one shows up

  • Static nested: Builder in the builder pattern, Map.Entry, the lazy holder idiom for singletons (section 16), private node classes (LinkedList.Node).
  • Inner: ArrayList.Itr — the iterator needs the list's array and size.
  • Anonymous: pre-Java 8 callbacks (new Comparator<>() {...}), or when you need to implement an interface with several methods or extend an abstract class inline.
  • Lambda: the modern replacement for anonymous classes of functional interfaces (section 24).
Interview angle: "Static nested vs inner class?" — inner holds a reference to an outer instance, needs outer.new Inner(), and can leak memory. "Why must captured variables be effectively final?" — the value is copied into the object; mutation would make the two copies disagree (and cause races across threads). "How is this different in a lambda and an anonymous class?" — lambda: enclosing object; anonymous: itself.

15. Enums & Records

In plain words

An enum is a type with a fixed, known list of values — days of the week, order statuses, planets. Each value is a real object, created once, so enums can have fields, constructors and methods, and you compare them safely with ==.

A record (Java 16) is a short way to write a class that is just data. You list the fields once; Java writes the constructor, the accessors, equals, hashCode and toString, and makes every field private final.

An enum is the set of sizes on a coffee menu: small, medium, large — you cannot order "extra-medium". A record is a filled-in form: once printed, two forms with the same answers are considered the same form.

Enums are classes

enum Planet {
    MERCURY(3.303e+23, 2.4397e6),
    EARTH(5.976e+24, 6.37814e6);                    // constants first, then a semicolon

    private final double mass, radius;              // fields
    Planet(double mass, double radius) {            // constructor is always private
        this.mass = mass; this.radius = radius;
    }
    double surfaceGravity() { return 6.67300E-11 * mass / (radius * radius); }
}
Planet.EARTH.surfaceGravity();                      // 9.80 (printed with %.2f)

// Constant-specific bodies: each constant has its own implementation
enum Op {
    PLUS("+")  { int apply(int a, int b) { return a + b; } },
    TIMES("*") { int apply(int a, int b) { return a * b; } };
    final String symbol;
    Op(String symbol) { this.symbol = symbol; }
    abstract int apply(int a, int b);
}
Op.TIMES.apply(6, 7);                               // 42
enum Day { MON, TUE, ... } compiles to roughly: public final class Day extends java.lang.Enum<Day> { public static final Day MON = new Day("MON", 0); created once, when Day is initialised public static final Day TUE = new Day("TUE", 1); ... public static Day[] values() -- a fresh copy of all constants public static Day valueOf(String name) -- exact-name lookup, throws if unknown }

Built-in methods, switch and the enum collections

enum Day { MON, TUE, WED, THU, FRI, SAT, SUN }

Day.valueOf("WED").ordinal();     // 2   (position, starting at 0)
Day.values().length;              // 7
Day.FRI.name();                   // "FRI"
Day.SAT.compareTo(Day.MON);       // 5   (compares ordinals)
Day.valueOf("Funday");            // IllegalArgumentException

String type = switch (d) {        // case labels are written WITHOUT "Day."
    case SAT, SUN -> "weekend";
    default       -> "weekday";
};                                // "weekend" for SAT

EnumMap<Day, Integer> hours = new EnumMap<>(Day.class);
hours.put(Day.FRI, 6); hours.put(Day.MON, 8);
hours;                            // {MON=8, FRI=6}  -- always in declaration order
EnumSet.range(Day.MON, Day.WED);  // [MON, TUE, WED]
  • EnumMap is backed by a plain array indexed by ordinal, and EnumSet by a bit mask (one long for up to 64 constants). Both are faster and smaller than HashMap/HashSet — always prefer them for enum keys.
  • Compare enums with ==: it is null-safe and each constant exists exactly once.
  • Never persist ordinal() (in a database or file). Inserting a new constant in the middle shifts every number. Store name() or an explicit code field.
  • A switch over an enum in a switch expression (Java 14) must be exhaustive; if you list every constant, no default is needed.

Singleton via enum

enum IdGenerator {
    INSTANCE;
    private long next = 1;
    synchronized long nextId() { return next++; }
}
IdGenerator.INSTANCE.nextId();   // 1
IdGenerator.INSTANCE.nextId();   // 2

Effective Java calls this the best way to write a singleton: the JVM guarantees exactly one instance, creation is thread-safe, serialization cannot create a second copy, and reflection cannot call the constructor (Constructor.newInstance refuses enum types). Its only limit: an enum cannot extend another class.

Records (Java 16)

record Money(long cents, String currency) {
    Money {                                        // COMPACT constructor: no parameter list
        if (cents < 0) throw new IllegalArgumentException("negative: " + cents);
        currency = currency.toUpperCase();         // reassign the PARAMETER; fields set after
    }
    Money add(Money o) { return new Money(cents + o.cents, currency); }   // returns a new object
    static Money zero(String cur) { return new Money(0, cur); }
}

Money m = new Money(500, "usd");
m;                                  // Money[cents=500, currency=USD]
m.cents();                          // 500   -- accessor is cents(), not getCents()
m.equals(new Money(500, "USD"));    // true  -- compares all components
m.add(Money.zero("usd")) == m;      // false -- a new object every time
new Money(-1, "usd");               // IllegalArgumentException: negative: -1
You writeThe compiler generates
record Money(long cents, String currency)private final fields cents, currency; a canonical constructor; accessors cents() and currency(); equals, hashCode, toString based on all components. The class is implicitly final and extends java.lang.Record.
  • The compact constructor is for validation and normalisation. The assignments this.cents = cents are added automatically at the end, so you work on the parameters.
  • A record may add methods, static fields and static factories, and implement interfaces. It may not declare extra instance fields or extend a class.
  • Records are shallowly immutable. A List component can still be changed by whoever holds that list — copy it: 
    record Team(String name, List<String> members) {
    Team { members = List.copyOf(members); }       // defensive copy, also unmodifiable
}
List<String> src = new ArrayList<>(List.of("amy"));
Team t = new Team("core", src);
src.add("bob");
t;                                                 // Team[name=core, members=[amy]]
  • Java 21 record patterns destructure them: if (obj instanceof Money(long c, String cur)) ... or case Money(var c, var cur) -> ... in a switch.

When not to use a record

  • JPA / Hibernate entities — they need a no-arg constructor, mutable fields and proxies (subclasses). Records fit DTOs and query projections instead.
  • The object must change over time, or has identity beyond its values (two users with the same name are still two users).
  • You need to hide some state, compute lazy/cached fields, or extend a base class.
  • The public API would lock you in: every component becomes a public accessor and part of equals, and renaming one breaks callers.
Interview angle: "Can an enum have a constructor? Can it be public?" — yes; no, it is always private. "Why is enum the best singleton?" — one instance guaranteed by the JVM, safe against serialization and reflection. "Is a record immutable?" — its fields are final, but only shallowly; copy mutable components. "Record vs Lombok @Data?" — record is built in, final and immutable; @Data generates setters and a mutable class.

16. static, final & Immutability

In plain words

static means "belongs to the class, not to any one object": there is one copy, shared by everyone, usable without new. final means "cannot be changed after it is set": a final variable is assigned once, a final method cannot be overridden, and a final class cannot be extended.

An immutable object is one whose state can never change after construction — like String. To "change" it, you create a new one. Immutable objects are automatically thread-safe and make perfect map keys.

static is the office whiteboard: one board, everyone reads and writes the same thing. final is writing in pen instead of pencil. An immutable object is a printed and laminated card — if you want different text, you print a new card.

static in all its forms

WhereMeaningExample
FieldOne copy per class, shared by all objectsstatic int instances;
MethodCalled on the class; no this, cannot touch instance fields directlyMath.max, factories
BlockRuns once, when the class is initialisedLoading a lookup table
Nested classNo hidden reference to an outer objectBuilder, holder idiom
ImportUse static members without the class nameimport static java.lang.Math.*;
public class StaticDemo {
    static final int MAX = 100;                         // compile-time constant (inlined by javac)
    static final Map<String, Integer> CODES;
    static {                                            // runs once, on first use of the class
        CODES = Map.of("IN", 91, "US", 1);
        System.out.println("StaticDemo initialised");
    }
    static class Holder {                               // lazy, thread-safe singleton idiom
        static { System.out.println("Holder initialised"); }
        static final StaticDemo INSTANCE = new StaticDemo();
    }
    static StaticDemo get() { return Holder.INSTANCE; }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println("main starts, MAX = " + MAX + ", IN = " + CODES.get("IN"));
        System.out.println(get() == get());
    }
}
// StaticDemo initialised
// main starts, MAX = 100, IN = 91
// Holder initialised           <-- only when get() is first called
// true

The holder idiom works because the JVM initialises a class lazily and under a lock: Holder is not touched until get() runs, and the JVM guarantees its static initialiser runs exactly once, even with many threads.

  • A static method cannot use instance state: static void f() { count++; } gives "non-static variable count cannot be referenced from a static context".
  • Static fields live with the class (in the Class object on the heap in modern HotSpot), so a static List or Map that keeps growing is a classic memory leak — it is never garbage collected while the class is loaded.
  • Static mutable state is global state: hard to test, and shared by all threads. Prefer constants (static final) and pass everything else in.

final in all its forms

Applied toEffect
Local variable / parameterAssigned once
Instance fieldMust be assigned exactly once by the end of every constructor ("blank final" if not assigned at declaration). Gets a JMM guarantee: other threads see its constructed value once the object is safely published.
Static fieldAssigned once, at declaration or in a static block. A static final primitive or String with a constant value is a compile-time constant and gets inlined into calling classes.
MethodCannot be overridden
ClassCannot be extended (String, Integer, records)
final List<String> names = new ArrayList<>();
names.add("still mutable");          // allowed -- the OBJECT changes, the reference does not
names = new ArrayList<>();           // error: cannot assign a value to final variable names

int limit = 3;                       // never reassigned -> EFFECTIVELY FINAL (Java 8)
Runnable r = () -> System.out.println("limit " + limit);   // OK, prints "limit 3"
// adding limit++ anywhere gives:
// error: local variables referenced from a lambda expression must be final or effectively final

final reference is not an immutable object. final freezes the arrow, not the thing it points to. This is the single most common confusion in this topic.

How to build an immutable class: the checklist

  1. Make the class final (or give it only private constructors), so no subclass can add mutable state or override accessors.
  2. Make every field private final.
  3. Provide no setters and no methods that change fields.
  4. Defensive copy in: copy every mutable argument (lists, arrays, Date) in the constructor.
  5. Defensive copy out: never return a mutable internal object; return a copy or an unmodifiable view.
  6. "Modifying" methods return a new object (withItem, plusDays).
  7. Do not let this escape from the constructor.
// BROKEN: final fields, but it holds the caller's mutable objects
final class LeakyOrder {
    private final List<String> items;
    private final Date placedAt;
    LeakyOrder(List<String> items, Date placedAt) { this.items = items; this.placedAt = placedAt; }
    List<String> items() { return items; }
    Date placedAt() { return placedAt; }
}

// CORRECT
public final class Order {                                 // 1. final class
    private final String id;                               // 2. private final fields
    private final List<String> items;
    private final Date placedAt;

    public Order(String id, List<String> items, Date placedAt) {
        this.id = Objects.requireNonNull(id);
        this.items = List.copyOf(items);                   // 4. copy in (and unmodifiable)
        this.placedAt = new Date(placedAt.getTime());      //    copy the mutable Date
    }
    public String id() { return id; }                      // 3. no setters
    public List<String> items() { return items; }          //    already unmodifiable: safe
    public Date placedAt() { return new Date(placedAt.getTime()); }  // 5. copy out
    public Order withItem(String item) {                   // 6. change = new object
        List<String> next = new ArrayList<>(items);
        next.add(item);
        return new Order(id, next, placedAt);
    }
}

The same attack on both — mutate the caller's list and date after construction, mutate what the getters return:

list.add("HACK"); when.setTime(999_999_999_999L); leaky.items().add("HACK2");
leaky.items() + " " + leaky.placedAt().getTime();   // [pen, HACK, HACK2] 999999999999

list.add("HACK"); when.setTime(999_999_999_999L); order.placedAt().setTime(123);
order.items().add("HACK2");                         // UnsupportedOperationException
order.items() + " " + order.placedAt().getTime();   // [pen] 0
order.withItem("ink").items();                      // [pen, ink]  -- order itself is still [pen]

Better still, avoid mutable parts altogether: use Instant or LocalDate (immutable, Java 8) instead of Date, and a record with a compact constructor for the copying.

Why immutability is worth it

  • Thread-safe for free — nothing changes, so no locks are needed.
  • Safe map keys and set elements — the hash code can never change under the collection (see the mutable-key bug in section 13).
  • Easy to reason about and cache — share one instance everywhere (String pool, Integer cache, BigDecimal.ZERO).
  • Cost: more objects created on each "change". Modern GCs handle short-lived objects cheaply; for heavy building, use a mutable builder (StringBuilder) and freeze at the end.

Why is String immutable? So it can be shared from the string pool, used safely as a HashMap key (its hash is cached), passed across threads without locking, and trusted in security checks (a file path or class name cannot change after it is validated).

Interview angle: "How do you make a class immutable?" — recite the checklist and mention defensive copies both ways; that detail separates good answers from average ones. "final vs finally vs finalize?" — modifier; block that always runs after try; deprecated GC hook. "Can a static method be overridden?" — no, it is hidden. "What does effectively final mean?" — never reassigned after initialisation, so lambdas and inner classes may capture it.
Part 3 — Core APIs

17. Exceptions

In plain words

An exception is an object that says "something went wrong here". When code throws it, Java stops the normal flow and walks back up the call stack until some catch block handles it. If nobody catches it, the thread dies and prints a stack trace (the list of method calls that led to the problem).

Java splits problems into two kinds: checked exceptions (things a careful program should expect, like a missing file — the compiler forces you to deal with them) and unchecked exceptions (programming bugs like null access, or JVM trouble like running out of memory — the compiler does not force anything).

A fire alarm in a building. When smoke is detected (throw), normal work stops and the alarm travels up floor by floor until someone responsible (catch) deals with it. The cleanup crew that always tidies the room, fire or no fire, is finally.

The hierarchy

CheckedUnchecked (RuntimeException)Error
MeaningExpected, recoverable problem outside your controlA bug in the calling codeSerious JVM/system failure
ExamplesIOException, SQLExceptionNullPointerException, IllegalArgumentExceptionOutOfMemoryError, StackOverflowError
Compiler ruleMust catch or declare with throwsNo ruleNo rule
Should you catch it?Yes, where you can do something usefulUsually no — fix the bug; catch at a top-level boundaryAlmost never

try / catch / finally — the exact rules

  • catch blocks are tried top to bottom; the first matching type wins. A subclass catch must come before its superclass catch, or the compiler rejects it as unreachable.
  • finally runs whether the try block finished normally, returned, or threw. It is for cleanup.
  • If the try block executes return x, the value of x is saved first, then finally runs. Changing a primitive in finally does not change the returned value — but mutating a returned object does.
  • A return inside finally overrides the try's return and even swallows an exception in flight. Never do it.
@SuppressWarnings("finally")
static int returnInFinally() {
    try { return 1; } finally { return 2; }
}                                          // returns 2

@SuppressWarnings("finally")
static String swallow() {
    try { throw new IllegalStateException("lost!"); }
    finally { return "finally won"; }      // the exception silently disappears
}                                          // returns "finally won"

static int captured() {
    int x = 10;
    try { return x; } finally { x = 20; }  // 10 was already saved
}                                          // returns 10

static List<String> capturedRef() {
    List<String> list = new ArrayList<>();
    try { list.add("try"); return list; }
    finally { list.add("finally"); }       // same object, so the change shows
}                                          // returns [try, finally]

When does finally NOT run?

public static void main(String[] args) {
    try {
        System.out.println("in try");
        System.exit(0);                    // JVM starts shutting down right here
    } finally {
        System.out.println("finally");     // never printed
    }
}
// Output: in try

finally is skipped when the JVM itself stops: System.exit(), Runtime.halt(), a crash, the process being killed (kill -9), or a power cut. It also never runs if the try block never ends (infinite loop, deadlock), and a daemon thread's finally is skipped when the JVM exits. Shutdown hooks (Runtime.addShutdownHook) run on System.exit, but finally blocks do not.

try-with-resources (Java 7) and suppressed exceptions

Any object that implements AutoCloseable can be declared in the try header. Java closes it automatically, in reverse order of declaration, even if the body throws. If close() also throws, that exception is not lost and does not hide the real one: it is attached to the main exception as a suppressed exception.

static class Res implements AutoCloseable {
    private final String name;
    Res(String name) { this.name = name; System.out.println("open " + name); }
    @Override public void close() throws IOException {
        System.out.println("close " + name);
        throw new IOException("close failed: " + name);
    }
}

try (Res a = new Res("A"); Res b = new Res("B")) {
    System.out.println("body");
    throw new IllegalStateException("body failed");
} catch (Exception e) {
    System.out.println("caught: " + e.getMessage());
    for (Throwable s : e.getSuppressed())
        System.out.println("  suppressed: " + s.getMessage());
}
// open A
// open B
// body
// close B                         <- reverse order
// close A
// caught: body failed             <- the REAL error wins
//   suppressed: close failed: B
//   suppressed: close failed: A

With the old try/finally style, an exception from close() in finally would replace the body's exception, and you would debug the wrong problem. Since Java 9 you can also use an existing effectively-final variable: try (reader) { ... }.

Multi-catch (Java 7)

for (String in : new String[]{"abc", null}) {
    try {
        System.out.println(Integer.parseInt(in.trim()));
    } catch (NumberFormatException | NullPointerException e) {
        System.out.println("multi-catch: " + e.getClass().getSimpleName());
    }
}
// multi-catch: NumberFormatException
// multi-catch: NullPointerException

The variable e is implicitly final. The alternatives cannot be parent and child (IOException | FileNotFoundException does not compile — the parent already covers the child).

Custom exceptions and chaining

// Checked: the caller is expected to recover (show a message, retry with less)
class InsufficientFundsException extends Exception {
    private final long shortBy;
    InsufficientFundsException(String msg, long shortBy) { super(msg); this.shortBy = shortBy; }
    long shortBy() { return shortBy; }       // extra data the handler can use
}

// Unchecked wrapper that keeps the original as its CAUSE
class DataAccessException extends RuntimeException {
    DataAccessException(String msg, Throwable cause) { super(msg, cause); }
}

try {
    throw new SQLException("connection refused");
} catch (SQLException e) {
    throw new DataAccessException("could not load user 42", e);   // chain, don't drop
}
// e.getMessage()            -> could not load user 42
// e.getCause().getMessage() -> connection refused

Exception chaining means wrapping a low-level exception in a higher-level one while keeping the original as the cause. The stack trace then shows both, with a Caused by: line. Translating exceptions at layer boundaries (SQL error becomes a "data access" error) keeps the upper layers independent of JDBC details — Spring does exactly this.

final vs finally vs finalize

What it isUse
finalKeywordVariable cannot be reassigned, method cannot be overridden, class cannot be extended
finallyBlock after try/catchCleanup code that runs whether or not an exception happened
finalize()Method on ObjectCalled by the GC "sometime" before collecting an object. Deprecated since Java 9 and deprecated for removal since Java 18 (JEP 421): no timing guarantee, may never run, slows GC, can resurrect objects. Use try-with-resources or java.lang.ref.Cleaner.

Best practices

  • Catch specific types, not Exception or Throwable, except at a top-level boundary (a request handler, a thread's run loop) where you log and return an error.
  • Never swallow: an empty catch {} hides bugs. At least log it; usually rethrow or wrap.
  • Keep the cause when wrapping: new XException("context", e). Losing the original stack trace is the most common logging mistake.
  • Log or rethrow, not both — doing both prints the same error many times up the stack.
  • Don't use exceptions for normal control flow. Creating one fills in a stack trace, which is slow compared to an if.
  • Fail fast with clear messages: Objects.requireNonNull(user, "user must not be null"), IllegalArgumentException for bad arguments, IllegalStateException for "wrong time to call this".
  • If you catch InterruptedException and cannot rethrow it, restore the flag: Thread.currentThread().interrupt().
  • Prefer unchecked exceptions for application errors in modern code (Spring, most libraries); use checked only when the caller can realistically recover.
Interview angle: "Checked vs unchecked?" — say what the compiler enforces, give two examples of each, and your rule for choosing. Follow-ups: "Does finally run if you return from try?" (yes, after the value is saved), "What if finally also returns?" (it wins and swallows exceptions), "When does finally not run?" (System.exit, JVM crash/kill), "What are suppressed exceptions?" (close-time failures attached to the main exception by try-with-resources). Bonus: since Java 14 (default on in 15), helpful NullPointerException messages tell you exactly which reference was null, e.g. user.name().trim() fails with Cannot invoke "String.trim()" because the return value of "NpeMsg$User.name()" is null.

18. Generics

In plain words

Generics (Java 5) let you write a class or method once with a type parameter — a placeholder like T — and fill in the real type when you use it: List<String>, Map<Long, User>. The compiler then checks that you only put Strings in and it knows you get Strings out, so no casts and no surprise ClassCastException at runtime.

The catch: generics exist only at compile time. The compiler checks the types and then erases them, so at runtime a List<String> is just a List.

A labelled storage box. The label "Books only" lets the warehouse staff (the compiler) refuse a shoe at the door, and whoever opens it knows they will find books. But once the box is on the shelf (runtime), the label has been peeled off — the box itself does not know what it holds.

Why generics: before and after

// Before Java 5: everything is Object, casts everywhere, errors at runtime
List names = new ArrayList();
names.add("amy");
names.add(42);                     // compiler happy
String s = (String) names.get(1);  // ClassCastException at runtime

// With generics: the mistake is a compile error
List<String> typed = new ArrayList<>();   // diamond <> since Java 7
typed.add("amy");
// typed.add(42);                  // does not compile
String t = typed.get(0);           // no cast

Generic classes, methods and bounded types

class Box<T> {                          // T = type parameter
    private T value;
    Box(T value) { this.value = value; }
    T get() { return value; }
}
Box<String> b = new Box<>("hi");
String s = b.get();                      // "hi", no cast

// Generic METHOD: the <T> before the return type declares it
static <T> T firstOrNull(List<T> list) { return list.isEmpty() ? null : list.get(0); }
firstOrNull(List.of("x", "y"));          // T inferred as String -> "x"
GenDemo.<String>firstOrNull(List.of("x"));   // explicit "type witness", rarely needed

// BOUNDED type: T must be Comparable, so compareTo is allowed
static <T extends Comparable<T>> T max(List<T> list) {
    T best = list.get(0);
    for (T t : list) if (t.compareTo(best) > 0) best = t;
    return best;
}
max(List.of(3, 9, 4));                   // 9
max(List.of("pear", "apple", "zucchini")); // "zucchini"

Naming convention: T type, E element, K/V key/value, R result. Multiple bounds use &: <T extends Number & Comparable<T>> (a class first, then interfaces). The JDK's own signature is the more flexible <T extends Comparable<? super T>>, so it also works when a parent class implements Comparable.

Invariance: why List<Integer> is not a List<Number>

Even though Integer is a Number, a List<Integer> is not a List<Number> (generics are invariant). If it were, you could add a Double into a list of Integers through the List<Number> reference. Arrays made the opposite choice (they are covariant) and pay for it at runtime:

Integer[] ia = {1, 2};
Object[] oa = ia;          // allowed: arrays are covariant
oa[0] = "boom";            // compiles, but throws ArrayStoreException: java.lang.String

List<Integer> ints = List.of(1, 2, 3);
// List<Number> nums = ints;   // compile error: generics catch it earlier

Wildcards and PECS

A wildcard ? means "some type I don't know". Bounded wildcards restore flexibility safely:

WildcardAcceptsRead givesCan addRole
List<? extends Number>List<Integer>, List<Double>, List<Number>Numbernothing (except null)Producer — you read from it
List<? super Integer>List<Integer>, List<Number>, List<Object>Object onlyIntegerConsumer — you write into it
List<?>any listObjectnothing (except null)Read-only, type doesn't matter

PECS: Producer Extends, Consumer Super. If a parameter gives you values, use extends; if it takes values from you, use super. If it does both, use a plain T.

static double sum(List<? extends Number> nums) {   // PRODUCER: we only read
    double s = 0;
    for (Number n : nums) s += n.doubleValue();
    return s;
}
sum(List.of(1, 2, 3));          // 6.0
sum(List.of(1.5, 2.5));         // 4.0

static void addInts(List<? super Integer> sink) {  // CONSUMER: we only write
    sink.add(1); sink.add(2);
}
addInts(new ArrayList<Number>());   // [1, 2]
addInts(new ArrayList<Object>());   // [1, 2]

// Both at once -- the shape of Collections.copy
static <T> void copy(List<? super T> dst, List<? extends T> src) {
    for (T t : src) dst.add(t);
}
List<Object> dst = new ArrayList<>(List.of("start"));
copy(dst, List.of(1, 2, 3));    // [start, 1, 2, 3]
Object | Number List<? super Integer> : Integer, Number, Object (go UP) / | \ Integer Double Long List<? extends Number> : Number, Integer, Double, Long (go DOWN) Why can't you add to "? extends Number"? The real list might be List<Double>; adding an Integer would corrupt it. Why do reads from "? super Integer" give Object? The real list might be List<Object> holding anything.

JDK examples: Collections.max(Collection<? extends T>, Comparator<? super T>), list.sort(Comparator<? super E>) — a Comparator<Object> can sort a List<String>.

Type erasure and its consequences

After checking, the compiler replaces each type parameter with its bound (Object if unbounded, or the first bound), and inserts casts where you read values. One compiled class serves all type arguments.

List<String> ls = new ArrayList<>();
List<Integer> li = new ArrayList<>();
ls.getClass() == li.getClass();     // true -- both are java.util.ArrayList at runtime
You can't...WhyWorkaround
new T()At runtime T is unknownPass a factory: Supplier<T> or Class<T>
new T[10] or new List<String>[10]Arrays must know their element type at runtimeUse List<T>; or (T[]) new Object[n] internally (as ArrayList does)
x instanceof List<String>The <String> part no longer existsx instanceof List<?>, then check elements
List<int>Erasure to Object needs reference typesList<Integer> (boxing) or int[] / IntStream
Overload f(List<String>) and f(List<Integer>)Both erase to f(List) — "name clash"Use different method names
static T fieldStatic belongs to the one shared class, not to each Box<X>Make the static member generic on its own
catch (T e) / generic exception classesThe catch needs a real runtime typeCatch a concrete type
// Workaround for new T(): pass a factory
static <T> List<T> fill(int n, Supplier<T> factory) {
    List<T> out = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) out.add(factory.get());
    return out;
}
fill(3, StringBuilder::new);        // 3 new StringBuilders

Bridge methods

When a class implements a generic interface with a concrete type, erasure would break overriding: Comparable<T>.compareTo(T) erases to compareTo(Object), but your class declares compareTo(Name). The compiler adds a hidden bridge method compareTo(Object) that casts and calls yours. You can see it with reflection:

class Name implements Comparable<Name> {
    final String s; Name(String s) { this.s = s; }
    public int compareTo(Name o) { return s.compareTo(o.s); }
}
for (Method m : Name.class.getDeclaredMethods())
    if (m.getName().equals("compareTo"))
        System.out.println(m.getParameterTypes()[0].getSimpleName() + " bridge=" + m.isBridge());
// Name bridge=false
// Object bridge=true     <- generated by the compiler

Raw types and heap pollution

A raw type is a generic type used without its type argument (List instead of List<String>). It exists only for compatibility with pre-Java-5 code. It turns the checks off, and the error then shows up far from the real bug:

List<String> names = new ArrayList<>();
List raw = names;           // raw type: warning only
raw.add(42);                // "unchecked" warning, but compiles -- heap pollution
String first = names.get(0);
// ClassCastException: class java.lang.Integer cannot be cast to class java.lang.String
// (thrown HERE, at the hidden cast, not where 42 was added)

Use List<?> when you truly don't care about the type. Treat "unchecked" warnings as errors. @SafeVarargs is how a generic varargs method promises it doesn't pollute the heap.

Interview angle: "What is type erasure?" — generic types are checked at compile time and removed from bytecode; the compiler inserts casts. Then list two consequences (no new T(), no instanceof List<String>) and mention bridge methods. "Explain PECS" — give Collections.copy(List<? super T> dst, List<? extends T> src). "Why is List<Integer> not a List<Number>?" — invariance protects you from adding a Double, unlike covariant arrays and their ArrayStoreException.

19. The Collections Framework

In plain words

The Collections Framework (in java.util) is Java's set of ready-made data structures: lists, sets, queues and maps. Interfaces (List, Set, Map...) say what a structure can do; implementations (ArrayList, HashSet, TreeMap...) say how it does it, and therefore how fast each operation is.

Write your code against the interface (List<String> x = new ArrayList<>()) so you can swap the implementation later without touching the rest.

Kitchen containers. "Something to hold liquid" is the interface; a jug, a bottle and a thermos are implementations. All hold liquid, but one pours fast, one seals tight and one keeps it hot — you pick based on what you do most.

The hierarchy

  • List — ordered by index, duplicates allowed.
  • Set — no duplicates (decided by equals/hashCode, or by the comparator for TreeSet).
  • Queue — hold items for processing (usually FIFO); Deque ("deck") adds both ends, so it works as a stack too.
  • Map — key to value pairs, unique keys. Not a Collection because its elements are pairs.
  • Also: SortedSet/NavigableSet and SortedMap/NavigableMap add sorted views and floor/ceiling; Java 21 added SequencedCollection/SequencedMap (JEP 431) with getFirst(), getLast(), reversed() for anything with a defined order.

Each implementation, inside

ClassBuilt onOrderNullsNotes
ArrayListResizable array (default capacity 10, allocated on first add; grows by 50%)IndexYesThe default list
LinkedListDoubly linked nodesIndexYesExtra node object per element; poor cache locality
ArrayDequeCircular resizable arrayEndsNoBest stack and queue
PriorityQueueBinary heap in an arraySmallest first on pollNoIteration order is NOT sorted
HashSetA HashMap (elements are the keys)NoneOneNeeds good hashCode/equals
LinkedHashSetLinkedHashMapInsertionOneDedupe while keeping order
TreeSetTreeMap (red-black tree)SortedNo*Needs Comparable or a Comparator
HashMapArray of buckets (see s20)NoneOne key, many valuesThe default map
LinkedHashMapHashMap + doubly linked list through entriesInsertion or accessYesLRU cache building block
TreeMapRed-black treeSorted by keyNo* keysfloorKey, ceilingKey, headMap, tailMap

*Unless your comparator explicitly handles null.

Complexity table

Classget / accessaddremovecontainsNext / iterate
ArrayListO(1)O(1) amortised at end, O(n) middleO(n) (O(1) at end)O(n)O(1) each
LinkedListO(n)O(1) at endsO(1) at ends / via iterator; O(n) to findO(n)O(1) each
ArrayDequeO(1) ends onlyO(1) amortised at endsO(1) at endsO(n)O(1) each
PriorityQueuepeek O(1)O(log n)poll O(log n), remove(x) O(n)O(n)unsorted
HashSet / LinkedHashSet—O(1) avgO(1) avgO(1) avgO(capacity) / O(n)
TreeSetfirst/last O(log n)O(log n)O(log n)O(log n)sorted
HashMap / LinkedHashMapO(1) avgO(1) avgO(1) avgkey O(1) avg, value O(n)O(capacity) / O(n)
TreeMapO(log n)O(log n)O(log n)key O(log n), value O(n)sorted

Iterating a HashMap visits every bucket, so a map that once held a million entries and is now nearly empty still iterates slowly. LinkedHashMap follows its linked list and costs only O(n).

Order in practice

List<String> in = List.of("banana", "apple", "cherry", "date");
new LinkedHashSet<>(in);     // [banana, apple, cherry, date]  insertion order
new TreeSet<>(in);           // [apple, banana, cherry, date]  sorted
PriorityQueue<Integer> pq = new PriorityQueue<>(List.of(5, 1, 8, 3, 2));
System.out.println(pq);      // [1, 2, 8, 3, 5]  heap layout, not sorted!
pq.poll();                   // 1

// LRU cache in 5 lines: access order + evict eldest
Map<String, Integer> lru = new LinkedHashMap<>(16, 0.75f, true) {
    protected boolean removeEldestEntry(Map.Entry<String, Integer> e) { return size() > 3; }
};
lru.put("a", 1); lru.put("b", 2); lru.put("c", 3);
lru.get("a");                // "a" becomes most recent
lru.put("d", 4);             // evicts "b", the least recently used
System.out.println(lru);     // {c=3, a=1, d=4}

// Java 21 sequenced collections
List<Integer> seq = new ArrayList<>(List.of(1, 2, 3));
seq.getFirst(); seq.getLast(); seq.reversed();   // 1, 3, [3, 2, 1]

How to choose

Need key -> value? yes -> sorted keys / floor-ceiling? TreeMap keep insertion order / LRU? LinkedHashMap many threads? ConcurrentHashMap otherwise HashMap no -> unique elements only? sorted? TreeSet keep insertion order? LinkedHashSet otherwise HashSet always take the smallest/largest next? PriorityQueue stack or FIFO queue? ArrayDeque otherwise (a plain list) ArrayList

LinkedList almost never wins: even "insert in the middle" needs an O(n) walk to find the spot, and ArrayList's System.arraycopy shift is very fast in practice. Legacy classes Vector, Stack and Hashtable synchronize every method; use ArrayList, ArrayDeque and HashMap/ConcurrentHashMap instead.

Immutable and unmodifiable collections

String[] arr = {"x", "y", "z"};
List<String> asList = Arrays.asList(arr);
asList.set(0, "X");          // ok -- and it writes through: arr is now [X, y, z]
asList.add("w");             // UnsupportedOperationException (fixed size)
Arrays.asList("a", null);    // [a, null] -- nulls fine
Arrays.asList(new int[]{1, 2, 3}).size();   // 1 !  a List<int[]> with one element

List<String> backing = new ArrayList<>(List.of("x", "y", "z"));
List<String> unmod = Collections.unmodifiableList(backing);
unmod.set(0, "X");           // UnsupportedOperationException
backing.add("w");
System.out.println(unmod);   // [x, y, z, w]  -- a read-only VIEW, sees changes

List<String> copy = List.copyOf(backing);  // real immutable snapshot (Java 10)
List<String> of = List.of("x", "y", "z");  // Java 9
of.set(0, "X");              // UnsupportedOperationException
List.of("a", null);          // NullPointerException
of.contains(null);           // NullPointerException (even asking!)
Set.of("a", "a");            // IllegalArgumentException: duplicate element
Stream.of(1, 2).toList().add(3);   // UnsupportedOperationException (Java 16)
Arrays.asListCollections.unmodifiableListList.of / List.copyOf
add / removeThrowsThrowsThrows
setAllowed (writes to the array)ThrowsThrows
NullsAllowedAllowedRejected (NPE)
Changes underneath show?Yes (array-backed)Yes (it is a view)No — truly immutable
Since1.21.29 / 10
Interview angle: "ArrayList vs LinkedList?" — ArrayList for almost everything: O(1) random access, compact memory, cache friendly; LinkedList only if you truly add/remove at both ends through an iterator (and then ArrayDeque is usually better). "HashMap vs TreeMap vs LinkedHashMap?" — O(1) unordered vs O(log n) sorted vs O(1) insertion/access order. "List.of vs unmodifiableList?" — immutable copy with no nulls vs read-only view that still reflects the original. Note that immutability is shallow: a List.of(mutableObject) still lets you change the object.

20. How HashMap Works Inside

In plain words

A HashMap is an array of buckets. To store a key, Java turns it into a number (its hash), uses that number to pick a bucket index, and puts the entry in that bucket. To find the key later it repeats the same math and looks in just that one bucket — which is why get and put are O(1) on average.

When two keys land in the same bucket (a collision), the bucket holds a small linked list of entries, and if that list grows long (and the table is big enough) it becomes a balanced tree.

A coat check with numbered hooks. The attendant computes a hook number from your ticket, so she walks straight to one hook instead of searching the whole room. If two coats end up on the same hook, she checks each ticket on that hook — still quick as long as hooks rarely get crowded.

Step 1: hashing and spreading

// Inside HashMap (Java 8+)
static final int hash(Object key) {
    int h;
    return (key == null) ? 0 : (h = key.hashCode()) ^ (h >>> 16);
}
// Bucket index: (n - 1) & hash       where n = table length, always a power of 2
  • Why (n - 1) & hash? When n is a power of two, n - 1 is all ones in binary (16 - 1 = 1111), so the AND keeps just the low bits: the same result as hash % n for non-negative numbers, but faster, and never negative even when hashCode() is negative.
  • Why spread with h ^ (h >>> 16)? A small table only looks at the lowest few bits. Keys whose hash codes differ only in the high bits would all collide. XOR-ing the top 16 bits into the bottom 16 lets high bits influence the index.
int h = "apple".hashCode();        // 93029210
int spread = h ^ (h >>> 16);       // 93030097
int index = spread & (16 - 1);     // 1

int a = 0x10000, b = 0x20000;      // differ only in high bits
(a & 15) + "," + (b & 15)                          // "0,0"  -- both in bucket 0
(spread(a) & 15) + "," + (spread(b) & 15)          // "1,2"  -- spread apart

Step 2: put and get

put(key, value) 1. table empty? -> allocate it now (lazy: new HashMap() has no array until the first put) 2. i = (n - 1) & hash(key) 3. bucket i empty? -> place a new Node there 4. else walk the bucket: same hash AND (same reference OR key.equals(k))? -> replace value, return old value otherwise append a new Node at the TAIL 5. bucket now has 9+ nodes? -> treeify (or resize if the table is under 64) 6. ++size > threshold? -> resize (double the table) get(key): compute i the same way, walk that bucket comparing hash first (cheap), then equals.

That is why hashCode and equals must agree: hashCode picks the bucket, equals picks the entry within it. put returns the previous value (null if new): pm.put("x", 1) gives null, then pm.put("x", 2) gives 1.

Step 3: load factor and resize

The load factor (default 0.75) is how full the table may get. The threshold is capacity × loadFactor. When size goes above it, the table doubles and every entry is moved. 0.75 is a trade-off: lower wastes memory, higher means more collisions.

HashMap<Integer, String> m = new HashMap<>();   // table is still null here
// after 1st put:  capacity=16 threshold=12
// after 12th put: capacity=16 threshold=12
// after 13th put: capacity=32 threshold=24   <- resized

new HashMap<>(100);   // capacity rounds UP to a power of two: 128 (threshold 96)

On a resize from n to 2n, each entry either stays at index i or moves to i + n, decided by the single bit hash & n. Java 8 splits each bucket into a "lo" and "hi" list and keeps the relative order. If you know the size in advance, pre-size: new HashMap<>(expected * 4 / 3 + 1), or since Java 19 HashMap.newHashMap(expected), to avoid repeated resizing.

Step 4: collisions and treeify

A bucket starts as a linked list. When a put makes a bucket reach 9 nodes (it already had 8 = TREEIFY_THRESHOLD), HashMap wants to turn it into a red-black tree so lookups in that bucket become O(log n) instead of O(n). But if the whole table is smaller than 64 (MIN_TREEIFY_CAPACITY), it resizes instead — a small table probably just needs more buckets. A tree shrinks back to a list when a resize split leaves 6 or fewer nodes (UNTREEIFY_THRESHOLD).

// Every key has the same hashCode -> all land in ONE bucket
record BadKey(int id) implements Comparable<BadKey> {
    @Override public int hashCode() { return 42; }
    @Override public int compareTo(BadKey o) { return Integer.compare(id, o.id); }
}

// Table already at 64 (new HashMap<>(64)); bucket type read via reflection:
// nodes=8  bucket=Node       (linked list)
// nodes=9  bucket=TreeNode   <- 9th node triggers treeify

// Default table of 16:
// nodes=8  cap=16 bucket=Node
// nodes=9  cap=32 bucket=Node       <- table too small: resize instead
// nodes=10 cap=64 bucket=Node       <- resize again
// nodes=11 cap=64 bucket=TreeNode   <- now treeify

(The bucket type was read with reflection and --add-opens java.base/java.util=ALL-UNNAMED; do not do this in real code.) Trees order nodes by hash, then by compareTo if the keys are Comparable, so making keys Comparable helps in the worst case. Treeify exists mainly as a defence against hash-flooding attacks, where someone sends many keys with the same hash to make your server do O(n) work per lookup.

The null key

HashMap<String, Integer> nm = new HashMap<>();
nm.put(null, 1); nm.put(null, 2);
nm.get(null);                                    // 2, size 1, stored in bucket 0 (hash(null) = 0)
new Hashtable<String, Integer>().put(null, 1);          // NullPointerException
new ConcurrentHashMap<String, Integer>().put(null, 1);  // NullPointerException

ConcurrentHashMap forbids null because get(k) == null would be ambiguous between threads: "absent" or "mapped to null"? You cannot safely follow up with containsKey when another thread may change the map in between.

Why keys should be immutable

final class MutableKey {
    int id;
    MutableKey(int id) { this.id = id; }
    @Override public boolean equals(Object o) { return o instanceof MutableKey m && m.id == id; }
    @Override public int hashCode() { return Integer.hashCode(id); }
}
Map<MutableKey, String> mm = new HashMap<>();
MutableKey key = new MutableKey(1);
mm.put(key, "data");
key.id = 2;                           // hash changes, but the entry stays in the OLD bucket
mm.get(key);                          // null  -- looks in the bucket for hash(2)
mm.get(new MutableKey(1));            // null  -- right bucket, but equals fails (id is now 2)
mm.size();                            // 1     -- the entry is stuck: a memory leak
mm.containsValue("data");             // true

Use immutable keys: String, Integer, records with immutable fields, enums. String also caches its hash code, which makes it a fast key.

Java 7 vs Java 8

Java 7Java 8+
Bucket structureLinked list only (worst case O(n))List, turns into red-black tree (worst case O(log n))
Insert position in bucketHead of listTail of list
ResizeRehash each entry; list order reversedSplit into lo/hi lists by hash & oldCap; order kept
Concurrent misuseHead insertion during a concurrent resize could form a cycle, so get looped forever (100% CPU)No cycle, but still lost updates and corrupted state
Hash spreadingSeveral shifts and XORsOne XOR: h ^ (h >>> 16)
Entry classEntryNode and TreeNode

Not thread-safe

Two threads calling put at the same time can overwrite each other's entries, lose updates to size, or corrupt a bucket during resize. Options: ConcurrentHashMap (the right answer: CAS on empty buckets plus a lock on one bucket's head node, lock-free reads) or Collections.synchronizedMap (one lock for the whole map, slower). Hashtable is legacy.

Interview angle: "How does HashMap work?" is one of the most asked Java questions. Walk through: hashCode → spread → (n - 1) & hash → bucket → compare hash then equals → tail insert → treeify at the 9th node if table ≥ 64 → resize past 0.75 × capacity by doubling. Then the follow-ups: why power-of-two sizes, why equals/hashCode must be consistent, what a mutable key does, the null key in bucket 0, the Java 7 infinite loop, and why ConcurrentHashMap for threads. Average O(1), worst case O(log n) per bucket since Java 8.

21. Comparable, Comparator & Sorting

In plain words

To sort objects, Java needs to know how to compare two of them. Comparable is the object's own built-in, "natural" order: the class implements compareTo (Strings sort alphabetically, numbers by value). A Comparator is a separate rule you pass in from the outside, so you can sort the same objects many different ways.

Both return an int: negative if the first is smaller, zero if equal, positive if the first is bigger.

Books on a shelf. Comparable is the order printed on the spine — the library's call number, one fixed order. A Comparator is you deciding today to arrange them by colour, or by height, without changing the books.

Comparable<T>Comparator<T>
Packagejava.langjava.util
Methodint compareTo(T other)int compare(T a, T b)
Where it livesInside the class being sortedOutside: a lambda, a constant, a separate class
How many ordersOne (natural order)As many as you like
Used byCollections.sort(list), TreeSet, TreeMap, PriorityQueue by defaultlist.sort(cmp), new TreeMap<>(cmp), stream.sorted(cmp)
Use whenThere is one obvious order and you own the classSeveral orders, or you can't change the class

Both in code

record Employee(String name, String dept, int salary) implements Comparable<Employee> {
    @Override public int compareTo(Employee o) { return name.compareTo(o.name); }  // natural: by name
}
List<Employee> staff = new ArrayList<>(List.of(
    new Employee("Maya", "Eng", 120), new Employee("Arun", "Sales", 90),
    new Employee("Zoe", "Eng", 150),  new Employee("Liam", "Sales", 90),
    new Employee("Chen", "Eng", 120)));

Collections.sort(staff);   // natural order -> [Arun, Chen, Liam, Maya, Zoe]

// Comparator chain: dept A-Z, then salary high-to-low, then name A-Z
staff.sort(Comparator.comparing(Employee::dept)
        .thenComparing(Employee::salary, Comparator.reverseOrder())
        .thenComparing(Employee::name));
// Eng 150 Zoe
// Eng 120 Chen
// Eng 120 Maya
// Sales 90 Arun
// Sales 90 Liam

The Comparator toolkit (Java 8+)

MethodWhat it does
Comparator.comparing(keyFn)Compare by an extracted key (which must be Comparable)
comparing(keyFn, keyCmp)Compare by a key using a given comparator for the key
comparingInt / Long / DoubleSame, without boxing — prefer for numbers
.thenComparing(...)Tie-breaker, used only when earlier comparisons return 0
.reversed()Reverse the whole chain so far
Comparator.naturalOrder() / reverseOrder()Natural order and its reverse
nullsFirst(cmp) / nullsLast(cmp)Handle null elements instead of throwing NPE
List<String> withNull = new ArrayList<>(Arrays.asList("pear", null, "apple"));
withNull.sort(Comparator.nullsFirst(Comparator.naturalOrder()));
// [null, apple, pear]
withNull.sort(Comparator.nullsLast(Comparator.<String>naturalOrder().reversed()));
// [pear, apple, null]

TreeMap<String, Integer> ci = new TreeMap<>(String.CASE_INSENSITIVE_ORDER);
ci.put("Apple", 1); ci.put("apple", 2);
// {Apple=2}  -- the comparator says they're equal, so it's the SAME key

Trap: comparing(A).thenComparing(B).reversed() reverses both A and B. To reverse only one key, pass Comparator.reverseOrder() as its second argument, as in the salary example above.

Sorting stability

A sort is stable if elements that compare equal keep their original relative order. List.sort, Collections.sort, Arrays.sort(Object[]) and Stream.sorted use TimSort, which is stable and O(n log n) (close to O(n) on nearly sorted data). Arrays.sort(int[]) uses dual-pivot quicksort, not stable — which does not matter because equal primitives are indistinguishable.

// Sort by name first, then by salary: equal salaries stay in name order
staff.sort(Comparator.naturalOrder());
staff.sort(Comparator.comparingInt(Employee::salary));
// [90:Arun, 90:Liam, 120:Chen, 120:Maya, 150:Zoe]
Before (sorted by name): Arun90 Chen120 Liam90 Maya120 Zoe150 Stable sort by salary: Arun90 Liam90 Chen120 Maya120 Zoe150 \____/ ties keep A-before-L and C-before-M

The a - b overflow bug

Comparator<Integer> bad  = (x, y) -> x - y;          // looks clever, is broken
Comparator<Integer> good = Integer::compare;

int big = 2_000_000_000, neg = -2_000_000_000;
bad.compare(neg, big);     // 294967296   positive?! -4e9 overflowed and wrapped
good.compare(neg, big);    // -1

List<Integer> nums = new ArrayList<>(List.of(big, neg, 0));
nums.sort(bad);            // [2000000000, -2000000000, 0]   wrong
nums.sort(good);           // [-2000000000, 0, 2000000000]   right

Use Integer.compare, Long.compare, Double.compare or Comparator.comparingInt. Subtraction is only safe when you know both values are small and non-negative — and it's not worth the risk.

The comparator contract

  • Antisymmetric: sign of compare(a, b) = minus sign of compare(b, a).
  • Transitive: if a > b and b > c, then a > c.
  • Consistent: if compare(a, b) == 0, then a and b compare the same against everything else.
  • Recommended: consistent with equals (compare == 0 exactly when equals is true). Sorted collections use only the comparator, so if they disagree, sets behave oddly:
new HashSet<>(List.of(new BigDecimal("1.0"), new BigDecimal("1.00"))).size();   // 2 (equals: scale differs)
new TreeSet<>(List.of(new BigDecimal("1.0"), new BigDecimal("1.00"))).size();   // 1 (compareTo: same value)

Breaking the contract (for example a comparator that returns random results or mixes up signs) can make TimSort throw IllegalArgumentException: Comparison method violates its general contract! — but only for some inputs, so it shows up as a rare production bug.

Interview angle: "Comparable vs Comparator?" — one natural order inside the class vs any number of external orders. Then show a chain with comparing/thenComparing/reversed. Expected follow-ups: "Is Java's sort stable?" (yes for objects, TimSort), "What's wrong with a - b?" (overflow), and "What if compareTo is inconsistent with equals?" (TreeSet/TreeMap treat compare-equal elements as duplicates).

22. Iterators, Fail-fast & Fail-safe

In plain words

An Iterator is a cursor that walks through a collection one element at a time: hasNext() asks "is there more?", next() gives the next one, and remove() deletes the one you just got. The for-each loop uses an iterator behind the scenes.

If you change a normal collection (add or remove) while iterating it without going through the iterator, Java's iterators notice and throw ConcurrentModificationException. That is fail-fast. Concurrent collections instead let the loop continue safely — fail-safe (or "weakly consistent").

Reading a numbered list aloud while someone else crosses lines out. A fail-fast reader stops and shouts "the list changed under me!". A fail-safe reader either works from a photocopy taken at the start, or just carries on and may or may not see the latest edits.

Iterator and ListIterator

// for (String s : list) { ... }  is compiled to roughly:
Iterator<String> it = list.iterator();
while (it.hasNext()) {
    String s = it.next();
    ...
}

// ListIterator: both directions, index, set and add while walking
List<String> words = new ArrayList<>(List.of("a", "b", "c"));
ListIterator<String> li = words.listIterator();
while (li.hasNext()) {
    int idx = li.nextIndex();
    String w = li.next();
    li.set(w.toUpperCase());         // replace the element just returned
    if (idx == 0) li.add("a2");      // insert after it; the cursor skips past it
}
// words = [A, a2, B, C]
StringBuilder back = new StringBuilder();
while (li.hasPrevious()) back.append(li.previous());
// back = "CBa2A"
IteratorListIterator
Works onEvery CollectionList only
DirectionForwardForward and backward (hasPrevious, previous)
Modifyremove()remove(), set(e), add(e)
PositionNonextIndex(), previousIndex()

ConcurrentModificationException and modCount

Each ArrayList/HashMap keeps a counter, modCount, that goes up on every structural change (add, remove, clear, resize — but not set). An iterator copies it into expectedModCount when created. Every next() compares the two; if they differ, it throws.

list = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5] modCount = 0 iterator created expectedModCount = 0 next() -> 1 0 == 0 ok next() -> 2 0 == 0 ok list.remove(2) modCount = 1 (NOT through the iterator) next() 1 != 0 -> ConcurrentModificationException it.remove() instead removes AND sets expectedModCount = modCount -> safe
List<Integer> list = new ArrayList<>(List.of(1, 2, 3, 4, 5));
for (Integer x : list) if (x == 2) list.remove(x);
// ConcurrentModificationException  (list is now [1, 3, 4, 5])

// The famous quirk: removing the SECOND-TO-LAST element does not throw
List<Integer> q = new ArrayList<>(List.of(1, 2, 3));
for (Integer x : q) if (x == 2) q.remove(x);
// no exception, q = [1, 3] -- but 3 was never visited!
// hasNext() is just "cursor != size": cursor=2, size=2, so the loop ends before next() can check.

Map<String, Integer> hm = new HashMap<>(Map.of("a", 1, "b", 2));
for (String k : hm.keySet()) hm.put(k + "x", 0);   // ConcurrentModificationException

The exception has "Concurrent" in its name but happens here in a single thread. Fail-fast is also only "best effort": with real multi-threading, it is not guaranteed to fire, so never rely on it for correctness — it exists to reveal bugs.

The right ways to remove while iterating

// 1. Iterator.remove()
Iterator<Integer> it = r.iterator();
while (it.hasNext()) if (it.next() % 2 == 0) it.remove();
// [1, 2, 3, 4, 5] -> [1, 3, 5]

// 2. removeIf (Java 8) -- shortest, and O(n) for ArrayList (one compaction pass)
r2.removeIf(x -> x % 2 == 0);                 // [1, 3, 5]

// 3. On maps, go through a view
scores.values().removeIf(v -> v < 50);        // {a=10, b=55, c=70} -> {b=55, c=70}
scores.entrySet().removeIf(e -> e.getKey().startsWith("tmp"));

// 4. Build a new collection (streams / filter) instead of mutating
List<Integer> odds = r.stream().filter(x -> x % 2 != 0).toList();

// 5. Index loop going BACKWARDS (removing forwards skips elements)
for (int i = list.size() - 1; i >= 0; i--) if (list.get(i) % 2 == 0) list.remove(i);

Removing with it.remove() in a loop over an ArrayList is O(n) per removal (each shifts the tail), so O(n²) overall; removeIf does it in one pass.

Fail-safe: CopyOnWriteArrayList and ConcurrentHashMap

// CopyOnWriteArrayList: every write copies the whole array; iterators use a SNAPSHOT
List<Integer> cow = new CopyOnWriteArrayList<>(List.of(1, 2, 3));
for (Integer x : cow) { if (x == 1) cow.add(99); System.out.print(x + " "); }
// prints: 1 2 3        (the snapshot doesn't see 99)
// cow afterwards: [1, 2, 3, 99]
cow.iterator().remove();   // UnsupportedOperationException -- snapshot iterators are read-only

// ConcurrentHashMap: weakly consistent iterators -- never throw CME
Map<String, Integer> chm = new ConcurrentHashMap<>(Map.of("a", 1, "b", 2, "c", 3));
for (String k : chm.keySet()) if (k.equals("a")) chm.remove("b");
// no exception; map is now {a=1, c=3}
Fail-fastFail-safe: snapshotFail-safe: weakly consistent
ExamplesArrayList, HashMap, HashSet, TreeMapCopyOnWriteArrayList, CopyOnWriteArraySetConcurrentHashMap, ConcurrentLinkedQueue, ConcurrentSkipListMap
Change during iterationThrows CME (best effort)Never throws; iterator doesn't see itNever throws; may or may not see it
Iterates overThe live collectionA copy of the array at iterator creationThe live structure
CostCheapEvery write copies O(n) — for read-mostly lists (listeners)Cheap; fine-grained concurrency
iterator.remove()SupportedUnsupportedSupported
