Interview angle: "What is ConcurrentModificationException and how do you avoid it?" — explain modCount vs expectedModCount, then offer Iterator.remove, removeIf, or a concurrent collection. "Fail-fast vs fail-safe?" — live check that throws vs copy/weakly-consistent iterators that don't. The second-to-last-element quirk is a favourite trick question: it shows you know hasNext() doesn't check modCount.

23. Optional

In plain words

Optional<T> (Java 8) is a small box that either holds one value or is empty. A method returns Optional<User> instead of a User that might be null, so the type itself tells the caller "there may be no result — handle that case". It is meant mainly as a return type.

Instead of if (x != null) checks everywhere, you chain steps like map, filter and orElse, and the empty case flows through automatically.

A parcel locker. You get a slip that says "locker 12" — it might contain your package or be empty, and the slip makes you check before walking away. Compare with null, which is like being handed nothing and finding out only when you try to open it.

Creating one

Optional.of("x");            // Optional[x]      value must be non-null
Optional.of(null);           // NullPointerException
Optional.ofNullable(null);   // Optional.empty   use when the value might be null
Optional.empty();            // Optional.empty

static Optional<User> findById(int id) {
    return Optional.ofNullable(db.get(id));   // Map.get returns null when absent
}

Transforming: map, flatMap, filter

findById(id) -> map(User::address) -> map(Address::city) -> orElse("unknown") Optional[User] Optional[Address] Optional["Pune"] "Pune" Optional[User] Optional.empty (null) Optional.empty "unknown" Optional.empty Optional.empty Optional.empty "unknown" Each step runs only if there is a value; a null result turns the chain empty.
record Address(String city) {}
record User(String name, Address address, String email) {
    Optional<String> emailOpt() { return Optional.ofNullable(email); }
}
// db: 1 -> Asha (Pune, asha@x.io),  2 -> Ben (no address, no email)

findById(1).map(User::address).map(Address::city).orElse("unknown");   // "Pune"
findById(2).map(User::address).map(Address::city).orElse("unknown");   // "unknown"
findById(9).map(User::address).map(Address::city).orElse("unknown");   // "unknown"

// map vs flatMap: use flatMap when the function ALREADY returns an Optional
findById(1).map(User::emailOpt);       // Optional[Optional[asha@x.io]]  -- nested
findById(1).flatMap(User::emailOpt);   // Optional[asha@x.io]            -- flat

findById(1).filter(u -> u.name().startsWith("A"))
           .ifPresentOrElse(u -> System.out.println("hello " + u.name()),
                            () -> System.out.println("nobody"));        // hello Asha

Getting the value out

MethodIf presentIf emptySince
orElse(x)valuex (always evaluated!)8
orElseGet(() -> x)valuecalls the supplier8
orElseThrow(() -> ex)valuethrows your exception8
orElseThrow()valueNoSuchElementException10
get()valueNoSuchElementException — same as above, but the name hides it8
ifPresent(fn) / ifPresentOrElse(fn, run)runs fnnothing / runs run8 / 9
or(() -> otherOptional)itselfthe other Optional9
stream()1-element streamempty stream9
isPresent() / isEmpty()true / falsefalse / true8 / 11

orElse vs orElseGet — the side-effect trap

The argument to orElse is an ordinary expression, so Java evaluates it before calling the method — even when the Optional has a value and the result is thrown away. orElseGet takes a Supplier and calls it only when empty.

static int calls = 0;
static String expensiveDefault() {
    calls++;
    System.out.println("  expensiveDefault() called");
    return "default";
}

System.out.println("orElse on present value:");
String v1 = Optional.of("real").orElse(expensiveDefault());
System.out.println("  result=" + v1);
System.out.println("orElseGet on present value:");
String v2 = Optional.of("real").orElseGet(OptDemo::expensiveDefault);
System.out.println("  result=" + v2);
System.out.println("calls=" + calls);

// Output:
// orElse on present value:
//   expensiveDefault() called        <- ran for nothing
//   result=real
// orElseGet on present value:
//   result=real                      <- supplier never called
// calls=1

Rule: orElse for constants (orElse("unknown"), orElse(0)); orElseGet when the default costs something or has side effects (database call, new object, logging). A real bug: findUser(id).orElse(createUser(id)) creates a user in the database every time.

orElseThrow and chaining alternatives

User u = findById(9).orElseThrow(() -> new NoSuchElementException("user 9 not found"));
// NoSuchElementException: user 9 not found

findById(9).orElseThrow();   // NoSuchElementException: No value present
findById(9).get();           // NoSuchElementException: No value present

findById(9).or(() -> findById(2)).map(User::name).orElse("-");   // "Ben"

// Flatten a list of Optionals: keep only the present ones
List.of(1, 2, 9).stream()
    .map(OptDemo::findById)
    .flatMap(Optional::stream)                 // drops the empty ones
    .flatMap(u -> u.emailOpt().stream())
    .toList();                                 // [asha@x.io]

Anti-patterns

Don'tWhyDo instead
if (opt.isPresent()) return opt.get();Just a null check with extra stepsopt.orElse(...), map, ifPresent
opt.get() without checkingThrows on empty — the NPE problem again, renamedorElseThrow() (same behaviour, but the name admits it)
Optional fieldsOptional is not Serializable, adds an object per field, and wasn't designed for itNullable field; return Optional.ofNullable(field) from the getter
Optional parametersCallers must wrap; they can still pass null for the Optional itselfOverloads, or a nullable parameter checked inside
Optional<List<T>>Two ways to say "nothing"Return an empty list
Returning null from a method typed OptionalDefeats the whole pointReturn Optional.empty()
Optional<Integer> in hot pathsBoxing plus a wrapper objectOptionalInt/OptionalLong/OptionalDouble (e.g. IntStream.max())
== or synchronized on an OptionalIt is a value-based class; identity is not meaningfulequals: Optional.of("a").equals(Optional.of("a")) is true
OptionalInt max = IntStream.of().max();   // OptionalInt.empty
max.orElse(-1);                           // -1
Interview angle: "Why Optional?" — to make "maybe no result" explicit in a method's return type and push callers to handle it, reducing NPEs. "orElse vs orElseGet?" — eager vs lazy; describe the side-effect demo. "map vs flatMap?" — flatMap when the function already returns an Optional, to avoid Optional<Optional<T>>. "Where should you NOT use it?" — fields, parameters, collections, and anywhere get() without a check sneaks in. Brian Goetz's summary: Optional is for return types where "no result" is a normal outcome.
Part 4 — Functional Java

24. Lambdas & Functional Interfaces

In plain words

A lambda (Java 8) is a short, nameless function you can store in a variable or pass to a method: (a, b) -> a + b. It lets you hand behaviour around the way you hand data around.

A lambda needs a "slot" to fit into: a functional interface, which is simply an interface with exactly one abstract method. The lambda becomes the body of that one method. Runnable, Comparator and Function are all functional interfaces.

A job form with one blank line: "What should I do with each item? ______". The lambda is what you write on that line. The form (interface) decides what goes in and what comes out; you only fill in the action.

Syntax, from long to short

@FunctionalInterface
interface Calculator { int apply(int a, int b); }   // one abstract method

// Before Java 8: an anonymous class
Calculator addOld = new Calculator() {
    @Override public int apply(int a, int b) { return a + b; }
};

// Lambda forms
Calculator add = (a, b) -> a + b;                    // types inferred, expression body
Calculator mul = (int a, int b) -> { return a * b; }; // explicit types, block body needs return
Runnable hello = () -> System.out.println("hi");     // no parameters: empty ()
Function<String, Integer> len = s -> s.length();     // one parameter: () optional
BiFunction<Integer, Integer, Integer> sum = (var x, var y) -> x + y;  // var allowed (Java 11)

add.apply(2, 3);   // 5
mul.apply(2, 3);   // 6

The compiler works out the lambda's type from the target type — the interface it is assigned or passed to. The same lambda text () -> 42 can be a Supplier<Integer> or a Callable<Integer> depending on where it lands. A lambda on its own, with no target, does not compile (var f = x -> x; is an error).

@FunctionalInterface

The annotation is optional, but it makes the compiler check that the interface really has exactly one abstract method. If someone later adds a second abstract method, the build fails instead of every lambda breaking silently. default and static methods do not count, and neither do methods that match Object's public methods (that is why Comparator can declare equals and still be functional).

The built-in functional interfaces (java.util.function)

InterfaceMethodShapeTypical use
Function<T, R>R apply(T t)T in, R outmap, converting values
BiFunction<T, U, R>R apply(T t, U u)two in, one outMap.merge, replaceAll
Supplier<T>T get()nothing in, T outlazy values, factories, orElseGet
Consumer<T>void accept(T t)T in, nothing outforEach, side effects
BiConsumer<T, U>void accept(T t, U u)two in, nothing outMap.forEach
Predicate<T>boolean test(T t)T in, yes/no outfilter, removeIf
UnaryOperator<T>T apply(T t)Function where in and out are the same typeList.replaceAll, Stream.iterate
BinaryOperator<T>T apply(T a, T b)two T in, T outreduce
Function<String, Integer> len    = s -> s.length();
Supplier<List<String>> newList   = ArrayList::new;
Consumer<String> printer         = s -> System.out.println("> " + s);
Predicate<String> isEmpty        = String::isEmpty;
UnaryOperator<String> upper      = String::toUpperCase;
BinaryOperator<Integer> max      = Math::max;

len.apply("java");      // 4
newList.get().size();   // 0
printer.accept("hi");   // prints "> hi"
isEmpty.test("");       // true
upper.apply("abc");     // "ABC"
max.apply(3, 9);        // 9

Primitive specialisations — avoid boxing

Generics only work with objects, so Function<Integer, Integer> boxes every int into an Integer. For hot code Java offers primitive versions that take and return real int/long/double:

FamilyExamples
PredicateIntPredicate, LongPredicate, DoublePredicate
Function (primitive in)IntFunction<R>, IntUnaryOperator, IntToLongFunction
Function (primitive out)ToIntFunction<T>, ToLongFunction<T>, ToDoubleFunction<T>
Supplier / ConsumerIntSupplier, BooleanSupplier, IntConsumer, ObjIntConsumer<T>
OperatorsIntBinaryOperator, LongBinaryOperator, DoubleUnaryOperator
IntPredicate even      = n -> n % 2 == 0;     // even.test(4)          -> true
IntUnaryOperator sq    = n -> n * n;          // sq.applyAsInt(7)      -> 49
ToIntFunction<String> l = String::length;     // l.applyAsInt("abc")   -> 3
IntBinaryOperator plus = Integer::sum;        // plus.applyAsInt(2, 3) -> 5

Note the method names change (applyAsInt, test, getAsInt) so the compiler never has to box.

Composing functions

Default methods on these interfaces let you glue small functions into bigger ones without writing a new lambda.

f.andThen(g) : x -> f -> g -> result "do f, THEN g" f.compose(g) : x -> g -> f -> result "run g FIRST, feed it into f" plus2 = x + 2, times3 = x * 3, input 1 plus2.andThen(times3) : (1 + 2) * 3 = 9 plus2.compose(times3) : (1 * 3) + 2 = 5
Function<Integer, Integer> plus2  = x -> x + 2;
Function<Integer, Integer> times3 = x -> x * 3;
plus2.andThen(times3).apply(1);   // 9
plus2.compose(times3).apply(1);   // 5
Function.<Integer>identity().apply(42);   // 42 -- returns its input

Predicate<String> longWord = s -> s.length() > 3;
Predicate<String> startsA  = s -> s.startsWith("a");
longWord.and(startsA).test("apple");           // true  (both)
longWord.or(startsA).test("an");               // true  (either)
longWord.negate().test("apple");               // false
Predicate.not(String::isBlank).test(" ");      // false  (Java 11; handy in filter)

Consumer<List<String>> addA = l -> l.add("a");
List<String> list = new ArrayList<>();
addA.andThen(l -> l.add("b")).accept(list);    // list is [a, b]

and / or short-circuit exactly like && / ||: the second predicate is not called if the first already decides the answer.

Capturing variables: "effectively final"

A lambda can read local variables from the method around it, but only if they are final or effectively final — assigned once and never changed afterwards.

int base = 10;
Function<Integer, Integer> addBase = x -> x + base;   // OK: base never changes
addBase.apply(5);                                     // 15

int count = 0;
List.of("a", "b").forEach(s -> count++);
// error: local variables referenced from a lambda expression
//        must be final or effectively final

Why the rule? Local variables live on the thread's stack and vanish when the method returns, but the lambda may run later or on another thread. So Java copies the value into the lambda. If the variable could still change, the copy and the original would disagree — the rule removes that confusion (and data races on locals). Fields and array elements are not copied (the lambda holds a reference to the object), so they can be changed:

int[] counter = {0};                                 // the array reference is effectively final
List.of("a", "b", "c").forEach(s -> counter[0]++);   // compiles; counter[0] == 3
// Works, but it is a code smell and NOT thread-safe. Prefer stream.count() or an AtomicInteger.

this: lambda vs anonymous class

public class Demo {
    void show() {
        Runnable lambda = () -> System.out.println(this.getClass().getSimpleName());
        Runnable anon = new Runnable() {
            public void run() { System.out.println(this.getClass().getName()); }
        };
        lambda.run();   // Demo      -- 'this' is the enclosing object
        anon.run();     // Demo$1    -- 'this' is the anonymous object itself
    }
}
LambdaAnonymous class
thisThe enclosing instanceThe anonymous object
New scope?No — cannot redeclare a local variable name from the outer methodYes — can shadow outer names
State (fields)NoneCan have fields and several methods
TargetOnly functional interfacesAny interface or (abstract) class
Compiled toAn invokedynamic call site; no extra .class file; stateless lambdas are reused as one objectA separate Outer$1.class, new object per evaluation
Interview angle: "What is a functional interface?" — one abstract method; @FunctionalInterface is a compile-time check. "Why effectively final?" — the value is copied into the lambda, so it must not change. "Can a lambda throw a checked exception?" — only if the interface method declares it; Function.apply does not, so you must catch inside or write your own interface like ThrowingFunction.
Best practice: keep lambdas to one or two lines; if it grows, move it into a named private method and use a method reference. Prefer the standard interfaces over inventing your own, and use the primitive versions (IntPredicate, ToIntFunction) in hot paths.

25. The Streams API

In plain words

A stream (Java 8) is a way to describe "take these items, keep some, change them, and combine the result" as a chain of steps, instead of writing the loop yourself. A stream does not store data; it pulls items from a source (a collection, an array, a file, a generator) and pushes them through the steps.

Every pipeline has three parts: a source, zero or more intermediate operations (filter, map, sorted — each returns a new stream), and exactly one terminal operation (collect, count, forEach) that actually runs the whole thing and produces a result.

A factory conveyor belt. Setting up the machines (intermediate steps) does nothing on its own. Only when someone at the end presses "start, I want the boxes" (the terminal operation) do items begin to move — one by one through every machine.

Building a pipeline

record Emp(String name, String dept, int salary) {}

List<String> highPaidEng = emps.stream()                 // source
    .filter(e -> e.dept().equals("Eng"))                  // intermediate
    .filter(e -> e.salary() >= 100)
    .map(Emp::name)                                       // Emp -> String
    .sorted()                                             // intermediate, stateful
    .toList();                                            // terminal (Java 16)

// Other sources
Stream.of("a", "b");                  Arrays.stream(intArray);
IntStream.range(0, 10);               // 0..9
Stream.iterate(1, n -> n * 2);        // infinite: 1, 2, 4, 8, ...
Stream.generate(Math::random);        // infinite
Files.lines(path);                    // lines of a file (close it: try-with-resources)
"text".chars();                       // IntStream of chars

Laziness — proved with peek

peek runs an action on each element as it passes. It lets us see when work really happens:

Stream<String> s = Stream.of("a", "bb", "ccc")
    .peek(x -> System.out.println("peek " + x));
System.out.println("pipeline built, nothing printed yet");
System.out.println("count of long = " + s.filter(x -> x.length() > 1).count());
pipeline built, nothing printed yet
peek a
peek bb
peek ccc
count of long = 2

Now watch how elements move. Streams process vertically — one element goes through every step before the next one starts — and findFirst stops as soon as it has an answer:

Optional<Integer> first = Stream.of(1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6)
    .peek(n -> System.out.println("saw " + n))
    .filter(n -> n % 2 == 0)
    .map(n -> n * 10)
    .findFirst();
System.out.println("result " + first.get());

// Output:
// saw 1
// saw 2
// result 20        -- elements 3..6 were never touched
peek trap: since Java 9, count() may skip the pipeline entirely if it can know the size up front. Stream.of("a", "b", "c").peek(System.out::println).count() prints nothing and returns 3. Use peek only for debugging, never for real side effects.

Stateless vs stateful operations

KindOperationsWhat it means
Statelessfilter, map, flatMap, peek, mapToIntEach element handled on its own; nothing remembered. Cheap, parallel-friendly.
Statefulsorted, distinct, limit, skip, takeWhile, dropWhileMust remember earlier elements. sorted must see all input before emitting anything, and needs O(n) memory.
Stream.of(3, 1, 2)
    .peek(n -> System.out.println("before sort " + n))
    .sorted()
    .forEach(n -> System.out.println("after sort " + n));
// before sort 3, before sort 1, before sort 2, after sort 1, after sort 2, after sort 3

Short-circuiting

A short-circuiting operation can finish without looking at every element — which is what makes infinite streams usable.

Stream.iterate(1, n -> n * 2).limit(5).toList();            // [1, 2, 4, 8, 16]
IntStream.iterate(1, n -> n + 1).anyMatch(n -> n * n > 50);  // true (stops at 8)
Stream.iterate(1, n -> n < 20, n -> n * 3).toList();        // [1, 3, 9]   (Java 9)
Stream.of(1, 2, 3, 10, 4).takeWhile(n -> n < 5).toList();   // [1, 2, 3]   (Java 9)
Stream.of(1, 2, 3, 10, 4).dropWhile(n -> n < 5).toList();   // [10, 4]
// Others: findFirst, findAny, allMatch, noneMatch

takeWhile is not filter: it stops at the first failure (the 4 after 10 is dropped).

map vs flatMap

map turns each element into exactly one element. flatMap turns each element into a stream of zero or more elements and flattens them into one stream.

map(List::size) [[1,2], [3], []] -> [2, 1, 0] one out per one in flatMap(List::stream) [[1,2], [3], []] -> [1, 2, 3] each list "opened up"
List<List<Integer>> nested = List.of(List.of(1, 2), List.of(3), List.of());
nested.stream().map(List::size).toList();          // [2, 1, 0]
nested.stream().flatMap(List::stream).toList();    // [1, 2, 3]

Stream.of("hi there", "java")
    .flatMap(line -> Arrays.stream(line.split(" ")))
    .toList();                                     // [hi, there, java]
// Also: mapMulti (Java 16) -- a push-style flatMap that avoids creating small streams

reduce — fold everything into one value

int sum = Stream.of(1, 2, 3, 4).reduce(0, Integer::sum);      // 10   identity + accumulator
Optional<Integer> max = Stream.of(1, 7, 3).reduce(Integer::max); // Optional[7]  no identity
Stream.<Integer>empty().reduce(Integer::sum);                  // Optional.empty

// Three-arg form: result type differs from element type
int totalLen = Stream.of("ab", "cde")
    .reduce(0, (acc, w) -> acc + w.length(), Integer::sum);    // 5  (combiner used in parallel)

Rules for correct (and parallel-safe) reduce: the identity must really be neutral (0 for sum, 1 for product), and the operation must be associative ((a+b)+c == a+(b+c)). Subtraction is not, so a parallel reduce with (a, b) -> a - b gives wrong answers. For building collections, use collect, not reduce.

A stream can be used only once

Stream<Integer> once = Stream.of(1, 2, 3);
once.count();
once.count();   // IllegalStateException: stream has already been operated upon or closed

// Need it twice? Keep a Supplier and make a fresh stream each time
Supplier<Stream<Integer>> nums = () -> Stream.of(1, 2, 3);
nums.get().count(); nums.get().count();   // fine

Primitive streams and the cost of boxing

Stream<Integer> holds Integer objects; IntStream, LongStream and DoubleStream hold raw numbers and add math methods (sum, average, summaryStatistics, range).

IntStream.rangeClosed(1, 5).summaryStatistics();
// IntSummaryStatistics{count=5, sum=15, min=1, average=3.000000, max=5}
IntStream.of(3, 5).average().getAsDouble();           // 4.0
Stream.of("a", "bb").mapToInt(String::length).sum();  // 3   object -> primitive
IntStream.range(0, 3).boxed().toList();               // [0, 1, 2]  primitive -> object

On a 5-million-element List<Integer>, reduce(0, Integer::sum) (unbox, add, re-box every step) ran several times slower than mapToInt(Integer::intValue).sum() in our test, and a plain for loop over an int range was faster still. Exact numbers vary by machine and JIT warm-up, but the order holds: convert to a primitive stream as early as you can.

Parallel streams — and when they hurt

.parallel() (or collection.parallelStream()) splits the source into chunks and runs them on the shared ForkJoinPool.commonPool() (about one thread per CPU core), then combines results.

// BROKEN: many threads calling ArrayList.add at once
List<Integer> unsafe = new ArrayList<>();
IntStream.range(0, 10_000).parallel().forEach(unsafe::add);
// In our run, 15 of 20 trials lost elements or threw ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException

// CORRECT: let the stream build the result
List<Integer> safe = IntStream.range(0, 10_000).parallel().boxed().toList();  // always 10000

IntStream.range(0, 8).parallel().forEach(i -> System.out.print(i + " "));
// e.g. 2 5 3 4 7 6 0 1   -- order is NOT kept (differs every run)
IntStream.range(0, 8).parallel().forEachOrdered(i -> System.out.print(i + " "));
// 0 1 2 3 4 5 6 7

Parallel hurts when:

  • The data is small (thousands of elements) — splitting and joining cost more than the work.
  • The source splits badly: LinkedList, Stream.iterate, I/O lines. ArrayList, arrays and IntStream.range split well.
  • The steps block (HTTP, DB calls) — they tie up the common pool that the whole JVM shares.
  • The pipeline has order-dependent ops (limit, findFirst, sorted) on an ordered stream, or shared mutable state / locks.
  • Boxing dominates the cost anyway.

It helps for large, CPU-heavy, independent work on an easily split source. Always measure (JMH) before and after.

Stream vs loop

Prefer a stream whenPrefer a loop when
The logic is a clear filter / map / group / collectYou need break, continue, early return with complex conditions
You want a declarative "what, not how" styleYou need checked exceptions, index access, or to change local variables
Grouping and aggregating (collectors do the hard part)Hot inner loops over primitive arrays where every nanosecond counts
You may want to try parallelism cheaplyDebugging step by step matters more than brevity
Interview angle: "Are streams lazy?" — yes, intermediate ops only build a recipe; the terminal op runs it, element by element. "Difference between map and flatMap?" "Can you reuse a stream?" "Is parallel always faster?" — no; give two reasons. "What is wrong with forEach(list::add)?" — side effects on shared state; use collect.
Best practice: no side effects inside lambdas (except in forEach at the end); one operation per line; filter before map and before sorted to shrink work early; close streams that wrap resources (Files.lines).

26. Collectors & Method References

In plain words

A collector is a recipe that tells collect(...) how to pour the stream's items into a final container: a list, a set, a map, a single string, a count, or a map of groups. The Collectors class has ready-made recipes you can nest inside each other.

A method reference (String::length) is a shorter way to write a lambda that only calls one existing method.

Sorting mail. toList is one big pile. groupingBy(city) is one pigeonhole per city. groupingBy(city, counting()) is a pigeonhole per city with just a number written on it instead of the letters.

All examples below use this data:

record Emp(String name, String dept, int salary) {}
List<Emp> emps = List.of(
    new Emp("Asha", "Eng", 120), new Emp("Ben", "Eng", 100),
    new Emp("Cara", "Sales", 80), new Emp("Dev", "Sales", 90),
    new Emp("Eli", "HR", 70));

toList: three versions, three behaviours

List<String> a = emps.stream().map(Emp::name).collect(Collectors.toList());
a.add("X");                  // works -- in practice an ArrayList (spec does not promise the type)

List<String> b = emps.stream().map(Emp::name).toList();         // Java 16
b.add("X");                  // UnsupportedOperationException -- unmodifiable

List<String> c = emps.stream().map(Emp::name).collect(Collectors.toUnmodifiableList()); // Java 10

Stream.of("a", null).toList();                                    // [a, null]  allows null
Stream.of("a", null).collect(Collectors.toUnmodifiableList());    // NullPointerException
Collectors.toList()Stream.toList()toUnmodifiableList()
Modifiable?Yes (not guaranteed)NoNo
NullsAllowedAllowedNPE
SinceJava 8Java 16Java 10

Swapping collect(Collectors.toList()) for .toList() during a refactor is a classic bug if some later code calls add on the result.

toMap and the duplicate-key trap

Map<String, Integer> byName = emps.stream()
    .collect(Collectors.toMap(Emp::name, Emp::salary));   // names are unique: fine

emps.stream().collect(Collectors.toMap(Emp::dept, Emp::name));
// IllegalStateException: Duplicate key Eng (attempted merging values Asha and Ben)

// Fix: a merge function says what to do when two values share a key
Map<String, Integer> deptTotal = emps.stream()
    .collect(Collectors.toMap(Emp::dept, Emp::salary, Integer::sum, TreeMap::new));
// {Eng=220, HR=70, Sales=170}   4th arg picks the map type (sorted here)

Other merge choices: (a, b) -> a keep first, (a, b) -> b keep last. Also, toMap throws NullPointerException if a value is null (it uses Map.merge inside).

groupingBy — the SQL GROUP BY of Java

groupingBy( classifier , [mapFactory] , [downstream collector] ) | | | key for each which Map what to do with each group element (HashMap default) (toList default) Eng -> [Asha, Ben] --counting()--> 2 --summingInt(salary)--> 220 HR -> [Eli] --counting()--> 1 --summingInt(salary)--> 70 Sales -> [Cara, Dev] --counting()--> 2 --summingInt(salary)--> 170
Map<String, List<Emp>> byDept = emps.stream().collect(Collectors.groupingBy(Emp::dept));
// HashMap: key order not defined

Map<String, Long> counts = emps.stream()
    .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(Emp::dept, TreeMap::new, Collectors.counting()));
// {Eng=2, HR=1, Sales=2}

Map<String, List<String>> names = emps.stream()
    .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(Emp::dept, TreeMap::new,
             Collectors.mapping(Emp::name, Collectors.toList())));
// {Eng=[Asha, Ben], HR=[Eli], Sales=[Cara, Dev]}

Map<String, Integer> sums = emps.stream()
    .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(Emp::dept, TreeMap::new, Collectors.summingInt(Emp::salary)));
// {Eng=220, HR=70, Sales=170}

Map<String, Double> avg = emps.stream()
    .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(Emp::dept, TreeMap::new, Collectors.averagingInt(Emp::salary)));
// {Eng=110.0, HR=70.0, Sales=85.0}

Map<String, Optional<Emp>> top = emps.stream()
    .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(Emp::dept,
             Collectors.maxBy(Comparator.comparingInt(Emp::salary))));
// Eng -> Asha, HR -> Eli, Sales -> Dev

// Word frequency -- a very common interview task
Map<String, Long> freq = Arrays.stream("the cat and the hat and the bat".split(" "))
    .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(Function.identity(), TreeMap::new, Collectors.counting()));
// {and=2, bat=1, cat=1, hat=1, the=3}

Useful downstream collectors: counting, summingInt/Long/Double, averagingInt, minBy/maxBy, mapping, filtering and flatMapping (Java 9), toSet, joining, collectingAndThen (e.g. unwrap the Optional from maxBy). Use groupingByConcurrent for parallel streams.

partitioningBy, joining, teeing

// partitioningBy: exactly two groups, keyed true / false
Map<Boolean, List<String>> part = emps.stream()
    .collect(Collectors.partitioningBy(e -> e.salary() >= 100,
             Collectors.mapping(Emp::name, Collectors.toList())));
// {false=[Cara, Dev, Eli], true=[Asha, Ben]}
Stream.<Emp>empty().collect(Collectors.partitioningBy(e -> true));
// {false=[], true=[]}   both keys always present (groupingBy would give {})

// joining: strings only, with optional separator, prefix, suffix
emps.stream().map(Emp::name).collect(Collectors.joining(", ", "[", "]"));
// [Asha, Ben, Cara, Dev, Eli]

// teeing (Java 12): feed every element to TWO collectors, then merge the results
double avgAll = emps.stream().collect(Collectors.teeing(
    Collectors.summingInt(Emp::salary),
    Collectors.counting(),
    (sum, n) -> (double) sum / n));                 // 92.0

record MinMax(int min, int max) {}
MinMax mm = emps.stream().map(Emp::salary).collect(Collectors.teeing(
    Collectors.minBy(Integer::compare), Collectors.maxBy(Integer::compare),
    (mn, mx) -> new MinMax(mn.get(), mx.get())));   // MinMax[min=70, max=120]

Method references — four kinds

KindSyntaxExampleSame as lambda
Static methodClass::staticMethodInteger::parseInts -> Integer.parseInt(s)
Instance method of a particular object (bound)object::methodprefix::concat, System.out::printlns -> prefix.concat(s)
Instance method of an arbitrary object of a type (unbound)Class::instanceMethodString::toUpperCases -> s.toUpperCase()
ConstructorClass::newArrayList::new, int[]::new() -> new ArrayList<>()
Function<String, Integer> parse = Integer::parseInt;            // static
parse.apply("42") + 1;                                          // 43

String prefix = "Hello, ";
Function<String, String> greet = prefix::concat;               // bound: receiver fixed now
greet.apply("Ana");                                             // "Hello, Ana"

Function<String, String> up = String::toUpperCase;             // unbound: receiver is the 1st arg
BiFunction<String, String, Boolean> eq = String::equalsIgnoreCase;
eq.apply("Java", "JAVA");                                       // true  ("Java".equalsIgnoreCase("JAVA"))

Supplier<ArrayList<String>> mk = ArrayList::new;               // constructor
Function<Integer, int[]> arr = int[]::new;                      // arr.apply(3).length == 3
String[] out = Stream.of("x", "y").toArray(String[]::new);      // [x, y]

The trick for unbound references: the first parameter of the functional interface becomes the object the method is called on; the rest become its arguments. A bound reference evaluates its receiver once, when the reference is created (and throws NPE right then if it is null).

Interview angle: "Group employees by department and count / sum / find the top earner" is asked constantly — know groupingBy with a downstream collector by heart. Also: "What happens with duplicate keys in toMap?", "Difference between Collectors.toList() and Stream.toList()?", "Name the four kinds of method reference."
Best practice: pass TreeMap::new or LinkedHashMap::new when the output order matters (tests, reports). Always give toMap a merge function unless keys are guaranteed unique. Use a method reference when it is clearer than the lambda, not by reflex.

27. Date & Time API

In plain words

Java 8 added java.time, a clean and immutable set of classes for dates and times. It replaced the old java.util.Date and Calendar, which were confusing and error-prone.

The key idea: pick the class that says exactly what you mean. A birthday is a date with no time or zone (LocalDate). A moment something happened on a server is a point on the global timeline (Instant). A meeting in New York is a local time in a zone (ZonedDateTime).

"My birthday is 20 May" (LocalDate) is true everywhere. "The rocket launched at this exact moment" (Instant) is one point in time for the whole planet. "Lunch at 12:00 in Tokyo" (ZonedDateTime) needs the city to mean anything.

Why java.util.Date and Calendar are bad

Date d = new Date(2024, 1, 15);           // deprecated constructor
d.getYear() + 1900;   // 3924  -- year is an offset from 1900
d.getMonth();         // 1     -- months start at 0, so 1 means FEBRUARY

Calendar cal = Calendar.getInstance();
cal.set(2024, 1, 15);                     // also February: 0-based months again

Date shared = new Date(0);
Date alias = shared;
alias.setTime(86_400_000L);               // mutable: changes 'shared' too
  • Mutable — a Date passed to another method or stored in a field can be changed behind your back; not thread-safe.
  • Bad naming — Date is really an instant (milliseconds since 1970), not a date; its toString uses the JVM's default zone.
  • 0-based months, 1900-based years.
  • SimpleDateFormat is not thread-safe — sharing one as a static field corrupts results under load.
  • No separate types for "date only", "time only", "duration".

Convert at the edges when an old API forces you: date.toInstant() and Date.from(instant).

The main classes

Human view (no zone) Machine view LocalDate 2024-03-10 Instant 2024-03-10T06:30:00Z LocalTime 14:30 (nanoseconds since 1970-01-01T00:00Z, always UTC) LocalDateTime 2024-03-10T14:30 | + ZoneId("America/New_York") ^ v | toInstant() ZonedDateTime 2024-03-10T01:30-05:00[America/New_York] ---+ OffsetDateTime 2024-03-10T12:00+05:00 (fixed offset, no DST rules)
ClassHoldsUse for
LocalDateyear-month-dayBirthdays, holidays, due dates
LocalTimehour:min:sec.nanosOpening hours "09:00"
LocalDateTimedate + time, no zoneA wall-clock value whose zone is known elsewhere; not a moment in time
ZonedDateTimedate + time + zone rulesScheduling in a region (handles DST)
OffsetDateTimedate + time + fixed offsetAPIs and DB columns (ISO-8601 with offset)
Instantpoint on the UTC timelineTimestamps, logs, event times, storing in the DB
Durationseconds + nanosMachine time amounts: "90 seconds", timeouts
Periodyears, months, daysCalendar amounts: "2 months", age

Working with dates — all immutable

LocalDate date = LocalDate.of(2024, 2, 28);    // months are 1-based here
date.plusDays(1);                     // 2024-02-29  (2024 is a leap year)
date.plusDays(2);                     // 2024-03-01
LocalDate.of(2024, 1, 31).plusMonths(1);        // 2024-02-29  clamped to last valid day
date.with(TemporalAdjusters.lastDayOfMonth());  // 2024-02-29
date.getDayOfWeek();                  // WEDNESDAY

LocalDate x = LocalDate.of(2024, 1, 1);
x.plusDays(5);                        // result thrown away!
System.out.println(x);                // 2024-01-01 -- unchanged; write x = x.plusDays(5)

Period age = Period.between(LocalDate.of(1990, 5, 20), LocalDate.of(2024, 3, 10));
// P33Y9M19D  -> 33 years, 9 months, 19 days
Duration shift = Duration.between(LocalTime.of(9, 15), LocalTime.of(17, 45));
// PT8H30M, shift.toMinutes() == 510
ChronoUnit.DAYS.between(LocalDate.of(2024, 1, 1), LocalDate.of(2024, 12, 31));  // 365

Instant.ofEpochSecond(0);                        // 1970-01-01T00:00:00Z
Instant.ofEpochMilli(1_700_000_000_000L);        // 2023-11-14T22:13:20Z

Formatting and parsing

DateTimeFormatter f = DateTimeFormatter.ofPattern("dd/MM/yyyy HH:mm");   // immutable, thread-safe
LocalDateTime.of(2024, 3, 10, 14, 30).format(f);     // "10/03/2024 14:30"
LocalDateTime.parse("25/12/2024 08:05", f);          // 2024-12-25T08:05
LocalDate.parse("2024-07-04");                       // ISO format needs no formatter
DateTimeFormatter.ofPattern("EEE, d MMM yyyy", Locale.US).format(date);   // "Wed, 28 Feb 2024"

LocalDate.parse("2024-13-01");
// DateTimeParseException: ... Invalid value for MonthOfYear (valid values 1 - 12): 13
The YYYY bug: Y is the week-based year, y is the calendar year. LocalDate.of(2024, 12, 30) formatted with "YYYY-MM-dd" gives 2025-12-30, because that day belongs to week 1 of 2025. Also MM = month but mm = minutes, and HH = 0–23 but hh = 1–12.

Time zones and DST pitfalls

A zone (ZoneId.of("America/New_York")) is a set of rules that says what offset from UTC applies on each date — and those offsets change with daylight saving time (DST). An offset (+05:00) is just a fixed number.

ZoneId ny = ZoneId.of("America/New_York");
// US clocks jump from 02:00 to 03:00 on 2024-03-10
ZonedDateTime t = ZonedDateTime.of(2024, 3, 10, 1, 30, 0, 0, ny);
// 2024-03-10T01:30-05:00[America/New_York]
t.plusHours(1);   // 2024-03-10T03:30-04:00  -- exactly 1 hour of real time later
t.plusDays(1);    // 2024-03-11T01:30-04:00  -- same wall-clock time next day (only 23 real hours)

ZonedDateTime.of(2024, 3, 10, 2, 30, 0, 0, ny);
// 2024-03-10T03:30-04:00  -- 02:30 does not exist (the "gap"); shifted forward

// Clocks fall back 02:00 -> 01:00 on 2024-11-03: 01:30 happens TWICE (the "overlap")
ZonedDateTime o = ZonedDateTime.of(2024, 11, 3, 1, 30, 0, 0, ny);
o.withEarlierOffsetAtOverlap();   // 2024-11-03T01:30-04:00
o.withLaterOffsetAtOverlap();     // 2024-11-03T01:30-05:00

ZonedDateTime noon = ZonedDateTime.of(2024, 3, 9, 12, 0, 0, 0, ny);
Duration.between(noon, noon.plusDays(1)).toHours();   // 23, not 24

t.withZoneSameInstant(ZoneId.of("Asia/Tokyo"));  // 2024-03-10T15:30+09:00  same moment
t.withZoneSameLocal(ZoneId.of("Asia/Tokyo"));    // 2024-03-10T01:30+09:00  same clock, different moment
  • Store and compute in UTC (Instant / OffsetDateTime in UTC); convert to a user's zone only for display.
  • Never use LocalDateTime for events — it has no zone, so "2024-11-03T01:30" is ambiguous and cannot be ordered across users.
  • Use region IDs (Europe/Paris), not abbreviations like "EST"/"PST" (ambiguous, ignore DST) or fixed offsets for people who live somewhere.
  • Don't rely on the server's default zone (ZoneId.systemDefault()) — pass zones explicitly.
  • Inject a Clock (LocalDate.now(clock)) so tests can freeze time with Clock.fixed(...).
  • Zone rules change by law; the JDK ships a tz database that needs updating (JDK updates or the TZUpdater tool).
Interview angle: "Why was java.time introduced?" — mutability, thread-unsafe formatter, 0-based months, no clear types. "Instant vs LocalDateTime?" — a moment in time vs a wall-clock reading with no zone. "Period vs Duration?" — calendar units vs exact seconds; adding Period.ofDays(1) across DST keeps the clock time, adding Duration.ofDays(1) adds exactly 24 hours.
Part 5 — Concurrency

28. Threads & Their Lifecycle

In plain words

A process is a running program with its own memory. A thread is one path of execution inside a process. A process can have many threads; they all share the same heap (objects), but each has its own stack (local variables and call chain) and program counter.

Threads let a program do several things at once: serve many users, keep a UI responsive while downloading, use every CPU core.

A restaurant is a process: one kitchen, one fridge (shared memory). The cooks are threads. They work in parallel and share ingredients — which is fast, but if two cooks grab the same pan at once, things go wrong. The rest of Part 5 is about keeping the cooks from colliding.

Process vs thread

ProcessThread
MemoryOwn address space, isolatedShares heap with other threads of the process; own stack
Cost to createHeavy (new memory map, OS structures)Lighter, but each platform thread reserves its own stack (about 1 MB by default on 64-bit)
CommunicationIPC: pipes, sockets, filesJust read/write shared objects (needs synchronization)
Crash impactOther processes unaffectedAn uncaught error kills that thread; a JVM crash kills all
Context switchExpensiveCheaper

A classic Java Thread is a platform thread: a thin wrapper around one OS thread. Java 21 also adds cheap virtual threads (section 34).

Three ways to create work for a thread

// 1. Extend Thread (least flexible: uses up your one superclass)
Thread t1 = new Thread() {
    @Override public void run() { System.out.println("subclass: " + Thread.currentThread().getName()); }
};

// 2. Pass a Runnable (preferred: task separated from the thread that runs it)
Thread t2 = new Thread(() -> System.out.println("runnable: " + Thread.currentThread().getName()),
                       "worker-2");
t1.start(); t1.join();     // subclass: Thread-0
t2.start(); t2.join();     // runnable: worker-2

// 3. Callable: returns a value and may throw checked exceptions
Callable<Integer> task = () -> 6 * 7;
FutureTask<Integer> ft = new FutureTask<>(task);   // a Runnable that captures the result
new Thread(ft).start();
ft.get();                                           // 42 (blocks until done)

// Java 21 builder API
Thread b = Thread.ofPlatform().name("built").start(() -> System.out.println("builder"));
b.join();
RunnableCallable<V>
Methodvoid run()V call() throws Exception
Returns a valueNoYes, through a Future
Checked exceptionsMust catch insideCan throw; rethrown wrapped in ExecutionException by Future.get()
Use withnew Thread(r), executorsExecutors (submit), FutureTask

In real code you rarely call new Thread; you submit tasks to an ExecutorService (section 31).

Thread lifecycle

Each state, seen by calling getState() in a real run:

Thread sleeper = new Thread(() -> { try { Thread.sleep(500); } catch (InterruptedException e) {} });
sleeper.getState();   // NEW
sleeper.start();
sleeper.getState();   // RUNNABLE
Thread.sleep(100);
sleeper.getState();   // TIMED_WAITING   (inside sleep)
sleeper.join();
sleeper.getState();   // TERMINATED

// thread calling lock.wait()                    -> WAITING
// thread trying to enter synchronized(lock)
//   while main holds that lock                  -> BLOCKED

BLOCKED vs WAITING: BLOCKED means "I want to enter a synchronized block but another thread holds the lock" — it gets out automatically when the lock frees. WAITING means "I chose to wait until someone signals me" (wait, join, LockSupport.park). A thread waiting on a ReentrantLock shows as WAITING, not BLOCKED, because those locks use park.

start() vs run()

Thread r = new Thread(() -> System.out.println("run() executes on: " + Thread.currentThread().getName()),
                      "worker");
r.run();     // run() executes on: main     -- just a normal method call, no new thread!
r.start();   // run() executes on: worker   -- new thread, which then calls run()
r.join();
r.start();   // IllegalThreadStateException -- a thread can be started only once

join, sleep, wait

  • t.join() — the calling thread waits until t finishes. join(ms) waits at most that long. It also guarantees you see everything t wrote (happens-before, section 29).
  • Thread.sleep(ms) — pause the current thread. It is static: t.sleep(1000) still sleeps the caller, not t.
  • obj.wait() — release obj's lock and wait for a notify.
Thread.sleepObject.wait
Belongs toThread (static)Object (any object)
Releases locks?No — keeps every lock it holdsYes — releases that object's monitor
Must hold the lock?NoYes, else IllegalMonitorStateException
Wakes up whenTime passes (or interrupt)notify/notifyAll, timeout, interrupt, or spuriously
PurposePauseCoordinate between threads

Interruption — the polite way to stop a thread

Java has no safe way to kill a thread (Thread.stop was deprecated long ago and throws UnsupportedOperationException since Java 20, because it could leave objects half-updated). Instead you ask it to stop with t.interrupt(), which sets a flag. The thread must cooperate:

// Case 1: the thread is blocked in sleep / wait / join -> it gets InterruptedException
Thread worker = new Thread(() -> {
    try {
        Thread.sleep(10_000);
    } catch (InterruptedException e) {
        // throwing InterruptedException CLEARS the flag
        System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().isInterrupted());   // false
        Thread.currentThread().interrupt();                            // restore it
        System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().isInterrupted());   // true
    }
});
worker.start(); worker.interrupt();

// Case 2: the thread is busy computing -> it must check the flag itself
Thread loop = new Thread(() -> {
    while (!Thread.currentThread().isInterrupted()) {
        // do a chunk of work
    }
    // clean up and exit
});
Never swallow InterruptedException. An empty catch loses the stop request and the thread keeps running. Either rethrow it (declare throws InterruptedException) or call Thread.currentThread().interrupt() so code higher up can see it. Also: Thread.interrupted() (static) reads and clears the flag; isInterrupted() only reads it.

Daemon threads

The JVM exits when all non-daemon (user) threads have finished. Daemon threads are background helpers (GC, JIT compiler threads) that do not keep the JVM alive — when the last user thread ends, daemons are stopped abruptly, without running finally blocks.

Thread d = new Thread(() -> {
    try { Thread.sleep(60_000); } catch (InterruptedException e) {}
    System.out.println("never printed");
});
d.setDaemon(true);   // must be called BEFORE start()
d.start();
// main returns -> JVM exits immediately (our run finished in about 1 second, not 60)

Never use a daemon thread for work that must finish (writing a file, committing to a DB). A new thread inherits daemon status from the thread that created it.

Interview angle: "What happens if you call run() instead of start()?" "Can you start a thread twice?" "Difference between sleep and wait?" (lock release is the key point). "Name the thread states" and "BLOCKED vs WAITING". "How do you stop a thread?" — interruption, not stop().
Best practice: prefer Runnable/Callable + an executor over subclassing Thread; always name threads (it makes thread dumps readable); handle interruption properly; set an UncaughtExceptionHandler so failures are logged, not lost.

29. synchronized, volatile & the Java Memory Model

In plain words

When several threads share data, two separate problems appear. Atomicity: a step like count++ is really three steps (read, add, write), and another thread can sneak in between them. Visibility: a value one thread writes may sit in a CPU cache or register, so another thread keeps seeing the old value.

synchronized fixes both: only one thread at a time runs the guarded code, and changes are published when the lock is released. volatile fixes only visibility. The Java Memory Model (JMM) is the rulebook that says when one thread is guaranteed to see another thread's writes.

A shared whiteboard with one marker. synchronized means you must hold the marker to write, so no two people scribble over each other. volatile is like a rule "always look at the actual board, not the photo you took earlier" — you see the latest value, but two people can still both read "5" and both write "6".

Race condition: count++ loses updates

static int count = 0;

// 4 threads, each does count++ 100,000 times
Runnable inc = () -> { for (int i = 0; i < 100_000; i++) count++; };
Thread[] ts = new Thread[4];
for (int i = 0; i < 4; i++) { ts[i] = new Thread(inc); ts[i].start(); }
for (Thread t : ts) t.join();
System.out.println(count);   // expected 400000

Three real runs printed 135641, 128279 and 113633 — different every time, never 400000 (the exact number is nondeterministic). Why:

count = 5 Thread A: read 5 ............... add 1 -> 6 .......... write 6 Thread B: ......... read 5 .......... add 1 -> 6 ............... write 6 Result: 6 (two increments happened, one was LOST)

A race condition is any bug where the result depends on the timing of threads. The code read-modify-write (count++) and check-then-act (if (!map.containsKey(k)) map.put(k, v)) are the two classic shapes.

synchronized and intrinsic locks

Every Java object has a built-in lock called its intrinsic lock or monitor. synchronized acquires it on entry and releases it on exit — even if an exception is thrown.

class Counter {
    private int count;
    private final Object lock = new Object();

    public synchronized void inc() { count++; }        // locks 'this'
    public static synchronized void sInc() { }         // locks Counter.class (a different lock!)

    public void incBlock() {
        // ...work that needs no lock...
        synchronized (lock) {                          // locks only this block, on a private object
            count++;
        }
    }
}
// With synchronized: all our runs printed exactly 400000.
synchronized methodsynchronized block
Lock usedthis (or the Class for static)Any object you choose
ScopeWhole methodOnly the critical lines — shorter lock hold, more throughput
SafetyOutside code can also lock on your object and interfereA private final lock object cannot be locked by anyone else

Reentrancy: a thread that already holds a lock can enter another block guarded by the same lock without deadlocking itself. The JVM keeps a hold count per owner.

static synchronized void outer() { inner(); }   // holds Counter.class lock
static synchronized void inner() { ... }        // same lock again: allowed, count goes to 2
// Without reentrancy, a synchronized method calling another would deadlock on itself,
// and so would a subclass overriding a synchronized method and calling super.

Pitfalls: locking on a boxed Integer or a String literal (shared across the JVM), locking on a field that gets reassigned, and guarding the same data with different locks in different places (no protection at all).

The visibility problem

static boolean flag = false;            // plain field
new Thread(() -> { while (!flag) { } System.out.println("saw flag"); }).start();
Thread.sleep(200);
flag = true;
// In our runs the reader was STILL spinning after 1 second, every time:
// the JIT turned the loop into "if (!flag) loop forever".

static volatile boolean vflag = false;  // volatile field
// Same code with vflag: "saw flag" printed and the thread ended right away.

(Whether the plain version hangs depends on the JIT and hardware — it is not guaranteed either way, which is exactly the problem.)

volatile

Marking a field volatile means: every read goes to the latest written value, every write becomes visible to other threads, and the compiler/CPU may not reorder other memory operations across it in unsafe ways. Use it for:

  • Status flags: volatile boolean running, written by one thread and read by others.
  • Publishing an immutable object: volatile Config current, replaced as a whole.
  • The instance field in double-checked locking (below).

Why volatile does not make count++ atomic: count++ is still read, add, write — three separate steps. volatile makes each read and write fresh, but another thread can still write between your read and your write. Our run with volatile int printed 277680 (of 400000). Use synchronized or AtomicInteger for compound actions.

Happens-before, in easy words

"A happens-before B" is the JMM's promise: everything A's thread did before A is visible to B's thread after B. Without such a link, the other thread may see old values or see writes in a strange order. The main rules:

RuleA happens-before B when...
Program orderA comes before B in the same thread
Monitor lockA is an unlock of lock L, B is a later lock of L
VolatileA is a write to volatile V, B is a later read of V
Thread startA is t.start(), B is anything inside t
Thread joinA is anything inside t, B is after t.join() returns
TransitivityA hb B and B hb C, so A hb C

Also: final fields set in a constructor are visible to every thread once the constructor finishes (if this did not escape). Concurrent collections and executors document their own happens-before edges (putting into a ConcurrentHashMap hb getting it out).

int data; volatile boolean ready;
// Thread A                      // Thread B
data = 42;                       if (ready) {
ready = true;  // volatile write     System.out.println(data);   // guaranteed 42
                                 }
// The volatile write/read pair carries 'data' along with it.

Double-checked locking singleton

final class Config {
    private static volatile Config instance;          // volatile is ESSENTIAL
    private Config() { }

    static Config get() {
        Config local = instance;                      // one volatile read on the fast path
        if (local == null) {                          // check 1: no lock
            synchronized (Config.class) {
                local = instance;
                if (local == null) {                  // check 2: with lock
                    instance = local = new Config();
                }
            }
        }
        return local;
    }
}
// 4 threads calling Config.get() at once: constructor ran exactly once.

Why volatile? new Config() is allocate, run constructor, assign reference — and without volatile the CPU/JIT may assign the reference before the constructor finishes. Another thread passing check 1 would then get a half-built object. Simpler alternatives: the holder idiom (private static class Holder { static final Config I = new Config(); }, lazy and thread-safe thanks to class initialization) or an enum singleton.

wait / notify

wait, notify and notifyAll let threads coordinate on a condition ("buffer not empty") while holding the object's monitor.

final class Box {                       // bounded buffer, capacity 2
    private final Deque<Integer> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
    private final int cap = 2;

    synchronized void put(int v) throws InterruptedException {
        while (q.size() == cap) wait();  // releases the lock while waiting
        q.addLast(v);
        notifyAll();                     // wake consumers
    }
    synchronized int take() throws InterruptedException {
        while (q.isEmpty()) wait();
        int v = q.removeFirst();
        notifyAll();                     // wake producers
        return v;
    }
}
// Producer puts 1..5 then -1; consumer prints: 1 2 3 4 5
  • Always call them inside synchronized on the same object, or you get IllegalMonitorStateException.
  • Always wait in a while loop, never if: threads can wake spuriously, or another thread may grab the item first.
  • Prefer notifyAll: notify wakes one arbitrary thread, which may be the wrong kind (a producer when a consumer was needed), causing a stall.
  • In new code use BlockingQueue or Lock + Condition (section 30) instead.
Interview angle: "Is count++ thread-safe? Is it with volatile?" — no and no. "Difference between synchronized and volatile?" — mutual exclusion + visibility vs visibility only. "Explain happens-before." "Why does double-checked locking need volatile?" "Why must wait be in a loop?" "Is synchronized reentrant?" — yes.

30. Locks & Atomic Variables

In plain words

java.util.concurrent.locks (Java 5) gives you explicit locks: objects you lock and unlock yourself. They do what synchronized does, plus extras — try to lock without waiting forever, give up if interrupted, be fair to waiting threads, allow many readers at once.

Atomic variables (AtomicInteger, LongAdder) make single updates like "add one" thread-safe without any lock, using a special CPU instruction called compare-and-swap (CAS).

synchronized is a bathroom door that locks automatically. A ReentrantLock is a key you carry: you can peek to see if the room is free and walk away (tryLock), but you must remember to return the key. An atomic variable is a tally counter at a turnstile: everyone clicks it, no queue needed.

ReentrantLock

final class Account {
    private final ReentrantLock lock = new ReentrantLock();
    private int balance = 100;

    boolean withdraw(int amt) {
        lock.lock();                  // outside the try
        try {
            if (balance < amt) return false;
            balance -= amt;
            return true;
        } finally {
            lock.unlock();            // ALWAYS in finally, or one exception locks it forever
        }
    }
}
// 4 threads x 10 withdrawals of 5 from 100: balance ends at 0, never negative.

ReentrantLock rl = new ReentrantLock();
rl.lock(); rl.lock();
rl.getHoldCount();    // 2  -- reentrant, just like synchronized; unlock twice

Its three extras, each verified while another thread was holding the lock:

// 1. tryLock: don't wait (or wait a bounded time) -- avoids deadlock, allows a fallback
lock.tryLock();                                // false (held by another thread)
lock.tryLock(100, TimeUnit.MILLISECONDS);      // false after 100 ms
if (lock.tryLock()) {
    try { /* work */ } finally { lock.unlock(); }
} else {
    // do something else, retry later, or report "busy"
}

// 2. lockInterruptibly: waiting can be cancelled with interrupt()
try {
    lock.lockInterruptibly();
    try { /* work */ } finally { lock.unlock(); }
} catch (InterruptedException e) {
    // "lockInterruptibly: gave up after interrupt"
    Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
}
// (A thread stuck in lock() or on synchronized ignores interrupts.)

// 3. Fairness: longest-waiting thread gets the lock next
ReentrantLock fair = new ReentrantLock(true);  // default is false (unfair = faster)

Fair vs unfair: an unfair lock lets a newly arriving thread "barge" ahead of the queue if the lock happens to be free — this avoids a context switch and gives much better throughput, but a thread can in theory wait a long time. Fair locks prevent starvation at a large speed cost. Keep the default unless you have measured starvation.

Condition — wait/notify for explicit locks

A Condition is a named wait-set tied to a lock. Unlike a monitor (which has one wait-set), a lock can have several, so producers and consumers wait on separate queues and signal wakes the right kind.

final class BoundedBuffer<T> {
    private final Deque<T> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
    private final int cap;
    private final ReentrantLock lock = new ReentrantLock();
    private final Condition notFull  = lock.newCondition();
    private final Condition notEmpty = lock.newCondition();

    BoundedBuffer(int cap) { this.cap = cap; }

    void put(T t) throws InterruptedException {
        lock.lock();
        try {
            while (q.size() == cap) notFull.await();   // like wait(): releases the lock
            q.addLast(t);
            notEmpty.signal();                         // wake ONE consumer
        } finally { lock.unlock(); }
    }
    T take() throws InterruptedException {
        lock.lock();
        try {
            while (q.isEmpty()) notEmpty.await();
            T t = q.removeFirst();
            notFull.signal();                          // wake ONE producer
            return t;
        } finally { lock.unlock(); }
    }
}
// Producer puts 1..5 into a capacity-2 buffer; consumer's sum is 15.

This is essentially how ArrayBlockingQueue is built. Same rules as wait: hold the lock, and await in a while loop.

ReadWriteLock and StampedLock

// Many readers at once OR one writer -- good for read-heavy data
final class Cache {
    private final Map<String, String> map = new HashMap<>();
    private final ReadWriteLock rw = new ReentrantReadWriteLock();

    String get(String k) {
        rw.readLock().lock();
        try { return map.get(k); } finally { rw.readLock().unlock(); }
    }
    void put(String k, String v) {
        rw.writeLock().lock();
        try { map.put(k, v); } finally { rw.writeLock().unlock(); }
    }
}

A write lock can be downgraded to a read lock (take read, then release write), but a read lock can not be upgraded to write — trying it deadlocks. If reads are very short, the bookkeeping of a read-write lock can make it slower than a plain lock; measure first.

StampedLock (Java 8) adds an optimistic read: read without locking at all, then check that no writer came in; if one did, retry with a real read lock. Very fast for read-mostly data, but it is not reentrant, has no Condition, and is easy to misuse.

double distance() {
    long stamp = sl.tryOptimisticRead();     // no lock taken
    double cx = x, cy = y;                   // copy fields into locals
    if (!sl.validate(stamp)) {               // a writer slipped in -> fall back
        stamp = sl.readLock();
        try { cx = x; cy = y; } finally { sl.unlockRead(stamp); }
    }
    return Math.sqrt(cx * cx + cy * cy);     // after move(3, 4): 5.0
}

synchronized vs ReentrantLock

synchronizedReentrantLock
UnlockAutomatic (even on exception)Manual, in finally
Try without blocking / timeoutNotryLock(), tryLock(time)
Interruptible wait for lockNolockInterruptibly()
Fairness optionNoYes
Wait conditionsOne (wait/notify)Many (newCondition())
Lock across methodsNo (block-scoped)Yes (lock in one method, unlock in another — careful)
Virtual threads (Java 21)Pins the carrier thread while blocked inside (fixed in Java 24)Does not pin

Default to synchronized for simple cases (shorter, can't forget to unlock). Reach for ReentrantLock when you need one of its extras — or with many virtual threads on Java 21.

Atomic variables and CAS

Compare-and-swap (CAS) is one CPU instruction that says: "if the value at this address is still expected, set it to new and tell me true; otherwise change nothing and tell me false." Because the check and the write happen as one indivisible step, no lock is needed.

incrementAndGet() as a CAS loop: loop: prev = value read 5 next = prev + 1 compute 6 CAS(value, 5 -> 6) succeeds if value is still 5 -> return 6 fails if another thread changed it -> go back to loop Nobody ever waits on a lock; a thread that loses just retries with the fresh value.
AtomicInteger ai = new AtomicInteger();
// 4 threads x 100,000 ai.incrementAndGet()  ->  exactly 400000

AtomicInteger v = new AtomicInteger(5);
v.compareAndSet(5, 6);                  // true,  v = 6
v.compareAndSet(5, 7);                  // false, v still 6 (expected value was wrong)
v.updateAndGet(x -> x * 10);            // 60   (function may run more than once: keep it pure)
v.accumulateAndGet(42, Math::max);      // 60

// Writing the CAS loop by hand: square the value atomically
AtomicInteger m = new AtomicInteger(3);
int prev, next;
do {
    prev = m.get();
    next = prev * prev;
} while (!m.compareAndSet(prev, next));  // m = 9

AtomicReference<String> ref = new AtomicReference<>("v1");
ref.compareAndSet("v1", "v2");           // true -- compares REFERENCES (==), not equals()

Family: AtomicInteger, AtomicLong, AtomicBoolean, AtomicReference, AtomicIntegerArray, and field updaters. ABA problem: a value goes A to B and back to A; a CAS expecting A succeeds even though things changed in between. It matters for lock-free linked structures; AtomicStampedReference fixes it by pairing the value with a version number.

LongAdder — for hot counters

Under heavy contention, many threads CAS-ing the same variable keep failing and retrying, and the cache line bounces between cores. LongAdder (Java 8) spreads the count over several internal cells (roughly one per contending thread) and only adds them up when you call sum().

LongAdder hits = new LongAdder();
hits.increment();          // cheap even with many threads
hits.add(5);
long total = hits.sum();   // not an atomic snapshot if updates are still happening

// Our rough run, 8 threads x 2,000,000 increments each:
//   AtomicLong 842 ms, LongAdder 124 ms   (both correct: 16000000)
// Timings vary by machine; the gap grows with the number of contending threads.

Use LongAdder for statistics counters that are written a lot and read rarely. Use AtomicLong when you need the exact current value on every update (like generating IDs) or compareAndSet.

When to use which

NeedUse
A flag or reference written by one thread, read by othersvolatile
A single counter or value updated by many threadsAtomicInteger / AtomicLong / AtomicReference
A very hot counter, read rarelyLongAdder
Several fields that must change together (invariants)synchronized (simple) or ReentrantLock
Need timeout, interruptible wait, fairness, several conditionsReentrantLock + Condition
Read-mostly shared data with long readsReentrantReadWriteLock, or StampedLock for top speed
Producer–consumer handoffBlockingQueue (don't hand-roll it)
Shared mapConcurrentHashMap (section 33), not a locked HashMap
Interview angle: "synchronized vs ReentrantLock — when would you pick the lock?" (tryLock, timeout, interruptible, fairness, conditions). "What is CAS? Explain AtomicInteger.incrementAndGet." "What is the ABA problem?" "AtomicLong vs LongAdder?" "Why must unlock go in finally?" "Can a read lock be upgraded?"
Best practice: lock() right before try, unlock() in finally; keep critical sections short and never call unknown code (callbacks, I/O) while holding a lock; always acquire multiple locks in the same global order to avoid deadlock (section 34); prefer higher-level tools (ConcurrentHashMap, BlockingQueue, atomics) over hand-made locking.

31. Executors & Thread Pools

In plain words

Creating a new Thread for every small job is slow (each platform thread needs an OS thread and about 1 MB of stack) and dangerous (10,000 requests means 10,000 threads). A thread pool keeps a fixed team of worker threads alive and feeds them jobs from a queue. An Executor is the Java interface for "something that runs tasks for you" — you hand over the task and stop caring which thread runs it.

You submit Runnable (no result) or Callable (returns a value, may throw) tasks, and you get back a Future — a receipt you can use later to collect the result.

A restaurant kitchen. Customers do not hire a new cook for each order. Orders go on a rail (the queue), a fixed crew of cooks (the pool) takes them one by one, and the customer holds a ticket (the Future) to pick up the meal.

The interfaces

TypeWhat it adds
ExecutorOne method: execute(Runnable). Fire and forget.
ExecutorServicesubmit (returns a Future), invokeAll, invokeAny, lifecycle: shutdown, shutdownNow, awaitTermination. Since Java 19 it is AutoCloseable (close() = shutdown + wait).
ScheduledExecutorServiceschedule (run once after a delay), scheduleAtFixedRate, scheduleWithFixedDelay.
ThreadPoolExecutorThe real implementation behind most pools; every knob is exposed.
ForkJoinPoolWork-stealing pool for divide-and-conquer tasks; also runs parallel streams and default CompletableFuture async work.

The Executors factory methods — and their traps

FactoryThreadsQueueRisk
newFixedThreadPool(n)exactly nLinkedBlockingQueue, capacity Integer.MAX_VALUEQueue grows without limit under load → OutOfMemoryError
newSingleThreadExecutor()1unbounded LinkedBlockingQueueSame unbounded queue; useful for strict ordering
newCachedThreadPool()0 to Integer.MAX_VALUE, idle ones die after 60 sSynchronousQueue (holds nothing; hands off directly)A burst creates thousands of threads → out of memory / "unable to create native thread"
newScheduledThreadPool(n)n coredelay queue (unbounded)Tasks that throw silently stop repeating
newWorkStealingPool() (8)= CPU coresper-thread dequesA ForkJoinPool; not for blocking work
newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor() (21)one new virtual thread per tasknoneNo limit on concurrency: add a Semaphore to protect a database

I checked the first two rows with reflection on Java 21: the fixed pool's queue is a LinkedBlockingQueue with remainingCapacity() = 2147483647, and the cached pool uses a SynchronousQueue with maximum pool size 2147483647. That is why many style guides (Alibaba's Java manual is the famous one) forbid Executors.newXxx in production and ask you to build a ThreadPoolExecutor yourself so every limit is explicit.

How ThreadPoolExecutor decides what to do with a task

Building a pool the production way

ThreadPoolExecutor pool = new ThreadPoolExecutor(
        2,                                   // corePoolSize: threads kept alive even when idle
        4,                                   // maximumPoolSize: hard ceiling
        30, TimeUnit.SECONDS,                // keepAliveTime for threads above core
        new ArrayBlockingQueue<>(2),         // BOUNDED queue, capacity 2
        r -> new Thread(r, "worker-" + n.incrementAndGet()),   // name your threads!
        new ThreadPoolExecutor.AbortPolicy()); // what to do when full

// Submit 7 tasks that all block until we release them
for (int i = 1; i <= 7; i++) {
    try {
        pool.execute(blocker);
        System.out.printf("task %d accepted: threads=%d queued=%d%n",
                i, pool.getPoolSize(), pool.getQueue().size());
    } catch (RejectedExecutionException e) {
        System.out.println("task " + i + " REJECTED");
    }
}

Output (verified, deterministic because every task blocks on a latch):

task 1 accepted: threads=1 queued=0     <- core thread 1
task 2 accepted: threads=2 queued=0     <- core thread 2
task 3 accepted: threads=2 queued=1     <- core full, so queue
task 4 accepted: threads=2 queued=2     <- queue now full
task 5 accepted: threads=3 queued=2     <- extra thread
task 6 accepted: threads=4 queued=2     <- extra thread, max reached
task 7 REJECTED                         <- 4 running + 2 queued = capacity 6
terminated=true completed=6
Rejection policyBehaviourUse when
AbortPolicy (default)Throws RejectedExecutionExceptionCaller can handle "busy, try later" (e.g. return HTTP 503)
CallerRunsPolicyThe submitting thread runs the task itselfNatural back-pressure: the producer slows down because it is busy working
DiscardPolicySilently drops the new taskLoss is fine (e.g. metrics samples) — dangerous otherwise
DiscardOldestPolicyDrops the oldest queued task, retries the new oneOnly the latest data matters

Sizing a pool

  • CPU-bound work (compression, math): about number of cores (or cores + 1). More threads only add context switches.
  • I/O-bound work (HTTP, database): threads ≈ cores × (1 + wait time / compute time). A task that waits 90 ms and computes 10 ms on 8 cores → 8 × 10 = 80 threads. In Java 21, virtual threads are often simpler here (see s34).
  • Always bound the queue and pick a rejection policy on purpose. Name threads with a ThreadFactory so thread dumps are readable.

submit vs execute

execute(Runnable)submit(Runnable / Callable)
Declared inExecutorExecutorService
ReturnsvoidFuture<T>
If the task throwsException kills the worker thread (the pool replaces it) and is printed by the default UncaughtExceptionHandlerException is stored in the Future; nothing is printed. You only see it when you call get(), wrapped in ExecutionException
ExecutorService pool = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(2);
Future<Integer> f = pool.submit(() -> 10 / 0);
try {
    f.get();
} catch (ExecutionException e) {
    System.out.println("get() threw: " + e.getCause());
}
// get() threw: java.lang.ArithmeticException: / by zero

Future<String> ok = pool.submit(() -> "hello");
ok.get(1, TimeUnit.SECONDS);   // "hello" -- ALWAYS prefer the timeout version
ok.isDone();                   // true
// also: f.cancel(true) interrupts the running task; f.isCancelled()
Classic bug: pool.submit(task) and never call get(). If the task throws, the error disappears without a trace. Either use execute, call get(), or catch and log inside the task. Future.get() with no timeout blocks forever if the task hangs.

invokeAll, invokeAny and scheduling

List<Callable<Integer>> tasks = List.of(() -> 1, () -> 2, () -> 3);
int sum = 0;
for (Future<Integer> r : pool.invokeAll(tasks)) sum += r.get();   // waits for ALL
// sum = 6; futures come back in the same order as the tasks
pool.invokeAny(tasks);   // result of ONE task that succeeded; the rest are cancelled

ScheduledExecutorService ses = Executors.newScheduledThreadPool(1);
ScheduledFuture<String> once = ses.schedule(() -> "ran after delay", 50, TimeUnit.MILLISECONDS);
once.get();   // "ran after delay"
ses.scheduleAtFixedRate(this::poll, 0, 20, TimeUnit.MILLISECONDS);     // start every 20 ms
ses.scheduleWithFixedDelay(this::poll, 0, 20, TimeUnit.MILLISECONDS);  // 20 ms gap AFTER each run ends

Fixed rate vs fixed delay: fixed rate keeps a steady clock (if a run is late, the next ones start back-to-back to catch up, never overlapping); fixed delay waits the full delay after each run ends. If a periodic task throws, all its future runs are cancelled silently — wrap the body in try/catch.

Shutting down

ExecutorService single = Executors.newSingleThreadExecutor();
single.submit(() -> { try { Thread.sleep(10_000); } catch (InterruptedException e) {
    System.out.println("running task interrupted"); } });
for (int i = 0; i < 3; i++) single.submit(() -> {});   // these wait in the queue

List<Runnable> neverRan = single.shutdownNow();   // prints "running task interrupted"
neverRan.size();                                   // 3
single.awaitTermination(1, TimeUnit.SECONDS);      // true
shutdown()shutdownNow()
Stops accepting new tasks (they get RejectedExecutionException)Same
Lets running and queued tasks finishInterrupts running tasks, returns queued tasks as a list, never runs them
Returns immediately; call awaitTermination to waitReturns immediately; tasks that ignore interrupts keep running

The standard pattern: shutdown(), then awaitTermination(timeout), and if it returns false, shutdownNow(). Pool threads are non-daemon, so forgetting to shut down a pool keeps the JVM alive after main ends. In Java 19+ you can write try (ExecutorService ex = ...) { ... } and close() does the shutdown and wait for you.

ForkJoinPool and work stealing

A ForkJoinPool gives each worker its own double-ended queue (deque). A task splits itself (fork) and pushes the halves onto its own deque; the owner pops from the top (newest, still-hot in cache). An idle worker steals from the bottom of someone else's deque (the oldest, usually biggest piece). Owners and thieves work at opposite ends, so they rarely collide.

worker 1 deque: [ big-old | mid | small-new ] <- owner pushes/pops here (LIFO) ^ idle worker 2 steals here (FIFO) -- takes the biggest chunk
class SumTask extends RecursiveTask<Long> {     // RecursiveAction if nothing is returned
    private static final int THRESHOLD = 10_000;
    private final long[] a; private final int lo, hi;
    SumTask(long[] a, int lo, int hi) { this.a = a; this.lo = lo; this.hi = hi; }

    @Override protected Long compute() {
        if (hi - lo <= THRESHOLD) {                // small enough: just do it
            long s = 0;
            for (int i = lo; i < hi; i++) s += a[i];
            return s;
        }
        int mid = (lo + hi) >>> 1;
        SumTask left = new SumTask(a, lo, mid);
        SumTask right = new SumTask(a, mid, hi);
        left.fork();                               // queue left half (may be stolen)
        long r = right.compute();                  // do right half in this thread
        return left.join() + r;                    // then collect left
    }
}
long[] data = new long[1_000_000];                 // 1..1,000,000
long total = ForkJoinPool.commonPool().invoke(new SumTask(data, 0, data.length));
// total = 500000500000

The common pool is shared by the whole JVM: parallel streams and CompletableFuture.supplyAsync without an executor both use it. Its parallelism is cores − 1 (verified: getParallelism() == availableProcessors() - 1 printed true). Never put blocking I/O on it — you starve every parallel stream in the application.

Interview angle: "Why not Executors.newFixedThreadPool?" — unbounded queue, silent memory growth. "Walk me through what happens when a task is submitted" — the three checks in the diagram. "What does CallerRunsPolicy give you?" — back-pressure. "Difference between submit and execute for exceptions?" — Future swallows it until get().

32. CompletableFuture

In plain words

A plain Future can only be asked "are you done?" or be waited on with a blocking get(). A CompletableFuture (Java 8) lets you say "when this finishes, do that next" without blocking: you chain steps, combine several async results, and handle errors, like a pipeline. It is Java's version of a JavaScript Promise.

Ordering at a food court with a buzzer. You don't stand at the counter (blocking get()). You tell them "when the burger is ready, add fries, then buzz me", and meanwhile you find a table. If the kitchen runs out, the plan says what to serve instead.

Starting async work

ExecutorService io = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(4, r -> new Thread(r, "io-pool"));

CompletableFuture<User> user = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> loadUser(1), io);  // returns a value
CompletableFuture<Void> log  = CompletableFuture.runAsync(() -> audit("login"), io);  // no value
CompletableFuture<String> ready = CompletableFuture.completedFuture("x");              // already done

// Manual completion -- bridge a callback API into a future
CompletableFuture<String> cf = new CompletableFuture<>();
client.onResponse(body -> cf.complete(body));
client.onError(ex -> cf.completeExceptionally(ex));

Without the second argument, supplyAsync runs on ForkJoinPool.commonPool(). For anything that blocks (HTTP, JDBC, files) always pass your own executor, otherwise a few slow calls block the shared pool used by parallel streams.

thenApply vs thenCompose vs thenCombine

thenApply A --(T -> U)--> B map: plain function on the result thenCompose A --(T -> CompletableFuture<U>)--> B flatMap: next step is itself async thenCombine A ---\ >--(T, U) -> V--> C two INDEPENDENT futures, run in parallel B ---/
// thenApply: transform the value
CompletableFuture<String> name = fetchUser(1, io).thenApply(User::name);
name.join();                                                        // "user1"

// If the function returns a future, thenApply NESTS it
CompletableFuture<CompletableFuture<Integer>> nested =
        fetchUser(2, io).thenApply(u -> fetchScore(u, io));        // awkward: join().join()
// thenCompose flattens it -- use it when step 2 depends on step 1's result
CompletableFuture<Integer> score = fetchUser(2, io).thenCompose(u -> fetchScore(u, io));
score.join();                                                       // 20

// thenCombine: both start now, combine when both are done
CompletableFuture<Integer> price = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> 100, io);
CompletableFuture<Double>  rate  = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> 0.9, io);
price.thenCombine(rate, (p, r) -> p * r).join();                    // 90.0
MethodTakesStream / Optional twin
thenApplyFunction<T, U>map
thenComposeFunction<T, CompletableFuture<U>>flatMap
thenCombineanother future + BiFunction<T, U, V>zip
thenAcceptConsumer<T> → CompletableFuture<Void>forEach
thenRunRunnable (ignores the value)—
applyToEither / acceptEitheranother future; uses whichever finishes first—

Waiting for many: allOf and anyOf

List<CompletableFuture<User>> fs = IntStream.rangeClosed(1, 3)
        .mapToObj(i -> fetchUser(i, io)).toList();

List<String> names = CompletableFuture.allOf(fs.toArray(new CompletableFuture[0]))  // CompletableFuture<Void>
        .thenApply(v -> fs.stream().map(CompletableFuture::join)   // join is instant: all are done
                                   .map(User::name).toList())
