        .join();
// [user1, user2, user3]

CompletableFuture<Object> first = CompletableFuture.anyOf(slow, fast);   // Object, not T
first.join();   // "fast" (slow sleeps 300 ms; timing-based, but reliable in practice)

allOf returns Void — you collect results from the original futures. If any future fails, allOf fails too (after all have finished). anyOf completes with the first one to finish, even if that one failed.

Error handling: exceptionally, handle, whenComplete

CompletableFuture<Integer> boom = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> {
    throw new IllegalStateException("db down");
}, io);

boom.exceptionally(ex -> -1).join();                        // -1   (recover with a fallback)
// ex here is a CompletionException wrapping the IllegalStateException -- use ex.getCause()

boom.handle((v, ex) -> ex == null ? "ok " + v
                                  : "failed: " + ex.getCause().getMessage()).join();
// "failed: db down"   (always runs; sees value OR error; can change the result)

boom.whenComplete((v, ex) -> log(ex)).join();
// whenComplete only OBSERVES: join() still throws CompletionException (cause: db down)

boom.get();    // throws ExecutionException (checked)
boom.join();   // throws CompletionException (unchecked) -- nicer inside lambdas
MethodRuns onCan change the result?Like
exceptionally(fn)failure onlyyes: returns a replacement valuecatch
handle((v, ex) -> …)success and failureyes: returns a new valuecatch + normal path
whenComplete((v, ex) -> …)success and failureno: passes the original outcome onfinally
exceptionallyCompose (12)failure onlyyes, with another future (e.g. retry call)async catch

An exception skips every thenApply/thenCompose step after it, until the first exceptionally/handle — exactly like a throw jumping to the nearest catch.

Which thread runs the callback?

CompletableFuture.completedFuture("x")
        .thenApply(s -> ...);        // already done: runs right now in the CALLER thread -> main

CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> { sleep(100); return "y"; }, io)
        .thenApply(s -> ...);        // not done yet: runs in the thread that completes it -> io-pool

CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> "z", io)
        .thenApplyAsync(s -> ...);   // *Async without executor -> ForkJoinPool.commonPool-worker-N
        // .thenApplyAsync(fn, io)   // *Async with executor   -> that executor

All three results above were printed by the verification run. Rule: a non-async callback runs in whichever thread happens to complete the previous stage, or in the thread that registers it if the stage is already done. You do not control it. If a callback is slow or blocking, use the ...Async(fn, executor) variant so it does not steal time from an I/O thread (for example, a Netty event loop).

Timeouts (Java 9)

CompletableFuture<String> slowCall = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> { sleep(500); return "real"; }, io);
slowCall.completeOnTimeout("fallback", 50, TimeUnit.MILLISECONDS).join();   // "fallback"

CompletableFuture<String> slowCall2 = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> { sleep(500); return "real"; }, io);
slowCall2.orTimeout(50, TimeUnit.MILLISECONDS).join();
// throws CompletionException, cause: java.util.concurrent.TimeoutException

Both complete the future; they do not stop the underlying work. The task keeps running on its thread until it finishes, and cancel(true) on a CompletableFuture does not interrupt it either. For real cancellation, make the task check a flag or use an HTTP client timeout.

A realistic pipeline

CompletableFuture<Dashboard> dashboard =
        CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> userService.find(id), io)          // 1. user
            .thenCompose(user -> {
                var orders = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> orderService.recent(user), io);
                var recs   = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> recService.forUser(user), io);
                return orders.thenCombine(recs, (o, r) -> new Dashboard(user, o, r));  // 2. in parallel
            })
            .orTimeout(2, TimeUnit.SECONDS)                                     // 3. overall deadline
            .exceptionally(ex -> Dashboard.empty());                             // 4. degrade gracefully

Future vs CompletableFuture

Future (Java 5)CompletableFuture (Java 8)
Get the resultBlocking get() onlyget()/join(), or react with callbacks without blocking
Chain stepsNothenApply, thenCompose, …
Combine severalManual loop of get() callsthenCombine, allOf, anyOf
Error handlingExecutionException from get()exceptionally, handle, whenComplete
Complete it yourselfNocomplete, completeExceptionally
Timeoutsget(timeout) (blocks)orTimeout, completeOnTimeout (non-blocking, Java 9)

Common pitfalls

  • Calling join() too early: a.join(); b.join(); inside a loop makes the code sequential again. Start all futures first, then wait once with allOf.
  • Forgetting the returned future: cf.thenApply(...) returns a new future. Handlers attached to the old one do not see errors thrown in the new step.
  • Swallowed errors: a chain that ends in thenAccept and is never joined hides every exception. End chains with exceptionally or whenComplete that logs.
  • Blocking in the common pool: JDBC calls inside supplyAsync with no executor starve parallel streams across the JVM.
  • Deadlock by waiting on your own pool: a task in a 1-thread executor that calls join() on another task queued on the same executor waits forever.
Interview angle: "thenApply vs thenCompose?" — map vs flatMap; compose when the function returns a future. "What happens to an exception in the middle of a chain?" — later stages are skipped until exceptionally/handle. "Which pool runs supplyAsync?" — common ForkJoinPool unless you pass one; pass one for I/O. "get vs join?" — checked ExecutionException vs unchecked CompletionException.

33. Concurrent Collections

In plain words

The normal collections (HashMap, ArrayList) are not safe when several threads change them at once: updates get lost, and a HashMap can even be corrupted. java.util.concurrent has versions built for sharing: a map that lets many threads write different keys at the same time, a list that is cheap to read while others write, and queues that make threads wait politely for work.

A shared whiteboard. Collections.synchronizedMap is "one marker for the whole board": everyone queues for it. ConcurrentHashMap is a board split into many small boxes, each with its own marker: people writing in different boxes never wait for each other.

ConcurrentHashMap — how it works in Java 8+

Java 7 split the map into 16 "segments", each with its own lock. Java 8 threw that away. Now it is one array of bins (like HashMap) and:

  • Reads never lock. The table and node values are read with volatile semantics, so get sees the latest completed write.
  • Writing to an empty bin uses a single CAS (compare-and-swap, a lock-free CPU instruction) to install the first node.
  • Writing to a non-empty bin locks only that bin with synchronized on its first node. Two threads writing different bins run in parallel.
  • A bin with 8+ nodes becomes a red-black tree (TreeBin), same as HashMap.
  • Resizing is shared: threads that arrive during a resize help move bins to the new table.
  • size() sums per-thread counter cells (like LongAdder), so it is only an estimate while writes are happening.

Use the atomic methods — get-then-put is still a race

ConcurrentHashMap<String, Integer> chm = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();
ConcurrentHashMap<String, Integer> racy = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();
// 4 threads, each doing this 100,000 times:
chm.merge("hits", 1, Integer::sum);                  // atomic read-modify-write
racy.put("hits", racy.getOrDefault("hits", 0) + 1);  // two separate calls: NOT atomic

chm.get("hits");    // 400000
racy.get("hits");   // less than 400000 -- updates lost (true in every run; exact number varies)

Each single call on a ConcurrentHashMap is thread-safe, but a sequence of calls is not. Any "check then act" must be one call:

map.putIfAbsent(k, v);                                   // insert only if missing
map.computeIfAbsent(k, key -> new CopyOnWriteArrayList<>()).add(item);  // lazy create, once
map.compute(k, (key, old) -> old == null ? 1 : old + 1); // full control
map.merge(k, 1, Integer::sum);                           // counting
map.replace(k, expectedOld, newValue);                   // CAS-style update
// Bulk ops (Java 8) take a parallelism threshold:
long total = map.reduceValuesToLong(1, Integer::longValue, 0L, Long::sum);

The function passed to compute/merge runs while the bin is locked: keep it short, and never modify the same map from inside it (Java 9+ can throw IllegalStateException: Recursive update).

No null keys or values

chm.put("k", null);   // NullPointerException
chm.put(null, 1);     // NullPointerException
new HashMap<>().put(null, null);   // fine

Why? In a concurrent map, get(k) == null must mean "absent". If nulls were allowed you could not tell "absent" from "present with value null", and you cannot fix that with a follow-up containsKey because another thread may change the map in between.

Collections.synchronizedMap vs ConcurrentHashMap vs Hashtable

HashtableCollections.synchronizedMapConcurrentHashMap
LockingEvery method synchronized on the tableEvery method synchronized on one mutexCAS + per-bin lock; reads lock-free
Concurrent writers1 at a time1 at a timeMany (different bins)
IterationFail-fast; you must lock manuallyFail-fast; you must hold synchronized (map) while iteratingWeakly consistent: never throws ConcurrentModificationException, may or may not show recent changes
NullsNoWhatever the wrapped map allowsNo
StatusLegacy — do not useFine for low contention or wrapping a TreeMapThe default choice
Map<String, Integer> sync = Collections.synchronizedMap(new HashMap<>());
synchronized (sync) {                        // required, or iteration can throw CME
    for (String k : sync.keySet()) { ... }
}
for (Integer k : chm.keySet()) chm.put(k + 10, 0);   // ConcurrentHashMap: no exception

Sorted and concurrent: ConcurrentSkipListMap / ConcurrentSkipListSet (O(log n), lock-free).

CopyOnWriteArrayList

Every write (add, set, remove) copies the whole internal array and swaps in the new one. Readers and iterators work on the old array — a snapshot — with no locking.

List<String> cow = new CopyOnWriteArrayList<>(List.of("a", "b"));
for (String s : cow) cow.add(s + "!");   // iterator sees the snapshot [a, b]
System.out.println(cow);                 // [a, b, a!, b!]  -- no exception, no infinite loop

List<String> al = new ArrayList<>(List.of("a", "b"));
for (String s : al) al.add(s);           // ConcurrentModificationException

Perfect for read-mostly lists: event listeners, config, routing tables. Terrible for write-heavy lists: each add is O(n) and creates garbage. The iterator does not support remove.

BlockingQueue and producer-consumer

A BlockingQueue adds methods that wait: put blocks while the queue is full, take blocks while it is empty. This is the safe way to hand work from one thread to another, and exactly what ThreadPoolExecutor uses inside.

ActionThrowsReturns special valueBlocksTimes out
Insertadd(e)offer(e) → falseput(e)offer(e, time, unit)
Removeremove()poll() → nulltake()poll(time, unit)
Lookelement()peek()——
private static final int POISON = -1;                 // "no more work" signal
BlockingQueue<Integer> queue = new ArrayBlockingQueue<>(3);   // bounded = back-pressure

Thread producer = new Thread(() -> {
    try {
        for (int i = 1; i <= 5; i++) queue.put(i);    // waits if 3 items are already queued
        queue.put(POISON);
    } catch (InterruptedException e) { Thread.currentThread().interrupt(); }
});
Thread consumer = new Thread(() -> {
    try {
        int sum = 0;
        while (true) {
            int item = queue.take();                  // waits if empty
            if (item == POISON) break;
            System.out.println("consumed " + item);
            sum += item;
        }
        System.out.println("sum = " + sum);
    } catch (InterruptedException e) { Thread.currentThread().interrupt(); }
});
producer.start(); consumer.start();
producer.join(); consumer.join();
// consumed 1 ... consumed 5, then: sum = 15   (FIFO order, so the output is deterministic)
QueueBounded?InsideNotes
ArrayBlockingQueueYes (fixed at creation)Array, one lock for put and takePredictable memory; optional fairness
LinkedBlockingQueueOptional (default Integer.MAX_VALUE)Linked nodes, two locks (put lock, take lock)Higher throughput; always pass a capacity
PriorityBlockingQueueNoHeapTakes the smallest element first
DelayQueueNoHeap by delayElement available only after its delay expires
SynchronousQueueCapacity 0Direct hand-offEach put waits for a take (cached thread pool)
ConcurrentLinkedQueueNoLock-free linked list (CAS)Not blocking: poll() returns null when empty; great for many threads, non-blocking use

Synchronizers: CountDownLatch, CyclicBarrier, Semaphore

// CountDownLatch: "wait until N things have happened" -- ONE-SHOT
CountDownLatch ready = new CountDownLatch(3);
for (String svc : List.of("db", "cache", "queue")) new Thread(ready::countDown).start();
ready.await();                 // main continues when the count hits 0

// CyclicBarrier: "N threads wait for EACH OTHER", then all go on -- REUSABLE
CyclicBarrier barrier = new CyclicBarrier(3, () -> System.out.println("phase finished by all"));
// each of 3 workers: for (int p = 0; p < 2; p++) barrier.await();
// prints "phase finished by all" twice: once per phase

// Semaphore: "at most N at a time" -- a pool of permits
Semaphore permits = new Semaphore(2);
permits.acquire();
try { callRateLimitedApi(); }
finally { permits.release(); }  // 6 tasks on 6 threads: max inside at once = 2 (verified)
CountDownLatchCyclicBarrierSemaphore
Who waitsOne or more "waiters" for other threads' eventsThe worker threads wait for each otherThreads wanting a permit
ReusableNo (count only goes down)Yes (resets after each trip)Yes
Typical useStart-up: wait for services; tests: wait for N callbacksPhased simulations, parallel algorithms in roundsLimit concurrent DB connections or API calls

Also know: Phaser (a flexible, reusable barrier with a changing number of parties) and Exchanger (two threads swap objects).

Interview angle: "How is ConcurrentHashMap thread-safe without locking the whole map?" — CAS on empty bins, synchronized on a bin's head node, volatile reads. "Why no nulls?" — ambiguity of get returning null. "Is if (!map.containsKey(k)) map.put(k, v) safe on a ConcurrentHashMap?" — no, use putIfAbsent/computeIfAbsent. "Write a producer-consumer" — bounded BlockingQueue + poison pill.

34. Virtual Threads & Deadlocks

In plain words

A normal Java thread (a platform thread) is a thin wrapper around an operating-system thread: expensive to create, about 1 MB of stack, and you can have only a few thousand. A virtual thread (final in Java 21) is a thread managed by the JVM itself. Millions can exist. When one waits for I/O, the JVM parks it on the heap and uses the real OS thread for another virtual thread.

A deadlock is when two or more threads each hold something the other needs, so all of them wait forever.

Virtual threads are like a restaurant where waiters (OS threads) never stand by a table waiting for the diner to decide — they take an order, move on, and come back when called. Deadlock is two people in a narrow hallway, each politely waiting for the other to step aside first.

Platform vs virtual threads

Using them

Thread vt = Thread.ofVirtual().name("my-virtual").start(() ->
        System.out.println("isVirtual=" + Thread.currentThread().isVirtual()));   // isVirtual=true
Thread.startVirtualThread(() -> work());              // shortest form
vt.isDaemon();                                        // true -- always daemon

// 10,000 tasks that each block for 1 second
try (ExecutorService ex = Executors.newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor()) {
    IntStream.range(0, 10_000).forEach(i -> ex.submit(() -> {
        Thread.sleep(Duration.ofSeconds(1));          // virtual thread unmounts while sleeping
        return i;
    }));
}   // close() waits for all tasks
// Verified run: finished in 1407 ms. A fixed pool of 100 platform threads needs 100 rounds = ~100 s.

The model is "one virtual thread per task". Do not pool virtual threads — they are cheap to create and pooling defeats the purpose. To limit load on a downstream system, use a Semaphore, not a small pool.

When they help and when they don't

WorkloadVirtual threads?Why
Web server, many requests each waiting on DB/HTTP (thread-per-request)Yes — the main use caseThroughput limited by how many requests can wait at once, not by CPU
CPU-bound (image resize, crypto, number crunching)No benefitOnly N cores exist; a pool of about N platform threads is just as good
Heavy ThreadLocal caches (e.g. per-thread buffers)CarefulA million threads = a million copies. Java 21 preview: ScopedValue
Long blocking inside synchronized (Java 21–23)Careful: pinningSee below

Virtual threads make blocking code cheap, not faster: a single request still takes as long. The win is that you can write simple blocking code instead of reactive/callback code and still handle huge concurrency.

Pinning

In Java 21, a virtual thread that blocks while inside a synchronized block (or a native method) cannot unmount — it pins its carrier thread. With only one carrier per core, a few pinned threads can stall the whole application.

synchronized (LOCK) {
    Thread.sleep(50);    // or a JDBC call -- pins the carrier in Java 21
}
// Run with -Djdk.tracePinnedThreads=short to detect it. Verified output:
// VirtualThread[#20]/runnable@ForkJoinPool-1-worker-1 reason:MONITOR
//     PinDemo.lambda$main$0(PinDemo.java:6) <== monitors:1

// Fix for Java 21: use a ReentrantLock, which virtual threads can unmount from
private final ReentrantLock lock = new ReentrantLock();
lock.lock();
try { callDatabase(); } finally { lock.unlock(); }

Java 24 (JEP 491) removed pinning for synchronized; on Java 21 it still matters, and interviewers like to ask.

Deadlock: the four conditions (Coffman)

A deadlock is possible only if all four hold. Break any one and it cannot happen.

ConditionMeaningHow to break it
Mutual exclusionA resource can be held by one thread at a timeUse lock-free or immutable data where possible
Hold and waitA thread holds one lock while waiting for anotherAcquire all locks at once, or release before waiting
No preemptionA lock cannot be taken away; only the owner releases ittryLock with timeout: give up and release what you hold
Circular waitT1 waits for T2, T2 waits for T1 (a cycle)Global lock ordering: everyone takes locks in the same order
t1 holds A ----wants----> B ^ | | held by held by | | v A <----wants---- t2 holds B cycle = deadlock

A deadlock you can reproduce every time

static final Object A = new Object();
static final Object B = new Object();
CountDownLatch bothHoldFirstLock = new CountDownLatch(2);   // forces the bad timing

Thread t1 = new Thread(() -> {
    synchronized (A) {
        bothHoldFirstLock.countDown(); await(bothHoldFirstLock);
        synchronized (B) { System.out.println("t1 got both"); }   // never printed
    }
}, "t1");
Thread t2 = new Thread(() -> {
    synchronized (B) {                                          // OPPOSITE order
        bothHoldFirstLock.countDown(); await(bothHoldFirstLock);
        synchronized (A) { System.out.println("t2 got both"); }   // never printed
    }
}, "t2");
t1.start(); t2.start();
Thread.sleep(300);

// Detect it from inside the JVM -- the same check jstack runs
long[] ids = ManagementFactory.getThreadMXBean().findDeadlockedThreads();
// deadlocked threads: 2
// t1 state=BLOCKED waiting for lock owned by t2
// t2 state=BLOCKED waiting for lock owned by t1

Without the latch it is timing-dependent (it may run fine 999 times), which is exactly why deadlocks escape testing.

Fixes

// Fix 1: lock ordering -- always lock the lower id first
void transfer(Account from, Account to, long amount) {
    Account first  = from.id() < to.id() ? from : to;
    Account second = first == from ? to : from;
    synchronized (first) {
        synchronized (second) {
            from.withdraw(amount);
            to.deposit(amount);
        }
    }
}

// Fix 2: tryLock with timeout -- back off instead of waiting forever
static void transfer(Lock first, Lock second) throws InterruptedException {
    while (true) {
        if (first.tryLock(50, TimeUnit.MILLISECONDS)) {
            try {
                if (second.tryLock(50, TimeUnit.MILLISECONDS)) {
                    try { doWork(); return; }
                    finally { second.unlock(); }
                }
            } finally { first.unlock(); }
        }
        Thread.sleep(ThreadLocalRandom.current().nextInt(10));   // random back-off
    }
}
// Two threads calling this with the locks in OPPOSITE order: finishes, no deadlock (verified)

Other fixes: hold locks for as short a time as possible, never call unknown code (listeners, callbacks) while holding a lock, and prefer higher-level tools (ConcurrentHashMap, queues) over nested locks.

Livelock and starvation

  • Livelock: threads are not blocked — they keep running — but make no progress, because each keeps reacting to the other. Two tryLock loops that back off and retry with the same fixed delay can keep colliding forever. Fix: random back-off (as above).
  • Starvation: one thread never gets the resource because others always win — e.g. a greedy thread re-grabbing an unfair lock, or low-priority tasks behind an endless stream of high-priority ones. Fix: fair locks (new ReentrantLock(true)), bounded work per turn.

Detecting a deadlock in production: jstack

$ jps                      # find the process id
$ jstack <pid>             # or: jcmd <pid> Thread.print
Found one Java-level deadlock:
=============================
"t1":
  waiting to lock monitor 0x000000013210fdd0 (object 0x0000000787f14ac8, a java.lang.Object),
  which is held by "t2"

"t2":
  waiting to lock monitor 0x0000000132117100 (object 0x0000000787f14ab8, a java.lang.Object),
  which is held by "t1"

That is real output from a program that deadlocks (addresses differ per run). Look for threads in state BLOCKED (waiting for a monitor) or WAITING on a lock. Take 2–3 dumps a few seconds apart: a thread stuck on the same line in every dump is your suspect. VisualVM and JDK Mission Control also have a "detect deadlock" button.

Interview angle: "Virtual vs platform threads?" — JVM-managed, cheap, unmount on blocking; for I/O-bound concurrency, not CPU work; don't pool them. "What is pinning?" — blocking inside synchronized keeps the carrier busy (Java 21). "Four conditions of deadlock and how to prevent it?" — break circular wait with lock ordering, or no-preemption with tryLock. "How do you find one in prod?" — jstack / jcmd Thread.print.
Part 6 — The JVM Inside

35. JVM Memory: Heap, Stack & Metaspace

In plain words

When a Java program runs, the JVM splits its memory into areas with different jobs. The heap holds every object you create with new, and is shared by all threads. Each thread also gets its own stack, which holds one frame per method call currently running (its local variables and parameters). Metaspace holds the description of each loaded class: its methods, fields and bytecode.

An office. The heap is the shared filing room — everyone can reach any folder, and a cleaner (the garbage collector) removes folders nobody points to. Each worker's stack is the pile of sticky notes on their own desk: the top note is the task they are on now, and it is thrown away the moment the task ends. Metaspace is the company handbook describing how each kind of form works.

The runtime data areas

What lives where

AreaHoldsShared?Error when it runs out
HeapAll objects and arrays, including their instance fields; the String pool (since Java 7); static fields (stored with the Class object, since Java 8)All threadsOutOfMemoryError: Java heap space
JVM stackOne frame per active call: local variables (primitives and references), operand stack, return addressPer threadStackOverflowError
Metaspace (Java 8+, replaced PermGen)Class metadata: method bytecode, field and method descriptions, runtime constant poolAll threadsOutOfMemoryError: Metaspace
Code cacheNative code produced by the JIT compilerAll threadsJIT stops compiling ("CodeCache is full" warning)
PC registerAddress of the bytecode instruction the thread is executingPer thread—
Native method stackFrames for native (C/C++) code called through JNIPer threadStackOverflowError

Tracing one method call

void process() {
    int count = 3;                       // primitive: value lives IN the stack frame
    String name = "report";              // reference in the frame -> String object in the heap (pool)
    List<Integer> ids = new ArrayList<>(); // reference in the frame -> ArrayList object in the heap
    ids.add(count);                      // Integer object on the heap, referenced by the list's array
}   // frame popped: count, name, ids disappear; the ArrayList is now unreachable -> garbage
STACK (thread main) HEAP +-----------------------+ | process() | | count = 3 | | name = ref --------+-----> "report" (String) | ids = ref --------+-----> ArrayList --> Object[] --> Integer(3) +-----------------------+ | main() | | args = ref --------+-----> String[0] +-----------------------+

"Primitives live on the stack, objects live on the heap" is the right first answer, with two refinements: a primitive field of an object lives inside that object on the heap; and the JIT's escape analysis may avoid allocating an object on the heap at all if it never leaves the method (it replaces it with local scalars).

How an object is allocated

  • Each thread gets a private chunk of Eden called a TLAB (thread-local allocation buffer). Allocating is just "bump a pointer" inside the thread's own TLAB — no locking, a few machine instructions. That is why new in Java is very cheap.
  • Every object has a header (mark word for hash code, lock state and GC age, plus a pointer to its class): 12 bytes on a 64-bit JVM with compressed pointers, and objects are padded to multiples of 8 bytes. So an empty new Object() is 16 bytes and an Integer is 16 bytes to hold 4 bytes of data.
  • Very large objects (bigger than half a G1 region) go straight into special humongous regions instead of Eden.
  • Escape analysis: if the JIT proves an object never leaves the method, it can replace it with plain local variables (scalar replacement) — no heap allocation at all.

StackOverflowError vs OutOfMemoryError

static int depth = 0;
static void recurse() { depth++; recurse(); }   // no base case

try { recurse(); }
catch (StackOverflowError e) { System.out.println("StackOverflowError after ~" + (depth / 1000) * 1000 + " calls"); }

List<long[]> hog = new ArrayList<>();
try { while (true) hog.add(new long[1_000_000]); }   // 8 MB each, all still reachable
catch (OutOfMemoryError e) { hog = null; System.out.println(e.getMessage()); }

try { int[] huge = new int[Integer.MAX_VALUE]; }
catch (OutOfMemoryError e) { System.out.println(e.getMessage()); }

Verified with java -Xmx64m -Xss512k MemDemo:

StackOverflowError after ~6000 calls        (with -Xss2m: ~45000; varies run to run)
OutOfMemoryError: Java heap space after 7 blocks
OutOfMemoryError: Requested array size exceeds VM limit
ErrorTypical causeFirst fix
StackOverflowErrorInfinite or very deep recursion; toString/equals/hashCode that call each other on a cycle (bidirectional JPA relations)Fix the base case, make it iterative; raise -Xss only if the depth is legitimate
OOM: Java heap spaceMemory leak, or genuinely too much data loaded at onceHeap dump, find what holds the objects (s38); stream data instead of loading it all
OOM: GC overhead limit exceededMore than 98% of time spent in GC recovering under 2% of the heap (Parallel GC)Same as heap space — usually a leak
OOM: MetaspaceToo many classes: class loader leak on hot redeploy, runaway proxy/lambda generationFind the loader that is never released; set -XX:MaxMetaspaceSize to fail early
OOM: unable to create native threadToo many platform threads (OS limit or no memory for stacks)Bounded pools or virtual threads
OOM: Direct buffer memoryByteBuffer.allocateDirect outside the heap (Netty, NIO)Release buffers; -XX:MaxDirectMemorySize
OOM: Requested array size exceeds VM limitAsked for an array close to Integer.MAX_VALUE elementsA bug in size calculation

Both are Errors, not Exceptions: you normally don't catch them. After an OOM the application may be in a broken state; let it crash, collect the heap dump, and restart.

The flags you should know

FlagMeaningExample
-XmsInitial heap size-Xms2g
-XmxMaximum heap size (default: 1/4 of physical RAM)-Xmx2g
-XssStack size per thread (default 1 MB on Linux x64; 2 MB on macOS Apple Silicon, checked with -XX:+PrintFlagsFinal)-Xss512k
-XmnYoung generation size-Xmn512m
-XX:MaxMetaspaceSizeCap metaspace (default: unlimited)-XX:MaxMetaspaceSize=256m
-XX:MaxRAMPercentageHeap as % of container memory — use in Docker/Kubernetes instead of a fixed -Xmx-XX:MaxRAMPercentage=75
-XX:+HeapDumpOnOutOfMemoryErrorWrite a heap dump when OOM happenswith -XX:HeapDumpPath=/dumps

In servers, set -Xms equal to -Xmx so the heap never has to grow or shrink at runtime. Remember that the Java process uses more than -Xmx: metaspace, thread stacks, code cache, direct buffers and the GC's own structures all live outside the heap. Leave headroom in a container or the OS will kill the process (Kubernetes OOMKilled) without any Java error.

Interview angle: "Where are local variables, objects, static fields stored?" — locals in the stack frame (references point to the heap), objects on the heap, static fields on the heap with the class mirror (not in PermGen/metaspace since Java 8). "PermGen vs Metaspace?" — PermGen was a fixed-size heap region (Java 7 and earlier, OOM: PermGen space); Metaspace is native memory that grows by default. "Is the stack thread-safe?" — yes, each thread has its own, so local variables are never shared.

36. Garbage Collection

In plain words

In Java you never free memory yourself. The garbage collector (GC) finds objects that the program can no longer reach and reuses their memory. "Reachable" means: you can get to the object by following references starting from a GC root — a local variable in a running method, a static field, an active thread, and so on. Everything else is garbage, even if it still points to other objects.

Balloons tied with strings. Hold a string (a root) and that balloon stays, along with any balloons tied to it. Cut the last string and it floats away — it doesn't matter that it is still tied to other loose balloons.

Reachability and GC roots

GC roots reachable (kept) unreachable (garbage) local variables in live frames ---> Order ---> Customer static fields ---> CACHE map ---> Session Cart <---> Item active threads ---> Thread object (a cycle, but no path JNI references, monitors held from any root: collected)

Because the JVM traces from roots, reference cycles are not a problem (unlike simple reference counting in, say, older Python or Objective-C). And System.gc() is only a hint; finalize() is deprecated (Java 9) and should never be used — use try-with-resources or Cleaner.

static class Node { Node other; }
Node a = new Node(), b = new Node();
a.other = b; b.other = a;                          // a cycle
WeakReference<Node> watchA = new WeakReference<>(a);   // watch without keeping it alive
a = null; b = null;                                // no root reaches the cycle any more
System.gc();
watchA.get() == null;                              // true -- the cycle was collected (verified)

The generational hypothesis

Studies of real programs show that most objects die young (temporary strings, iterators, request objects), and the few that survive a while tend to live a long time. So the heap is split into generations and the young one is collected often and cheaply.

The young collection is a copying collector: it copies the few live objects out and treats the whole of Eden as empty afterwards. Its cost depends on the number of live objects, not the amount of garbage — which is why short-lived garbage is almost free. The verified log below (G1, -Xmx64m -Xlog:gc, a loop allocating 200,000 temporary 1 KB arrays and keeping 200) shows each young GC shrinking the used heap from 38 MB to 1 MB in under a millisecond:

[0.005s][info][gc] Using G1
[0.040s][info][gc] GC(0) Pause Young (Normal) (G1 Evacuation Pause) 24M->1M(64M) 0.888ms
[0.050s][info][gc] GC(1) Pause Young (Normal) (G1 Evacuation Pause) 38M->1M(64M) 0.396ms
[0.056s][info][gc] GC(2) Pause Young (Normal) (G1 Evacuation Pause) 38M->1M(64M) 0.493ms

Minor, major, full

KindWhat it collectsFrequency / cost
Minor (young) GCYoung generation onlyVery often; short pause (ms)
Major GCOld generation (in G1: "mixed" collections take young + some old regions)Less often; can be concurrent
Full GCWhole heap (+ metaspace), usually stop-the-world and single-pass compactingRare; long pause — a warning sign in G1/ZGC

The basic algorithm: mark, sweep, compact

heap before: [A][x][B][x][x][C][x] x = garbage 1. mark: trace from roots, flag A, B, C as live 2. sweep: [A][ ][B][ ][ ][C][ ] free the rest -- but memory is now FRAGMENTED 3. compact: [A][B][C][ ] slide live objects together, update references -- new objects get fast "bump the pointer" allocation

Copying (used for the young generation) combines sweep and compact: copy live objects to an empty space and forget the old one. Fast when few survive, but needs spare space.

Stop-the-world

A stop-the-world (STW) pause is when the GC freezes all application threads (at "safepoints") so the object graph cannot change under it. Every HotSpot collector has some STW phases; modern ones do most of the work concurrently with the application and keep pauses short. Throughput (total work done) and latency (length of pauses) pull against each other: that trade-off is the whole reason for having several collectors.

The collectors

CollectorFlagHowPausesBest for
Serial-XX:+UseSerialGCOne thread, generational, STW for everythingLong on big heapsSmall heaps, 1 CPU containers, CLI tools
Parallel (throughput)-XX:+UseParallelGCMany threads, STW, generationalHundreds of ms+ on big heapsBatch jobs where total throughput matters, not pauses. Default in Java 8
G1 (Garbage First)-XX:+UseG1GCHeap split into equal regions (1–32 MB, picked from heap size); concurrent marking; collects the regions with the most garbage first; aims for a pause targetTarget -XX:MaxGCPauseMillis=200 (default)General server apps. Default since Java 9 (verified: Java 21 here prints -XX:+UseG1GC)
ZGC-XX:+UseZGC (production 15; generational in 21 with -XX:+ZGenerational, default mode in 23)Almost everything concurrent, including moving objects (coloured pointers + load barriers)Under 1 ms, independent of heap sizeLarge heaps (GBs to TBs), latency-sensitive services
Shenandoah-XX:+UseShenandoahGC (production 15; in OpenJDK builds, not Oracle JDK)Concurrent compaction (Brooks pointers / load barriers)A few ms, independent of heap sizeSame niche as ZGC; Red Hat builds
Epsilon-XX:+UseEpsilonGC (experimental)Allocates, never collectsNonePerformance tests, very short-lived jobs

CMS (Concurrent Mark Sweep) was deprecated in Java 9 and removed in Java 14 — if you see it in old configs, it needs replacing with G1 or ZGC.

G1 in a little more depth

G1 heap = ~2048 equal regions, each labelled on the fly: [E][E][O][ ][S][O][H][H][E][ ][O][E][ ][O] E = eden S = survivor O = old H = humongous (huge object) [ ] = free young GC: evacuate all E + S regions (STW, parallel) -> copies live objects to free regions concurrent mark: when old occupancy passes ~45%, trace the old regions while the app runs mixed GC: evacuate young regions + the old regions with the MOST garbage ("garbage first")

Because it cleans a few regions at a time and chooses how many from its pause target, G1 keeps pauses roughly predictable without compacting the whole heap. A full GC in G1 means it fell behind (allocation faster than concurrent marking could keep up, or no free regions for evacuation) — treat it as a signal to grow the heap or fix allocation.

Tuning basics

  • Start with defaults. Set heap size (-Xms=-Xmx, or MaxRAMPercentage in containers) and choose the collector. Most apps need nothing else.
  • Turn on GC logging (Java 9+ unified logging): -Xlog:gc*:file=gc.log:time,uptime:filecount=5,filesize=20m. Analyze with GCeasy or GCViewer.
  • Pauses too long on G1? Try lowering -XX:MaxGCPauseMillis, or move to ZGC. Frequent full GCs? Usually the heap is too small or there is a leak — tuning flags won't fix a leak.
  • Allocate less: the cheapest GC is the one that doesn't happen. Avoid boxing in hot loops, reuse builders, stream instead of loading everything.

Reference types

Object strong = new Object();                                  // normal reference
WeakReference<Object> weak = new WeakReference<>(new Object()); // nothing else points to it
WeakReference<Object> weakButHeld = new WeakReference<>(strong);
SoftReference<byte[]> soft = new SoftReference<>(new byte[1024]);
ReferenceQueue<Object> queue = new ReferenceQueue<>();
PhantomReference<Object> phantom = new PhantomReference<>(new Object(), queue);

System.gc();
weak.get() == null;          // true  -- cleared at the first GC
weakButHeld.get() == null;   // false -- still strongly reachable through 'strong'
soft.get() == null;          // false -- kept while memory is not low
phantom.get() == null;       // true  -- ALWAYS null; only tells you "it was collected"
queue.remove(1000) == phantom;   // true -- enqueued after collection

Map<Object, String> cache = new WeakHashMap<>();
Object key = new Object();
cache.put(key, "metadata");
key = null; System.gc();
cache.size();                // 0 -- entry vanished with its key

The results above are from a verified run (with G1, where System.gc() triggers a full collection; the spec does not guarantee it, so never rely on System.gc() in real code).

TypeCollected whenget()Used for
StrongNever, while reachable—Everything normal
SoftReferenceOnly when the JVM is low on memory (before throwing OOM)Object until clearedMemory-sensitive caches (but a real cache like Caffeine is better)
WeakReferenceAt the next GC once no strong/soft refs remainObject until clearedWeakHashMap, canonicalizing maps, listener registries that shouldn't keep listeners alive; ThreadLocalMap keys
PhantomReferenceAfter the object is finalized/collected; enqueued on a ReferenceQueueAlways nullCleanup of native resources after the object is gone (java.lang.ref.Cleaner, Java 9, is built on it)
Interview angle: "How does the GC decide an object is garbage?" — not reachable from GC roots; cycles are fine. "Why generations?" — most objects die young, so collecting the young space is cheap. "What is the default GC and why?" — G1 since Java 9: balanced throughput and predictable pauses via regions. "When would you use ZGC?" — big heaps and sub-millisecond pause needs. "Weak vs soft reference?" — weak: cleared at the next GC; soft: cleared only under memory pressure. "Can you force GC?" — no, System.gc() is a request.

37. Class Loading

In plain words

The JVM does not load all classes at start-up. It loads each class lazily, the first time it is really needed: it finds the .class bytes (in a JAR, a folder, over the network), checks them, and prepares them. Objects called class loaders do the finding, and they are arranged in a chain: each loader first asks its parent, so core classes like String always come from the JDK and can't be faked by your own classpath.

A library request desk. You ask your local branch for a book; the branch first asks the central library. Only if the central library doesn't have it does the branch look on its own shelves. That way everyone gets the one official copy of the classics.

Three phases: loading, linking, initialization

LOADING LINKING INITIALIZATION find .class --> verify --> prepare --> resolve --> run static initializers bytes, create bytecode is allocate static symbolic and static field Class object safe/valid fields, set to refs -> real assignments, in textual in metaspace DEFAULTS (0/null) (often lazy) order: <clinit>
  • Loading: a class loader reads the bytes and creates a Class object. Metadata goes to metaspace.
  • Verification: the bytecode verifier checks types, stack use and jumps are legal. This is why a hand-crafted .class file cannot corrupt the JVM.
  • Preparation: static fields get memory and default values (static int x = 5; is 0 here).
  • Resolution: symbolic names in the constant pool ("java/util/List") are turned into direct references, usually lazily when first used.
  • Initialization: the compiler-generated <clinit> method runs — all static field initializers and static { } blocks, top to bottom. Now x becomes 5. The JVM guarantees this runs once, under a lock, so it is thread-safe.

The loaders and parent delegation

String.class.getClassLoader();                          // null      (bootstrap is native code)
java.sql.Connection.class.getClassLoader().getName();   // "platform"
LoaderDemo.class.getClassLoader().getName();            // "app"
app.getParent().getName();                              // "platform"
app.getParent().getParent();                            // null      (bootstrap)

(All verified on Java 21.) Before Java 9 the middle loader was the extension loader reading jre/lib/ext; the module system replaced it with the platform loader.

Why delegation? (1) Security: your own java.lang.String on the classpath is never used — the bootstrap loader already provided the real one (and the JVM refuses to define classes in java.* packages from other loaders). (2) No duplicates: a class is loaded once by the highest loader that can see it. (3) A class's identity is its name + its loader: the same .class loaded by two loaders gives two different classes, and casting between them throws ClassCastException (a classic app-server puzzle: "cannot cast com.x.Foo to com.x.Foo").

Some systems deliberately break delegation: Tomcat's web-app loaders look in the WAR first (so each app can have its own library versions), and JDBC's ServiceLoader uses the thread context class loader so JDK code can load driver classes from your classpath.

When does a static initializer run? (verified)

static class Config {
    static final int MAX = 10;               // compile-time constant: copied into the caller
    static int counter = 5;                  // NOT a constant
    static { System.out.println("  [Config static block runs]"); }
    static void hello() { System.out.println("  Config.hello()"); }
}
static class Parent { static { System.out.println("  [Parent init]"); } static int p = 1; }
static class Child extends Parent { static { System.out.println("  [Child init]"); } static int c = 2; }

System.out.println("1. read constant MAX = " + Config.MAX);   // no init
System.out.println("2. declare array");
Config[] arr = new Config[3];                                 // no init
System.out.println("3. Config.class literal: " + Config.class.getSimpleName());  // no init
System.out.println("4. read non-constant counter");
int x = Config.counter;                                       // init happens HERE
System.out.println("5. call static method");
Config.hello();                                               // no second init
System.out.println("6. Child.p (field declared in Parent)");
int y = Child.p;                                              // only Parent is initialised
System.out.println("7. new Child()");
new Child();
System.out.println("8. Class.forName initialises by default");
Class.forName("InitDemo$Lazy");
1. read constant MAX = 10
2. declare array
3. Config.class literal: Config
4. read non-constant counter
  [Config static block runs]
5. call static method
  Config.hello()
6. Child.p (field declared in Parent)
  [Parent init]
7. new Child()
  [Child init]
8. Class.forName initialises by default
  [Lazy init]

A class is initialized on its first active use: new, calling a static method, reading/writing a non-constant static field, initializing a subclass (the parent goes first), reflection like Class.forName(name), or being the main class. It is not initialized by reading a compile-time static final constant (inlined by javac), creating an array of it, Foo.class, or accessing a parent's static field through a child. Class.forName(name, false, loader) loads without initializing.

This laziness is what makes the holder idiom singleton work: the inner Holder class, and therefore the instance, is created only when getInstance() first touches it, and the JVM's init lock makes it thread-safe with no synchronized of your own.

public final class Registry {
    private Registry() {}
    private static class Holder { static final Registry INSTANCE = new Registry(); }
    public static Registry getInstance() { return Holder.INSTANCE; }
}

Writing a custom class loader

Override findClass, not loadClass. The inherited loadClass already does the delegation (check cache, ask parent, then call your findClass), so you keep the safety guarantees for free.

static class DirLoader extends ClassLoader {
    private final Path dir;
    DirLoader(Path dir, ClassLoader parent) { super(parent); this.dir = dir; }

    @Override
    protected Class<?> findClass(String name) throws ClassNotFoundException {
        // called only after the parent chain failed
        Path file = dir.resolve(name.replace('.', '/') + ".class");
        try {
            byte[] bytes = Files.readAllBytes(file);
            return defineClass(name, bytes, 0, bytes.length);
        } catch (IOException e) {
            throw new ClassNotFoundException(name, e);
        }
    }
}

DirLoader l1 = new DirLoader(Path.of("plugins"), parent);
DirLoader l2 = new DirLoader(Path.of("plugins"), parent);
Class<?> c1 = l1.loadClass("Plugin");
Class<?> c2 = l2.loadClass("Plugin");
c1.getName().equals(c2.getName());          // true
c1 == c2;                                   // false -- different loaders, different classes
l1.loadClass("java.lang.String").getClassLoader() == null;   // true -- still delegated
c2.cast(c1.getDeclaredConstructor().newInstance());
// ClassCastException: Cannot cast Plugin to Plugin

This is how plugin systems, app servers and hot reload work: drop the loader (and every object from its classes), and the classes can be unloaded and reloaded fresh. Keep even one reference to an old object and the whole loader stays — the class loader leak from s38.

ClassNotFoundException vs NoClassDefFoundError

ClassNotFoundExceptionNoClassDefFoundError
KindChecked exceptionError (a LinkageError)
WhenYou load a class by name at runtime: Class.forName, loadClass, ClassLoader.findSystemClassThe class was there at compile time but the JVM cannot get it at run time
Typical causeTypo in the name, missing JDBC driver JAR, plugin not on classpathJAR missing from the runtime classpath, or the class's static initializer failed earlier
Class.forName("com.example.DoesNotExist");
// ClassNotFoundException: com.example.DoesNotExist

// Compiled UsesHelper + Helper, then deleted Helper.class:
// Exception in thread "main" java.lang.NoClassDefFoundError: Helper
//     at UsesHelper.main(UsesHelper.java:2)
// Caused by: java.lang.ClassNotFoundException: Helper

static class Broken {
    static int value = Integer.parseInt("not a number");   // static init throws
}
// first access:  ExceptionInInitializerError
// second access: NoClassDefFoundError   (the class is marked unusable forever)

The last case is a favourite trap: the log shows NoClassDefFoundError for a class that is clearly in the JAR. Scroll up to the first error — the real cause is the ExceptionInInitializerError from its static block.

Interview angle: "Explain parent delegation and why it exists" — ask parent first; security and uniqueness. "When is a static block executed?" — on first active use, once, thread-safely; not for compile-time constants. "Can two classes with the same name coexist?" — yes, in different loaders; they are different types. "CNFE vs NCDFE?" — dynamic lookup failed vs compiled-against class missing (or failed init) at runtime.

38. Performance, Profiling & Memory Leaks

In plain words

Java has a garbage collector, but it can still leak memory: a leak is an object you no longer need that is still reachable, usually because something long-lived (a static map, a listener list, a thread) keeps a reference to it. The GC only removes unreachable objects, so these pile up until OutOfMemoryError. Finding and fixing that — and slow code in general — is done with measurement tools, not guessing.

A coat check that hands out tickets but never takes coats back when people leave. Nothing is "lost" — every coat is neatly hung and tagged — but the room fills up anyway.

Common leak causes

CauseWhat happensFix
Static collections used as cachesstatic Map gets entries on every request, never removedBounded cache with eviction (Caffeine), TTL, or remove explicitly
Listeners / callbacks never removedAn event bus holds every registered listener, and each listener holds its whole screen/object graphUnregister in a close()/lifecycle hook; weak listener lists
ThreadLocal in thread poolsPool threads live forever, so their ThreadLocal values do too — and they leak into the next requestremove() in finally
Unclosed resourcesConnections, streams, statements hold native memory and handlestry-with-resources
Inner class / lambda referencesA non-static inner class (and an anonymous class) keeps a hidden reference to its outer instance; if the inner object outlives it, the outer object can't be collectedUse static nested classes for long-lived callbacks/tasks
Wrong equals/hashCode on keysMutable key or missing hashCode: put keeps adding "new" entries, remove never finds themImmutable keys (records) with proper equals/hashCode
Class loader leaksAfter a hot redeploy, one reference from a JDK/static object to the old app keeps every old class alive → OOM: MetaspaceClear drivers, threads and ThreadLocals on undeploy

The ThreadLocal leak you can see

static final ThreadLocal<String> CURRENT_USER = new ThreadLocal<>();
ExecutorService pool = Executors.newSingleThreadExecutor();     // ONE reused thread

pool.submit(() -> CURRENT_USER.set("alice")).get();            // request 1 forgets to clean up
pool.submit(() -> System.out.println("request 2 sees user = " + CURRENT_USER.get())).get();
// request 2 sees user = alice        -- a leak AND a security bug

pool.submit(() -> {
    CURRENT_USER.set("bob");
    try { /* handle request */ }
    finally { CURRENT_USER.remove(); }                         // always clean up
}).get();
pool.submit(() -> System.out.println("after remove(), user = " + CURRENT_USER.get())).get();
// after remove(), user = null

Inside, each thread has a ThreadLocalMap whose keys are weak references to the ThreadLocal but whose values are strong. The value lives as long as the thread — forever, in a pool.

The tools

ToolWhat it doesExample
jpsList running JVMs and their process idsjps -l
jcmdThe Swiss army knife: send any diagnostic command to a JVMjcmd <pid> GC.class_histogram, GC.heap_dump, Thread.print, VM.flags, JFR.start
jstatGC statistics sampled over timejstat -gcutil <pid> 1000
jmapHeap histogram and heap dumps (older; jcmd covers it)jmap -dump:live,format=b,file=heap.hprof <pid>
jstackThread dump; detects deadlocksjstack <pid>
Eclipse MAT / VisualVMOpen a .hprof heap dump: dominator tree, "leak suspects", paths to GC roots. VisualVM also shows live CPU/heap/threadsGUI
JFR (Java Flight Recorder) + JDK Mission ControlLow-overhead (~1%) always-on recording: allocations, locks, GC, hot methods, I/O. Free since Java 11java -XX:StartFlightRecording=duration=60s,filename=app.jfr
async-profilerSampling CPU/allocation/lock profiler without safepoint bias; outputs flame graphsasprof -d 30 -f flame.html <pid>

Investigating a leak step by step

// The leak: a static cache that only grows
static final Map<String, Session> SESSIONS = new HashMap<>();
for (int i = 0; ; i++) {
    SESSIONS.put("s" + i, new Session("s" + i, new byte[10_000]));   // added on login, never removed
}
1. CONFIRM heap after each GC keeps rising (sawtooth whose floor climbs)? jstat -gcutil / GC log / metrics 2. CAPTURE heap dump: jcmd <pid> GC.heap_dump /tmp/heap.hprof (or it is written automatically with -XX:+HeapDumpOnOutOfMemoryError) 3. COMPARE class histogram twice, minutes apart: which class count keeps growing? 4. ANALYZE open the dump in Eclipse MAT: Dominator tree / Leak Suspects -> biggest retained size -> "Path to GC roots" shows WHO holds it 5. FIX remove the reference (evict, unregister, remove(), close); add a bound 6. VERIFY re-run the load test: the post-GC floor stays flat

Real output from running the program above with -Xmx256m (3 seconds in):

$ jcmd <pid> GC.class_histogram
 num     #instances         #bytes  class name (module)
-------------------------------------------------------
   1:         19639       59593328  [B (java.base@21.0.7)          <- byte[] payloads
   2:           697         782256  [Ljdk.internal.vm.FillerElement; (java.base@21.0.7)
   3:         13657         327768  java.lang.String (java.base@21.0.7)
   4:          6920         221440  java.util.HashMap$Node (java.base@21.0.7)

$ jstat -gcutil <pid> 1000 3
  S0     S1     E      O      M     CCS    YGC     YGCT     FGC    FGCT     CGC    CGCT       GCT
     -      -  21.74  55.04  48.98   7.27      3     0.016     1     0.008     0     0.000     0.023
     - 100.00  12.50  64.49  48.98   7.27      4     0.019     1     0.008     2     0.000     0.027
     - 100.00  53.85  74.71  48.98   7.27      5     0.023     1     0.008     2     0.000     0.031

The O (old generation % used) column climbs 55 → 64 → 75 even though young GCs keep running: long-lived data is growing. Left alone, the run ended in OutOfMemoryError and, with -XX:+HeapDumpOnOutOfMemoryError, wrote a 270 MB .hprof. In MAT, the dominator tree would show one HashMap retaining nearly all of it, and "path to GC roots" leads to LeakDemo.SESSIONS. Note: GC.class_histogram forces a full GC (the FGC column shows 1), so be careful running it on a busy production box; heap dumps also pause the JVM and can be as large as the heap.

Where the time usually goes

SymptomLook atTypical fix
High CPUCPU flame graph (async-profiler / JFR "hot methods")Better algorithm, avoid regex/reflection in hot paths, cache results
Low CPU but slowThread dumps: threads WAITING on I/O, pools, locksBigger/separate pools, timeouts, async or virtual threads, fix N+1 queries
Latency spikesGC log pauses, JFR GC eventsLess allocation, right heap size, G1 pause target or ZGC
Lock contentionJFR "Java monitor blocked", async-profiler -e lockSmaller critical sections, concurrent collections, LongAdder
Memory grows until OOMHeap dump, MATThe leak steps above

JIT warm-up

The JVM starts by interpreting bytecode. Methods that run often ("hot") are compiled to machine code by the JIT (just-in-time) compiler — first quickly by C1, then with heavy optimization by C2 (inlining, escape analysis, loop unrolling), a scheme called tiered compilation. So a Java program gets faster after it has been running for a while.

for (int round = 1; round <= 5; round++) {
    long t0 = System.nanoTime();
    sink += work(2_000_000);                        // same work every round
    System.out.println("round " + round + ": " + (System.nanoTime() - t0) / 1000 + " us");
}
// Verified run (numbers vary by machine and run):
// round 1: 5573 us
// round 2: 4504 us
// round 3: 4002 us
// round 4: 3253 us
// round 5: 3241 us
// With -Xint (JIT disabled): about 28000 us every round

Consequences: benchmark numbers from a cold JVM are wrong; the first requests after a deploy are slower (warm the service up before routing traffic); and short-lived processes never get fully optimized (Java 21 options: CDS archives for start-up, GraalVM native image; Project Leyden continues this work).

Benchmarking correctly with JMH

A hand-written timing loop like the one above is misleading: no warm-up control, the JIT can remove work whose result is unused (dead-code elimination), it can fold constants, and GC pauses land at random. JMH (Java Microbenchmark Harness, from the OpenJDK team) handles all of this.

// Needs the org.openjdk.jmh:jmh-core and jmh-generator-annprocess dependencies
@BenchmarkMode(Mode.AverageTime)
@OutputTimeUnit(TimeUnit.NANOSECONDS)
@Warmup(iterations = 5, time = 1)        // let the JIT compile first
@Measurement(iterations = 5, time = 1)
@Fork(2)                                 // fresh JVMs, so one run's JIT choices don't leak into another
@State(Scope.Thread)
public class ConcatBench {
    @Param({"10", "1000"}) int n;

    @Benchmark
    public String plusInLoop() {
        String s = "";
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) s += i;
        return s;                        // RETURN the result so it can't be eliminated
    }

    @Benchmark
    public void builder(Blackhole bh) {
        StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder();
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) sb.append(i);
        bh.consume(sb.toString());       // or sink it into a Blackhole
    }
}

(This snippet is not in the verified set because it needs the JMH library; it follows the standard JMH API.) Before micro-optimizing anything, profile first (JFR or async-profiler) to find where time really goes — it is usually I/O, database queries, locks or allocation, not the loop you suspect.

Interview angle: "Can Java leak memory with a GC?" — yes: reachable but unneeded objects; give two causes (static map, ThreadLocal in a pool). "How would you debug an OOM in production?" — the six steps above: confirm with GC logs, heap dump, MAT dominator tree, path to GC roots. "Service is slow — what do you do?" — measure: JFR/async-profiler flame graph, thread dumps for lock contention, GC logs for pauses. "Why is the first request slow?" — class loading plus JIT warm-up. "How do you benchmark in Java?" — JMH, never a bare System.nanoTime loop.
Part 7 — Modern & Practical Java

39. Modern Java: 8 to 21

In plain words

Java changes every six months, but most companies only run the LTS (Long-Term Support) versions: 8 (2014), 11 (2018), 17 (2021) and 21 (2023). Each LTS bundles everything added since the last one. Knowing which feature came in which version tells an interviewer you have actually kept up, and it tells you what you may use on a given project.

The big story: Java 8 made Java functional (lambdas, streams). Java 11–17 made it less wordy (var, records, text blocks, switch expressions). Java 21 made it data-oriented and massively concurrent (pattern matching everywhere, virtual threads).

Like a car model that gets a facelift every year: the engine you learned on still works, but the newer model has cruise control, a reversing camera and better mileage. If you only know the 2014 model, you drive the new one badly.

The feature timeline

VersionFeatureWhat it gives you
8Lambdas, method references, functional interfacesPass behaviour as a value: list.sort((a, b) -> ...)
8Streams APIDeclarative pipelines: filter, map, collect
8Optional, java.time, default methodsExplicit "maybe absent"; a sane date API; interfaces that can evolve
9List.of, Set.of, Map.of, modules (JPMS)Immutable collection literals
10varLocal variable type inference
11 (LTS)HttpClient, String.strip/isBlank/lines/repeat, Files.readString, run java File.java directlyModern HTTP/2 client in the JDK
14Switch expressions (->, yield), helpful NullPointerException messagesSwitch returns a value, no fall-through
15Text blocks """Multi-line strings for JSON, SQL, HTML
16Records, pattern matching for instanceof, Stream.toList()Data classes in one line; no more cast after instanceof
17 (LTS)Sealed classes and interfacesClosed hierarchies the compiler knows about
21 (LTS)Pattern matching for switch, record patterns, sequenced collections, virtual threadsType-safe data dispatch; getFirst/getLast/reversed; millions of cheap threads

Java 8 — lambdas, streams, Optional

List<String> names = List.of("anna", "bob", "carla", "dan");
List<String> longUpper = names.stream()
        .filter(n -> n.length() > 3)       // lambda
        .map(String::toUpperCase)           // method reference
        .toList();                          // [ANNA, CARLA]   (toList() is Java 16)

Optional<String> firstD = names.stream().filter(n -> n.startsWith("d")).findFirst();
firstD.map(String::length).orElse(0);       // 3

Java 10/11 — var, String helpers, HttpClient

var map = new HashMap<String, List<Integer>>();     // type inferred: HashMap<String, List<Integer>>
map.computeIfAbsent("a", k -> new ArrayList<>()).add(1);   // {a=[1]}
for (var e : map.entrySet()) System.out.println(e.getKey() + "->" + e.getValue());  // a->[1]

"  hi  ".strip();          // "hi"   (Unicode-aware, unlike trim)
" ".isBlank();             // true
"ab".repeat(3);            // "ababab"
"a\nb\nc".lines().count(); // 3

HttpClient client = HttpClient.newBuilder().connectTimeout(Duration.ofSeconds(5)).build();
HttpRequest req = HttpRequest.newBuilder(URI.create("http://127.0.0.1:" + port + "/hello")).GET().build();
HttpResponse<String> resp = client.send(req, HttpResponse.BodyHandlers.ofString());
System.out.println(resp.statusCode() + " " + resp.body());   // 200 {"msg":"hi"}

// Non-blocking version returns a CompletableFuture
String body = client.sendAsync(req, HttpResponse.BodyHandlers.ofString())
                    .thenApply(HttpResponse::body).join();

var is only for local variables with an initializer (not fields, parameters or return types). It is still static typing — the compiler fixes the type once. Use it when the right side already says the type (var list = new ArrayList<String>()); avoid it when it hides meaning (var x = service.process()).

Java 14 — switch expressions

int letters = switch (day) {
    case MON, FRI, SUN -> 6;          // several labels, no break needed
    case TUE -> 7;
    case THU, SAT -> 8;
    case WED -> {
        int n = "WEDNESDAY".length();
        yield n;                        // yield returns a value from a block
    }
};                                      // SAT -> 8

Arrow cases never fall through. For an enum, the compiler checks that every constant is covered, so no default is needed — and adding a new constant later becomes a compile error instead of a silent bug.

Java 15 — text blocks

String json = """
        {
          "name": "Ann",
          "age": 30
        }
        """;               // indentation up to the closing """ is stripped; 4 lines

Java 16 — records and instanceof patterns

record Point(int x, int y) {
    Point {                                   // compact constructor: validation only
        if (x < 0 || y < 0) throw new IllegalArgumentException("negative");
    }
    double dist() { return Math.sqrt(x * x + y * y); }
}
Point p = new Point(3, 4);
p + " " + p.x() + " " + p.dist() + " " + p.equals(new Point(3, 4));
// Point[x=3, y=4] 3 5.0 true   -- toString, accessors, equals/hashCode generated

Object o = "hello";
if (o instanceof String s && s.length() > 3) {   // s is bound only when the test succeeds
    System.out.println(s.toUpperCase());          // HELLO
}

Java 17 + 21 — sealed types, switch patterns, record patterns

sealed interface Shape permits Circle, Square, Rect {}
record Circle(double r) implements Shape {}
record Square(double side) implements Shape {}
record Rect(double w, double h) implements Shape {}

static double area(Shape s) {
    return switch (s) {                                   // exhaustive: no default needed
        case Circle c -> Math.PI * c.r() * c.r();          // type pattern
        case Square(double side) -> side * side;           // record pattern (deconstruction)
        case Rect(double w, double h) when w == h -> w * w; // guard with "when"
        case Rect(double w, double h) -> w * h;
    };
}
// Circle(1) 3.14, Square(2) 4.00, Rect(2,3) 6.00, Rect(3,3) 9.00

static String describe(Object o) {
    return switch (o) {
        case null -> "null!";                              // null can be a case now
        case Integer i when i > 100 -> "big int " + i;
        case Integer i -> "int " + i;
        case String s -> "string of length " + s.length();
        case Point(int x, int y) -> "point at " + x + "," + y;
        default -> "something else";
    };
}
// 42 -> int 42, 500 -> big int 500, "abc" -> string of length 3,
// Point(1,2) -> point at 1,2, null -> null!, 2.5 -> something else

if (obj instanceof Point(int x, int y)) System.out.println(x + y);   // 15 for Point(7, 8)

Order matters: a more specific case (Integer i when i > 100) must come before the general one, or the compiler reports it as dominated. Sealed + records + switch patterns together are called data-oriented programming: the data is plain, and the operations live in exhaustive switches the compiler checks.

Java 21 — sequenced collections

List<Integer> list = new ArrayList<>(List.of(1, 2, 3));
list.getFirst() + " " + list.getLast() + " " + list.reversed();   // 1 3 [3, 2, 1]

LinkedHashMap<String, Integer> lhm = new LinkedHashMap<>();
lhm.put("b", 2); lhm.put("a", 1); lhm.putFirst("z", 0);
lhm + " " + lhm.firstEntry() + " " + lhm.lastEntry();             // {z=0, b=2, a=1} z=0 a=1

LinkedHashSet<String> lhs = new LinkedHashSet<>(List.of("x", "y"));
lhs.addFirst("w");
lhs + " " + lhs.reversed();                                        // [w, x, y] [y, x, w]

New interfaces SequencedCollection, SequencedSet and SequencedMap give every ordered collection the same first/last/reversed methods. Before 21 you wrote list.get(list.size() - 1) or iterated a whole LinkedHashSet to find its last element. reversed() is a view, not a copy.

Java 21 — virtual threads

AtomicInteger done = new AtomicInteger();
try (var exec = Executors.newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor()) {
    IntStream.range(0, 10_000).forEach(i -> exec.submit(() -> {
        Thread.sleep(Duration.ofMillis(100));   // blocking is cheap on a virtual thread
        done.incrementAndGet();
        return i;
    }));
}   // close() waits for every task
// 10000 tasks finished in well under 2 seconds on a laptop

Thread.ofVirtual().start(() -> System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().isVirtual()));  // true

A virtual thread is a thread managed by the JVM, not the OS. When it blocks (sleep, socket read, JDBC call) the JVM unmounts it from its carrier OS thread, which then runs another virtual thread. 10,000 platform threads would reserve about 1 MB of stack each (~10 GB of address space); 10,000 virtual threads cost a few MB. Use them for I/O-bound work (web requests, DB calls) — they do not make CPU-bound work faster. See section 34 for pinning and deadlocks.

Interview angle: "What changed between Java 8 and 21?" Answer by theme, not a list: less boilerplate (var, records, text blocks), safer modelling (sealed types, exhaustive switch, pattern matching), better APIs (HttpClient, sequenced collections, Stream.toList), and a new concurrency model (virtual threads). Follow-up traps: Stream.toList() returns an unmodifiable list while collect(Collectors.toList()) gives a mutable ArrayList in practice; records are shallowly immutable (a List field can still be mutated unless you copy it with List.copyOf).

40. I/O, NIO & Serialization

In plain words

I/O (input/output) is moving data between your program and the outside world: files, network, console. Java has two families: byte streams (InputStream/OutputStream) for raw bytes like images, and character streams (Reader/Writer) for text, which convert bytes to characters using a charset such as UTF-8.

NIO ("new I/O", Java 1.4, and NIO.2 in Java 7) adds Path and Files — the modern, simple way to work with files — plus channels and buffers for high-performance I/O. Serialization turns an object into bytes so it can be saved or sent, and back again.

Byte streams are a water pipe: they carry whatever flows. Character streams are the same pipe with a translator at the end who turns the flow into words. A buffer is a bucket: instead of carrying one cup at a time to the tap, you fill a bucket and make one trip.

The class family

Byte streams (binary)Character streams (text)
Base classesInputStream, OutputStreamReader, Writer
FilesFileInputStream, FileOutputStreamFileReader, FileWriter
BufferingBufferedInputStream, BufferedOutputStreamBufferedReader (has readLine), BufferedWriter
BridgeInputStreamReader / OutputStreamWriter turn bytes into chars with a charset
Objects / primitivesObjectInputStream, DataInputStreamPrintWriter (formatted text)
Use forImages, zip, protocol bytes, serialized objectsText files, CSV, logs, console input

Byte vs character, with buffering

try (OutputStream out = new BufferedOutputStream(new FileOutputStream(bin.toFile()))) {
    out.write(new byte[]{72, 105});                  // raw bytes 'H' 'i'
}
try (InputStream in = new BufferedInputStream(new FileInputStream(bin.toFile()))) {
    int b;
    while ((b = in.read()) != -1) System.out.print(b + " ");   // 72 105
}

try (BufferedWriter w = Files.newBufferedWriter(txt, StandardCharsets.UTF_8)) {
    w.write("héllo"); w.newLine(); w.write("world"); w.newLine();
}
try (BufferedReader r = Files.newBufferedReader(txt, StandardCharsets.UTF_8)) {
    String line;
    while ((line = r.readLine()) != null) System.out.println("> " + line);
}
Files.size(txt);   // 13 bytes, not 12 -- 'é' takes 2 bytes in UTF-8

Why buffer? Each unbuffered read() can be a system call into the OS — thousands of times slower than reading from memory. A buffered stream reads 8 KB at once and serves later calls from that array. Always pass a charset for text: the platform default differed between machines before Java 18 (now UTF-8 by default), which caused "works on my laptop" bugs.

try-with-resources (Java 7)

Any object that implements AutoCloseable can go in the try (...) header. Java closes it automatically, in reverse order of opening, even when an exception is thrown. If close() also throws, that exception is suppressed and attached to the main one instead of hiding it.

static class Res implements AutoCloseable {
    final String n;
    Res(String n) { this.n = n; System.out.println("open " + n); }
    public void close() throws Exception { System.out.println("close " + n); throw new Exception("close failed " + n); }
}

try (Res a = new Res("A"); Res b = new Res("B")) {
    System.out.println("body");
    throw new IllegalStateException("body failed");
} catch (Exception e) {
    System.out.println("caught: " + e.getMessage());
    for (Throwable s : e.getSuppressed()) System.out.println("  suppressed: " + s.getMessage());
}
open A
open B
body
close B
close A
caught: body failed
  suppressed: close failed B
  suppressed: close failed A

Before Java 7 you wrote finally { if (in != null) in.close(); }, and an exception from close() would replace the real error. That is why try-with-resources is the only acceptable style today.

java.nio.file — Path and Files (the modern way)

Path f = dir.resolve("sub/list.txt");
Files.createDirectories(f.getParent());
Files.write(f, List.of("apple", "banana", "cherry"));          // lines, UTF-8
Files.writeString(f, "date\n", StandardOpenOption.APPEND);       // Java 11
List<String> all = Files.readAllLines(f);                       // [apple, banana, cherry, date]

try (Stream<String> lines = Files.lines(f)) {                   // lazy, MUST be closed
    lines.filter(s -> s.contains("an")).count();                // 1
}
try (Stream<Path> walk = Files.walk(dir)) {                     // recursive tree walk
    walk.filter(Files::isRegularFile)
        .map(p -> dir.relativize(p).toString())
        .sorted().forEach(System.out::println);
}   // data.bin  notes.txt  sub/list.txt  sub/one.txt

Path p = Path.of("/home/ann/docs/report.pdf");                  // Java 11 (Paths.get before)
p.getFileName() + " " + p.getParent() + " " + p.getNameCount(); // report.pdf /home/ann/docs 4
Path.of("/a/b").resolve("c/d.txt");                             // /a/b/c/d.txt
Path.of("/a/b/../c").normalize();                               // /a/c
MethodLoadsUse when
Files.readString / readAllLinesWhole file into memorySmall files (config, test fixtures)
Files.linesOne line at a time, lazilyBig files; must use try-with-resources or it leaks a file handle
Files.newBufferedReaderStreamingFull control, custom parsing loops
Files.walk / Files.listDirectory entries lazilyRecursive / single-level listing; also must be closed

NIO channels and buffers (in brief)

A channel (FileChannel, SocketChannel) is a two-way connection to a file or socket. You never read bytes from it directly — you read into a ByteBuffer. A buffer has a position (next slot), a limit (end of valid data) and a capacity. flip() switches it from writing mode to reading mode.

try (FileChannel fc = FileChannel.open(ch, StandardOpenOption.CREATE, StandardOpenOption.WRITE)) {
    ByteBuffer buf = ByteBuffer.allocate(16);
    buf.put("NIO!".getBytes());      // position=4 limit=16
    buf.flip();                      // position=0 limit=4  -- now ready to be drained
    fc.write(buf);
}
try (FileChannel fc = FileChannel.open(ch, StandardOpenOption.READ)) {
    ByteBuffer buf = ByteBuffer.allocate(16);
    fc.read(buf);
    buf.flip();
    System.out.println(StandardCharsets.UTF_8.decode(buf));   // NIO!
}

Where NIO matters: non-blocking sockets with a Selector let one thread watch thousands of connections (this is how Netty, and therefore Spring WebFlux, work); FileChannel.transferTo does zero-copy file-to-socket transfer; memory-mapped files (fc.map) let huge files be treated as memory. For everyday file work, Files is enough.

Serialization: Serializable, serialVersionUID, transient

static class User implements Serializable {       // marker interface: no methods
    @Serial private static final long serialVersionUID = 1L;
    String name;
    transient String password;                     // skipped when writing
    int age;
}

try (var oos = new ObjectOutputStream(Files.newOutputStream(ser))) {
    oos.writeObject(new User("ann", "secret", 30));
}
try (var ois = new ObjectInputStream(Files.newInputStream(ser))) {
    User u = (User) ois.readObject();
    System.out.println(u);                         // ann/null/30  -- transient came back as null
}
  • serialVersionUID is the class's version number. When reading, Java compares the stored UID with the current class's UID; if they differ you get InvalidClassException. If you don't declare one, Java computes it from the class shape, so adding a harmless method can break reading old data. Always declare it.
  • transient fields are not written; on reading they get default values (null, 0, false). Use it for passwords, caches, and non-serializable fields like a Thread or DB connection. static fields are never serialized either (they belong to the class).
  • Every field's type must also be Serializable, or you get NotSerializableException. During reading, the constructor of the first non-serializable superclass runs; the serializable class's own constructor does not.

Why Java serialization is discouraged

ProblemWhy it hurts
SecurityreadObject can build any class on the classpath from attacker-controlled bytes. "Gadget chains" in common libraries led to remote code execution bugs (e.g. the 2015 Apache Commons Collections exploits). Mark Reinhold, Java's chief architect, has called it a "horrible mistake" from 1997.
Fragile versioningPrivate fields become part of a public wire format; refactoring breaks old data
Java-onlyA Python or JS service cannot read it
Bypasses constructorsInvariants checked in constructors are skipped; singletons can be duplicated unless you add readResolve
Size and speedVerbose format, slow compared to JSON libraries or binary formats

Alternatives: JSON with Jackson (the default in Spring), Protocol Buffers or Avro for compact typed binary between services, and plain database rows for persistence. If you must deserialize Java objects, set an ObjectInputFilter (Java 9) allow-list.

// Jackson 2.x -- works directly with records
public record User(String name, int age,
                   @JsonProperty("e_mail") String email,   // rename in JSON
                   @JsonIgnore String password) {}         // never written

ObjectMapper mapper = new ObjectMapper();                  // create once and reuse: thread-safe, expensive
mapper.writeValueAsString(new User("ann", 30, "ann@x.com", "secret"));
// {"e_mail":"ann@x.com","name":"ann","age":30}

mapper.readValue("{\"name\":\"bob\",\"age\":25,\"e_mail\":\"b@x.com\"}", User.class);
// User[name=bob, age=25, email=b@x.com, password=null]
Interview angle: "Difference between transient and static for serialization?" Neither is written, but transient is a per-object field you chose to skip, while static never belonged to the object. "What if you don't define serialVersionUID?" Java generates one from the class structure, so any change may make old files unreadable. "Would you use Java serialization for a REST API?" No — JSON (Jackson) for readability and cross-language, Protobuf for performance.

41. Design Patterns in Java

In plain words

A design pattern is a named, proven solution to a problem that keeps coming up in object-oriented code. The classic catalogue (the "Gang of Four" book, 1994) groups them into three kinds: creational (how objects are made — Singleton, Builder, Factory), structural (how objects are combined — Decorator, Adapter), and behavioural (how objects talk — Strategy, Observer, Template Method).

Patterns are a shared vocabulary: saying "use a Strategy here" replaces ten minutes of explanation. In modern Java many patterns shrink to a few lines thanks to lambdas, enums and records.

Like recipes in a cookbook. "Béchamel" tells another cook exactly what you mean. You don't invent a new sauce every time — but you also don't put béchamel on everything.

PatternKindProblem it solvesSeen in the JDK / Spring
SingletonCreationalExactly one shared instanceRuntime.getRuntime(), Spring beans (singleton scope)
BuilderCreationalMany optional constructor parametersStringBuilder, HttpRequest.newBuilder(), Lombok @Builder
Factory MethodCreationalCaller should not know the concrete classList.of, Calendar.getInstance(), NumberFormat.getInstance()
StrategyBehaviouralSwap an algorithm at runtimeComparator, Spring PasswordEncoder
ObserverBehaviouralNotify many listeners of a changeSwing listeners, Spring ApplicationEvent, reactive streams
DecoratorStructuralAdd behaviour by wrappingBufferedReader, Collections.unmodifiableList
AdapterStructuralMake an incompatible interface fitArrays.asList, InputStreamReader
Template MethodBehaviouralFixed algorithm skeleton, variable stepsAbstractList, Spring JdbcTemplate

Singleton — five ways

// 1) Eager: created when the class loads. Simple and thread-safe.
final class EagerSingleton {
    private static final EagerSingleton INSTANCE = new EagerSingleton();
    private EagerSingleton() {}
    static EagerSingleton getInstance() { return INSTANCE; }
}

// 2) Lazy: NOT thread-safe -- two threads can both see null and create two objects.
final class LazySingleton {
    private static LazySingleton instance;
    private LazySingleton() {}
    static LazySingleton getInstance() {
        if (instance == null) instance = new LazySingleton();
        return instance;
    }
}

// 3) Double-checked locking: lazy and fast, but volatile is essential.
final class DclSingleton {
    private static volatile DclSingleton instance;
