    private DclSingleton() {}
    static DclSingleton getInstance() {
        DclSingleton local = instance;              // one volatile read on the fast path
        if (local == null) {                        // 1st check, no lock
            synchronized (DclSingleton.class) {
                local = instance;
                if (local == null)                  // 2nd check, under the lock
                    instance = local = new DclSingleton();
            }
        }
        return local;
    }
}

// 4) Holder idiom: lazy AND thread-safe with no locking code.
final class HolderSingleton {
    private HolderSingleton() { System.out.println("Holder instance created"); }
    private static class Holder { static final HolderSingleton INSTANCE = new HolderSingleton(); }
    static HolderSingleton getInstance() { return Holder.INSTANCE; }
}
// Calling another static method first prints nothing; the instance is created
// only on the first getInstance(), because Holder is loaded only then.

// 5) Enum: Joshua Bloch's recommended form. Serialization- and reflection-safe.
enum Registry {
    INSTANCE;
    private final Map<String, String> data = new HashMap<>();
    void put(String k, String v) { data.put(k, v); }
    String get(String k) { return data.get(k); }
}
Registry.INSTANCE.put("env", "prod");   // Registry.INSTANCE.get("env") -> prod

Why volatile in double-checked locking? instance = new DclSingleton() is three steps: allocate memory, run the constructor, assign the reference. Without volatile, the JIT or CPU may reorder so the reference is published before the constructor finishes; another thread passes the first null-check and uses a half-built object. volatile forbids that reordering (happens-before, section 29).

When to use: truly global, stateless or read-mostly things (config, a registry). Downsides: hidden global state, hard to mock in tests. In Spring apps you almost never write one — beans are singletons by default and are injected.

Builder

final class Pizza {
    private final String size; private final boolean cheese; private final List<String> toppings;
    private Pizza(Builder b) { size = b.size; cheese = b.cheese; toppings = List.copyOf(b.toppings); }
    static Builder builder(String size) { return new Builder(size); }   // required param up front

    static final class Builder {
        private final String size; private boolean cheese; private final List<String> toppings = new ArrayList<>();
        private Builder(String size) { this.size = Objects.requireNonNull(size); }
        Builder cheese() { cheese = true; return this; }            // return this -> chaining
        Builder topping(String t) { toppings.add(t); return this; }
        Pizza build() {
            if (toppings.size() > 3) throw new IllegalStateException("max 3 toppings");  // validate once
            return new Pizza(this);
        }
    }
}
Pizza.builder("large").cheese().topping("olives").topping("basil").build();
// Pizza[large, cheese=true, [olives, basil]]

When: four or more parameters, many optional. It beats "telescoping constructors" (Pizza(size), Pizza(size, cheese), …) and produces an immutable object. For two or three fields, a record is simpler.

Factory (simple factory and Factory Method)

interface Notifier { String send(String msg); }
record EmailNotifier() implements Notifier { public String send(String m) { return "EMAIL: " + m; } }
record SmsNotifier()   implements Notifier { public String send(String m) { return "SMS: " + m; } }

// Simple (static) factory: one place decides the concrete class
static Notifier create(String channel) {
    return switch (channel) {
        case "email" -> new EmailNotifier();
        case "sms"   -> new SmsNotifier();
        default      -> throw new IllegalArgumentException("unknown channel " + channel);
    };
}
create("email").send("hi");                 // EMAIL: hi

// GoF Factory Method: a subclass overrides the creation step
abstract class Dialog {
    abstract Notifier createNotifier();     // the factory method
    String alert(String m) { return createNotifier().send(m.toUpperCase()); }
}
class SmsDialog extends Dialog { Notifier createNotifier() { return new SmsNotifier(); } }
new SmsDialog().alert("server down");      // SMS: SERVER DOWN

When: the caller should depend on an interface, and the choice of class depends on input or configuration. Static factory methods also get good names (Optional.empty(), Duration.ofSeconds) and can return cached instances.

Strategy — with lambdas

interface DiscountStrategy { double apply(double price); }       // a functional interface
record Checkout(DiscountStrategy strategy) {
    double total(double price) { return strategy.apply(price); }
}
Map<String, DiscountStrategy> strategies = Map.of(
        "none",       p -> p,
        "tenPercent", p -> p * 0.9,
        "flat5",      p -> Math.max(0, p - 5));
new Checkout(strategies.get("tenPercent")).total(100);   // 90.0
// none -> 100.0, flat5 -> 95.0

When: you see a growing if/else or switch choosing between algorithms (pricing rules, sorting, compression). Before Java 8 each strategy was a class; now it is usually a lambda. Comparator is the Strategy everyone already uses.

Observer

final class StockTicker {
    private final List<Consumer<Double>> listeners = new ArrayList<>();
    void subscribe(Consumer<Double> l) { listeners.add(l); }
    void setPrice(double p) { listeners.forEach(l -> l.accept(p)); }   // push to all
}
StockTicker t = new StockTicker();
List<Double> history = new ArrayList<>();
t.subscribe(p -> System.out.println("display: " + p));
t.subscribe(history::add);
t.subscribe(p -> { if (p > 150) System.out.println("ALERT: price above 150"); });
t.setPrice(120.0); t.setPrice(155.5);
// display: 120.0 / display: 155.5 / ALERT: price above 150 ; history = [120.0, 155.5]

When: one change must reach many independent parts that the source should not know about. Pitfalls: forgetting to unsubscribe (a classic memory leak), and a slow listener blocking the others. For thread safety use CopyOnWriteArrayList for the listener list. At system scale, the same idea becomes pub/sub with Kafka.

Decorator — the java.io example

interface Coffee { double cost(); String desc(); }
record Espresso() implements Coffee { public double cost() { return 2.0; } public String desc() { return "espresso"; } }
record Milk(Coffee inner) implements Coffee {
    public double cost() { return inner.cost() + 0.5; } public String desc() { return inner.desc() + " + milk"; } }
record Syrup(Coffee inner) implements Coffee {
    public double cost() { return inner.cost() + 0.7; } public String desc() { return inner.desc() + " + syrup"; } }
Coffee c = new Syrup(new Milk(new Espresso()));
c.desc() + " = " + c.cost();                 // espresso + milk + syrup = 3.2

// java.io uses exactly this: FilterReader is the decorator base class
final class CountingReader extends FilterReader {
    int count;
    CountingReader(Reader in) { super(in); }
    @Override public int read(char[] b, int off, int len) throws IOException {
        int n = super.read(b, off, len); if (n > 0) count += n; return n; }
    // (single-char read() overridden the same way)
}
try (CountingReader cr = new CountingReader(new StringReader("line one\nline two\n"));
     BufferedReader br = new BufferedReader(cr)) {       // decorator on a decorator
    while (br.readLine() != null) { }
    System.out.println(cr.count);                        // 18
}

When: you need combinations of extra behaviours (buffering + decoding + counting) without a subclass for every combination. Decorator vs inheritance: inheritance fixes behaviour at compile time; decorators compose at runtime.

Adapter

final class LegacyThermometer { double readFahrenheit() { return 98.6; } }   // can't change it
interface CelsiusSensor { double celsius(); }                                // what our code wants

record ThermometerAdapter(LegacyThermometer legacy) implements CelsiusSensor {
    public double celsius() { return (legacy.readFahrenheit() - 32) * 5 / 9; }
}
new ThermometerAdapter(new LegacyThermometer()).celsius();   // 37.0

When: integrating a third-party or legacy API whose shape does not match your interface. Adapter changes the interface; Decorator keeps the interface and adds behaviour. JDK examples: Arrays.asList (array to List), InputStreamReader (byte stream to character stream).

Template Method

abstract class DataImporter {
    final List<String> run(String raw) {                 // final: the skeleton cannot change
        List<String> rows = parse(raw);
        rows = rows.stream().filter(this::isValid).toList();
        log(rows.size() + " rows imported");
        return rows;
    }
    protected abstract List<String> parse(String raw);   // required step
    protected boolean isValid(String row) { return !row.isBlank(); }   // optional "hook"
    private void log(String m) { System.out.println("[" + getClass().getSimpleName() + "] " + m); }
}
class CsvImporter extends DataImporter {
    protected List<String> parse(String raw) { return List.of(raw.split(",")); }
}
class LineImporter extends DataImporter {
    protected List<String> parse(String raw) { return raw.lines().toList(); }
    @Override protected boolean isValid(String row) { return !row.startsWith("#"); }
}
new CsvImporter().run("a,,b, ,c");        // [CsvImporter] 3 rows imported -> [a, b, c]
new LineImporter().run("x\n#comment\ny"); // [LineImporter] 2 rows imported -> [x, y]

When: several classes share the same steps in the same order but differ in one or two steps. Modern alternative: pass the varying step as a lambda (Strategy) — favour composition when there is only one varying step.

Interview angle: Expect "implement a thread-safe singleton" (give holder or enum, explain DCL + volatile), "how can a singleton be broken?" (reflection calling the private constructor, serialization creating a copy, multiple class loaders — enum resists the first two), and "which patterns does the JDK/Spring use?" (Decorator in java.io, Strategy in Comparator, Template Method in JdbcTemplate, Proxy in Spring AOP and @Transactional, Factory in BeanFactory). Don't force patterns: name the problem first, then the pattern.

42. SOLID in Java

In plain words

SOLID is five rules of thumb (named by Robert C. Martin) for writing classes that are easy to change without breaking other things:

Single Responsibility — a class has one reason to change. Open/Closed — add features by adding code, not by editing working code. Liskov Substitution — a subclass must be usable anywhere its parent is. Interface Segregation — many small interfaces beat one fat one. Dependency Inversion — depend on interfaces, not on concrete classes.

A well-organised kitchen. Each drawer holds one kind of tool (S). You add a new appliance by plugging it in, not by rewiring the house (O). Any brand of kettle works in the socket (L). The toaster doesn't come with a built-in fridge you have to pretend to use (I). Appliances depend on the standard socket, not on one specific power plant (D).

S — Single Responsibility

// BEFORE: calculation, formatting and persistence in one class -- three reasons to change
class InvoiceBefore {
    double amount;
    double total() { return amount * 1.2; }
    String toHtml() { return "<p>" + total() + "</p>"; }
    void saveToDb() { /* JDBC code */ }
}

// AFTER: one job each
record Invoice(double amount) { double total() { return amount * 1.2; } }
class InvoiceHtmlPrinter { String print(Invoice i) { return "<p>" + i.total() + "</p>"; } }
interface InvoiceRepository { void save(Invoice i); }
class InMemoryInvoiceRepository implements InvoiceRepository {
    final List<Invoice> saved = new ArrayList<>();
    public void save(Invoice i) { saved.add(i); }
}
// new InvoiceHtmlPrinter().print(new Invoice(100)) -> <p>120.0</p>

"One reason to change" means one actor: the tax team changes total(), the design team changes the HTML, the DBA changes storage. When they share a class, their edits collide. Smell: a class named ...Manager or ...Utils with 40 methods.

O — Open/Closed

// BEFORE: every new shape means editing (and re-testing) this method
static double areaBefore(Object shape) {
    if (shape instanceof double[] circle) return Math.PI * circle[0] * circle[0];
    // else if square ... else if triangle ... keeps growing
    throw new IllegalArgumentException();
}

// AFTER: open for extension (new class), closed for modification
interface Shape { double area(); }
record Circle(double r) implements Shape { public double area() { return Math.PI * r * r; } }
record Square(double s) implements Shape { public double area() { return s * s; } }
static double totalArea(List<Shape> shapes) { return shapes.stream().mapToDouble(Shape::area).sum(); }
// totalArea(List.of(new Circle(1), new Square(2))) -> 7.14

Tools for OCP in Java: interfaces + polymorphism, Strategy (a lambda per rule), and Spring injecting a List<Handler> of all beans of a type. Java 21 nuance: for a closed set of types (sealed), an exhaustive switch is fine — the compiler forces you to update every switch when a type is added.

L — Liskov Substitution

// BEFORE: Square "is a" Rectangle in maths, but not in code
class Rectangle {
    protected int w, h;
    void setWidth(int w) { this.w = w; }
    void setHeight(int h) { this.h = h; }
    int area() { return w * h; }
}
class SquareBad extends Rectangle {
    @Override void setWidth(int w)  { this.w = w; this.h = w; }
    @Override void setHeight(int h) { this.w = h; this.h = h; }
}
static int resizeAndMeasure(Rectangle r) { r.setWidth(5); r.setHeight(4); return r.area(); }
resizeAndMeasure(new Rectangle());   // 20
resizeAndMeasure(new SquareBad());   // 16  -- caller's expectation broken

// AFTER: no inheritance between them; immutable siblings under one interface
record Rect(int w, int h) implements Shape { public double area() { return w * h; } }
record Sq(int side)       implements Shape { public double area() { return side * side; } }

A subclass must not strengthen preconditions (accept less), weaken postconditions (promise less) or throw new unchecked surprises. JDK violation everyone knows: List.of(...) is a List, but add throws UnsupportedOperationException.

I — Interface Segregation

// BEFORE: fat interface forces fake implementations
interface WorkerBefore { void work(); void eat(); }
class RobotBefore implements WorkerBefore {
    public void work() {}
    public void eat() { throw new UnsupportedOperationException("robots don't eat"); }
}

// AFTER: small role interfaces; implement only what applies
interface Workable { String work(); }
interface Eatable  { String eat(); }
class Human implements Workable, Eatable {
    public String work() { return "human works"; } public String eat() { return "human eats"; } }
class Robot implements Workable { public String work() { return "robot works"; } }
List<Workable> workers = List.of(new Human(), new Robot());   // human works, robot works

JDK examples of good segregation: Comparable, AutoCloseable, Runnable — one method each. Spring Data lets you choose CrudRepository vs the smaller Repository for the same reason.

D — Dependency Inversion

// BEFORE: high-level policy creates a concrete low-level detail
class MySqlDatabase { String find(int id) { return "row " + id; } }
class UserServiceBefore {
    private final MySqlDatabase db = new MySqlDatabase();   // hard-wired, cannot test without MySQL
    String name(int id) { return db.find(id); }
}

// AFTER: both depend on an abstraction; the dependency is injected
interface UserStore { String find(int id); }
class UserService {
    private final UserStore store;
    UserService(UserStore store) { this.store = store; }      // constructor injection
    String name(int id) { return store.find(id).toUpperCase(); }
}
UserService svc = new UserService(id -> "fake user " + id);  // test double as a lambda
svc.name(7);                                                   // FAKE USER 7

Dependency Inversion is the principle; dependency Injection is the technique (passing the dependency in); Spring's IoC container is a tool that does the injection for you. Prefer constructor injection with final fields over field @Autowired: dependencies are explicit, the object is never half-built, and tests need no framework.

PrincipleSmell that breaks itTypical fix
SRPGod class, "and" in the class descriptionSplit by actor / reason to change
OCPGrowing if/else on type or on a string codePolymorphism, Strategy, sealed + exhaustive switch
LSPOverrides that throw or ignore input; instanceof checks on subtypesComposition instead of inheritance
ISPMethods throwing UnsupportedOperationExceptionSplit into role interfaces
DIPnew ConcreteService() inside business logicInterface + constructor injection
Interview angle: Have one concrete story per principle, ideally from your own code. The Rectangle/Square LSP example and the "fat interface" ISP example are the ones most often asked. Senior follow-up: "Can SOLID be overdone?" Yes — an interface for every class with only one implementation, or ten tiny classes for a 20-line feature, is its own smell. Apply it where change is likely.

43. Reflection & Annotations

In plain words

Reflection lets a running program look at itself: list a class's fields and methods, create objects, call methods and read private fields — all by name, at runtime, without knowing the class when the code was compiled.

An annotation (@Something) is a label you attach to a class, method, field or parameter. By itself it does nothing. Some tool reads it — the compiler (@Override), an annotation processor at build time (Lombok), or a framework using reflection at runtime (@Test, @Autowired).

Reflection is an X-ray of an object: you can see the bones even though the skin (private) is closed. Annotations are sticky notes on the bones that say "test this" or "inject here" — the X-ray reader decides what to do with them.

Getting and inspecting a Class

static class Account {
    private String owner;
    private double balance;
    public static int created = 0;
    public Account() { this("nobody", 0); }
    public Account(String owner, double balance) { this.owner = owner; this.balance = balance; created++; }
    public void deposit(double amt) { balance += amt; }
    private String secret() { return "secret of " + owner; }
    public double getBalance() { return balance; }
}

Class<?> c1 = Account.class;                          // 1) class literal
Class<?> c2 = new Account().getClass();               // 2) from an object
Class<?> c3 = Class.forName("ReflectDemo$Account");   // 3) from a String name (loads the class)
// c1 == c2, c2 == c3 -> true true  (one Class object per class per class loader)

Arrays.stream(c1.getDeclaredMethods())                  // ALL methods declared here, incl. private
      .map(m -> Modifier.toString(m.getModifiers()) + " " + m.getReturnType().getSimpleName() + " " + m.getName())
      .sorted().forEach(System.out::println);
// private String secret
// public double getBalance
// public void deposit
c1.getMethods().length;   // 11 -- only PUBLIC ones, but including those inherited from Object

getDeclaredX = everything declared in this class (any visibility, no inherited). getX = public only, including inherited. The order of returned methods is not specified — never rely on it.

Creating objects, calling methods, breaking into private

Constructor<?> ctor = c1.getConstructor(String.class, double.class);
Object acc = ctor.newInstance("ann", 100.0);
c1.getMethod("deposit", double.class).invoke(acc, 50.0);
c1.getMethod("getBalance").invoke(acc);          // 150.0

Field bal = c1.getDeclaredField("balance");
bal.setAccessible(true);                         // switch off the private check
bal.set(acc, 999.0);
bal.get(acc);                                    // 999.0

Method secret = c1.getDeclaredMethod("secret");
secret.setAccessible(true);
secret.invoke(acc);                              // secret of ann
c1.getField("created").get(null);                // static field: pass null as the object

Exceptions thrown inside an invoked method arrive wrapped in InvocationTargetException; call getCause() for the real one. Since Java 16–17 (strong encapsulation of JDK internals, JEP 396 and 403), setAccessible(true) on JDK internals such as String.value throws InaccessibleObjectException unless you open the module with --add-opens. Your own classes on the classpath are still accessible.

Annotations: retention and target

@RetentionKept untilExample
SOURCECompiler only, dropped from .class@Override, @SuppressWarnings, Lombok's @Getter
CLASS (default)In the .class file but not visible to reflectionBytecode tools
RUNTIMEReadable with reflection at runtime@Test, @Autowired, @Entity, @JsonProperty

@Target limits where the annotation may appear: TYPE, METHOD, FIELD, PARAMETER, CONSTRUCTOR, RECORD_COMPONENT and more. Other meta-annotations: @Inherited (subclasses inherit a class annotation), @Repeatable, @Documented. Annotation elements may only be primitives, String, Class, enums, other annotations, or arrays of those.

@Retention(RetentionPolicy.CLASS) @Target(ElementType.TYPE) @interface BuildOnly {}
@BuildOnly static class Account { ... }
Account.class.isAnnotationPresent(BuildOnly.class);   // false -- CLASS retention is invisible at runtime

Custom annotations processed at runtime — a validator and a mini JUnit

source: @MyTest void add() { ... } | javac keeps it in the .class file (RUNTIME retention) v runtime: Class.getDeclaredMethods() -> method.getAnnotation(MyTest.class) | not null? v method.setAccessible(true); method.invoke(instance) -> PASS / FAIL
@Retention(RetentionPolicy.RUNTIME) @Target(ElementType.FIELD)
@interface NotBlank { String message() default "must not be blank"; }

static class Form {
    @NotBlank String username;
    @NotBlank(message = "email required") String email;
    String nickname;                                     // not validated
}

static List<String> validate(Object obj) throws IllegalAccessException {
    List<String> errors = new ArrayList<>();
    for (Field f : obj.getClass().getDeclaredFields()) {
        NotBlank nb = f.getAnnotation(NotBlank.class);
        if (nb == null) continue;
        f.setAccessible(true);
        Object v = f.get(obj);
        if (v == null || v.toString().isBlank()) errors.add(f.getName() + ": " + nb.message());
    }
    return errors;
}
validate(new Form("ann", " ", null));   // [email: email required]

@Retention(RetentionPolicy.RUNTIME) @Target(ElementType.METHOD)
@interface MyTest { String name() default ""; boolean enabled() default true; }

static class CalculatorTests {
    @MyTest(name = "adds numbers") void add() { if (1 + 1 != 2) throw new AssertionError("1+1"); }
    @MyTest void divide() { int x = 1 / 0; }
    @MyTest(enabled = false) void slow() { throw new AssertionError("should not run"); }
    void helper() {}                                     // no annotation: ignored
}

static void runTests(Class<?> testClass) throws Exception {
    Object instance = testClass.getDeclaredConstructor().newInstance();
    Method[] methods = testClass.getDeclaredMethods();
    Arrays.sort(methods, Comparator.comparing(Method::getName));   // order is not guaranteed
    for (Method m : methods) {
        MyTest t = m.getAnnotation(MyTest.class);
        if (t == null) continue;
        String label = t.name().isEmpty() ? m.getName() : t.name();
        if (!t.enabled()) { System.out.println("SKIP " + label); continue; }
        try {
            m.setAccessible(true);
            m.invoke(instance);
            System.out.println("PASS " + label);
        } catch (InvocationTargetException e) {
            System.out.println("FAIL " + label + " -> " + e.getCause());
        }
    }
}
PASS adds numbers
FAIL divide -> java.lang.ArithmeticException: / by zero
SKIP slow

That loop is, in miniature, what JUnit does with @Test and @Disabled, and what Bean Validation (Hibernate Validator) does with @NotBlank.

How frameworks use reflection

FrameworkWhat it does with reflection
SpringScans the classpath for @Component, reads constructor parameter types, instantiates beans, injects @Autowired fields, builds proxies for @Transactional / @Cacheable (JDK dynamic proxies or CGLIB/ByteBuddy subclasses)
JUnit 5Finds @Test methods, creates a new test instance per method, invokes lifecycle methods
Hibernate / JPAReads @Entity/@Column, sets private fields from result sets (needs a no-arg constructor)
JacksonDiscovers getters/fields/record components and @JsonProperty to map JSON
MockitoGenerates subclasses at runtime to intercept calls on mocks

Cost and risks

  • Speed: a reflective call skips compile-time linking and needs argument boxing and access checks. In a quick 5-million-call loop the reflective version was measurably slower than a direct call (exact numbers vary by machine and JIT warm-up). Since Java 18, core reflection is built on method handles, which narrowed the gap. Frameworks cache Method objects and pay the cost mostly at startup.
  • No compile-time safety: a renamed method becomes a runtime NoSuchMethodException.
  • Breaks encapsulation: setAccessible can change private and even final instance fields (records and static final fields are protected), and can call a singleton's private constructor:
Constructor<Single> sc = Single.class.getDeclaredConstructor();
sc.setAccessible(true);
sc.newInstance() != Single.INSTANCE;               // true -- singleton broken

Constructor<?> ec = EnumSingle.class.getDeclaredConstructors()[0];
ec.setAccessible(true);
ec.newInstance("X", 1);   // IllegalArgumentException: Cannot reflectively create enum objects
  • Native images and startup: GraalVM native images need reflection declared ahead of time; this is why Micronaut and Quarkus favour build-time annotation processing over runtime reflection.
Interview angle: "How does @Autowired work?" — Spring reads bean definitions, uses reflection to find constructors/fields with the annotation, resolves a bean of the matching type, and sets it (calling setAccessible for private fields). "Difference between an annotation processor and runtime reflection?" — a processor (javax.annotation.processing.Processor, e.g. Lombok, MapStruct) runs inside javac and generates code; reflection reads RUNTIME annotations while the program runs. "Why is my custom annotation always null?" — you forgot @Retention(RUNTIME).

44. Build Tools & Testing: Maven, Gradle, JUnit

In plain words

A build tool downloads the libraries your project needs (dependencies), compiles the code, runs the tests and packages a JAR — the same way on every machine and in CI. Maven uses an XML file (pom.xml) and a fixed lifecycle; Gradle uses a Groovy or Kotlin script (build.gradle(.kts)) and a task graph.

JUnit 5 is the standard framework for writing and running tests. Mockito creates fake versions of dependencies (mocks) so a unit test checks one class in isolation.

The build tool is a factory's assembly line: parts arrive (dependencies), are machined (compiled), inspected (tested) and boxed (packaged). Mockito is a crash-test dummy: you test the car without risking a real person.

Maven vs Gradle

MavenGradle
Configpom.xml, declarative XMLbuild.gradle.kts (Kotlin) or build.gradle (Groovy), code
ModelFixed lifecycle of phases; plugins bind goals to phasesGraph of tasks with inputs/outputs
SpeedRebuilds more; slower on big projectsIncremental builds, build cache, daemon — usually faster
FlexibilityConventions, custom logic needs a pluginVery flexible (and easier to make messy)
Where you see itMost enterprise Spring projectsAndroid (required), many newer and large multi-module projects
BothSame layout (src/main/java, src/test/java), same Maven Central repository, wrapper scripts (mvnw, gradlew) pin the tool version

The Maven lifecycle

validate -> compile -> test -> package -> verify -> install -> deploy | | | | | src/main JUnit via target/app.jar copy to upload to a -> target Surefire ~/.m2 (local) remote repo Running a phase runs every phase before it: mvn package = validate + compile + test + package mvn clean install (clean is a separate lifecycle: delete target/) mvn -DskipTests package | mvn test -Dtest=PriceCalculatorTest | mvn dependency:tree
<project>
  <modelVersion>4.0.0</modelVersion>
  <groupId>com.example</groupId>          <!-- organisation -->
  <artifactId>shop</artifactId>            <!-- project name -->
  <version>1.0.0-SNAPSHOT</version>        <!-- SNAPSHOT = still changing -->

  <properties>
    <maven.compiler.release>21</maven.compiler.release>
  </properties>

  <dependencies>
    <dependency>
      <groupId>com.fasterxml.jackson.core</groupId>
      <artifactId>jackson-databind</artifactId>
      <version>2.17.2</version>              <!-- default scope: compile -->
    </dependency>
    <dependency>
      <groupId>org.junit.jupiter</groupId>
      <artifactId>junit-jupiter</artifactId>
      <version>5.11.0</version>
      <scope>test</scope>                   <!-- only on the test classpath -->
    </dependency>
    <dependency>
      <groupId>org.mockito</groupId>
      <artifactId>mockito-junit-jupiter</artifactId>
      <version>5.12.0</version>
      <scope>test</scope>
    </dependency>
  </dependencies>
</project>
// build.gradle.kts -- the same project in Gradle
plugins { java }
java { toolchain { languageVersion = JavaLanguageVersion.of(21) } }
repositories { mavenCentral() }
dependencies {
    implementation("com.fasterxml.jackson.core:jackson-databind:2.17.2")
    testImplementation("org.junit.jupiter:junit-jupiter:5.11.0")
    testImplementation("org.mockito:mockito-junit-jupiter:5.12.0")
    testRuntimeOnly("org.junit.platform:junit-platform-launcher")
}
tasks.test { useJUnitPlatform() }
// ./gradlew build   ./gradlew test --tests PriceCalculatorTest   ./gradlew dependencies
Maven scopeGradle configurationMeaning
compileimplementation / apiNeeded to compile and run
providedcompileOnlyCompile only; the server supplies it (Servlet API, Lombok)
runtimeruntimeOnlyRun only (a JDBC driver)
testtestImplementationTests only (JUnit, Mockito)

Transitive dependencies: Jackson pulls in jackson-core and jackson-annotations automatically. When two libraries need different versions of the same thing, Maven picks the nearest in the tree ("nearest wins"), Gradle picks the highest. Fix conflicts with <dependencyManagement> / a BOM (Spring Boot's parent does this for you) or <exclusions>.

JUnit 5 basics

class PriceCalculatorTest {
    private PriceCalculator calc;

    @BeforeAll static void beforeAll() { System.out.println("beforeAll (once)"); }
    @BeforeEach void setUp() { calc = new PriceCalculator(); }    // fresh object per test
    @AfterEach  void tearDown() { }
    @AfterAll  static void afterAll() { System.out.println("afterAll (once)"); }

    @Test
    @DisplayName("10% off 200 is 180")
    void applyDiscount_tenPercent_reducesPrice() {
        // Arrange
        double price = 200;
        // Act
        double result = calc.applyDiscount(price, 10);
        // Assert
        assertEquals(180.0, result, 0.0001);            // expected first, then actual, then delta
    }

    @Test
    void applyDiscount_negativePercent_throws() {
        IllegalArgumentException ex = assertThrows(IllegalArgumentException.class,
                () -> calc.applyDiscount(100, -5));
        assertEquals("percent must be 0..100", ex.getMessage());
    }

    @Test
    void applyDiscount_groupedAssertions() {
        assertAll(                                       // reports ALL failures, not just the first
                () -> assertEquals(100.0, calc.applyDiscount(100, 0)),
                () -> assertEquals(0.0, calc.applyDiscount(100, 100)),
                () -> assertTrue(calc.applyDiscount(50, 50) < 50));
    }

    @ParameterizedTest(name = "{0} with {1}% -> {2}")
    @CsvSource({ "100, 0, 100", "100, 25, 75", "80, 50, 40" })
    void applyDiscount_table(double price, int percent, double expected) {
        assertEquals(expected, calc.applyDiscount(price, percent), 0.0001);
    }

    @ParameterizedTest
    @ValueSource(ints = { -1, 101, 500 })
    void applyDiscount_outOfRange_throws(int percent) {
        assertThrows(IllegalArgumentException.class, () -> calc.applyDiscount(10, percent));
    }

    @Test @Disabled("demo of a skipped test")
    void notReadyYet() { fail(); }
}

Run with the JUnit Platform launcher together with the Mockito test below: 13 tests found, 12 succeeded, 1 skipped; beforeAll and afterAll printed once, @BeforeEach ran 9 times for this class (each parameterized row counts as a test; the disabled test gets no lifecycle calls).

JUnit 5JUnit 4 equivalentNotes
@BeforeEach / @AfterEach@Before / @AfterRun around every test
@BeforeAll / @AfterAll@BeforeClass / @AfterClassMust be static (unless @TestInstance(PER_CLASS))
@Disabled@IgnoreSkip with a reason
assertThrows@Test(expected = ...)Returns the exception to inspect
@ExtendWith@RunWithPlug in Mockito, Spring
@Nested, @Tag, @Timeout—Group tests, filter, fail slow tests

Mockito basics

public class OrderService {
    public interface OrderRepository { Optional<Order> findById(long id); void save(Order o); }
    public interface EmailSender { void send(String to, String body); }
    public record Order(long id, String email, double total, boolean paid) {}

    private final OrderRepository repo;
    private final EmailSender email;
    public OrderService(OrderRepository repo, EmailSender email) { this.repo = repo; this.email = email; }

    public Order markPaid(long id) {
        Order o = repo.findById(id).orElseThrow(() -> new IllegalArgumentException("no order " + id));
        Order paid = new Order(o.id(), o.email(), o.total(), true);
        repo.save(paid);
        email.send(o.email(), "Order " + id + " paid: " + o.total());
        return paid;
    }
}

@ExtendWith(MockitoExtension.class)
class OrderServiceTest {
    @Mock OrderService.OrderRepository repo;
    @Mock OrderService.EmailSender email;
    @InjectMocks OrderService service;                   // mocks passed to the constructor

    @Test
    void markPaid_existingOrder_savesAndSendsEmail() {
        when(repo.findById(42L)).thenReturn(Optional.of(new OrderService.Order(42, "a@x.com", 99.5, false)));

        OrderService.Order result = service.markPaid(42);

        assertTrue(result.paid());                                        // state
        ArgumentCaptor<OrderService.Order> saved = ArgumentCaptor.forClass(OrderService.Order.class);
        verify(repo).save(saved.capture());                              // interaction
        assertTrue(saved.getValue().paid());
        verify(email, times(1)).send(eq("a@x.com"), contains("99.5"));
        verifyNoMoreInteractions(email);
    }

    @Test
    void markPaid_missingOrder_throwsAndSendsNothing() {
        when(repo.findById(anyLong())).thenReturn(Optional.empty());
        assertThrows(IllegalArgumentException.class, () -> service.markPaid(7));
        verify(repo, never()).save(any());
        verifyNoInteractions(email);
    }

    @Test
    void manualMock_withoutAnnotations() {
        OrderService.EmailSender sender = mock(OrderService.EmailSender.class);
        doThrow(new RuntimeException("SMTP down")).when(sender).send(anyString(), anyString());  // void method
        RuntimeException ex = assertThrows(RuntimeException.class, () -> sender.send("x", "y"));
        assertEquals("SMTP down", ex.getMessage());
    }
}
  • An unstubbed mock method returns a default: null, 0, false, empty collection / empty Optional.
  • If one argument uses a matcher (eq, any), all must — mixing raw values and matchers throws InvalidUseOfMatchersException.
  • @Mock = all fake; @Spy = real object with some methods stubbed (use doReturn(..).when(spy).m() to avoid calling the real method while stubbing).
  • With MockitoExtension, stubs you never use fail the test (UnnecessaryStubbingException) — strict stubs keep tests honest.
  • Don't mock what you don't own (e.g. HttpClient) or value objects; wrap third-party APIs behind your own interface and mock that.

Test naming and structure

PracticeExample / reason
Name = method_condition_expectedResultmarkPaid_missingOrder_throwsAndSendsNothing reads like a spec when it fails
Arrange / Act / Assert (Given / When / Then)Three visible blocks; one Act per test
One behaviour per testSeveral asserts are fine if they check one outcome
Independent and repeatableNo order dependence, no shared mutable static state, no real clock (inject Clock)
FastUnit tests in milliseconds; real DB tests go in integration tests (Testcontainers)
Test behaviour, not implementationOver-verifying every call makes refactoring break tests
/\ E2E / UI tests -- few, slow, brittle / \ /----\ Integration tests -- DB, HTTP, Spring context (@SpringBootTest, Testcontainers) / \ /--------\ Unit tests -- many, fast, mocks for dependencies (JUnit + Mockito)
Interview angle: "Mock vs stub vs spy?" — a stub returns canned answers (when/thenReturn), a mock is additionally verified for interactions (verify), a spy wraps a real object. "Maven install vs deploy?" — local ~/.m2 vs remote repository. "How do you handle a dependency conflict?" — mvn dependency:tree, then pin the version with dependencyManagement or exclude it. "What is SNAPSHOT?" — a mutable in-development version that may be re-downloaded; releases are immutable.
Revision

Java Cheat Sheet

In plain words

Everything from the notes compressed into lookup tables for the last hour before an interview: which collection to pick, what each modifier keyword really does, the classic "difference between X and Y" pairs, and the cost of common operations.

The single index card you are allowed to bring into the exam — no explanations, just the facts you keep forgetting.

Which collection?

You need…UseAvoid
A list, mostly reads by index or appendsArrayListLinkedList, Vector
Stack or queue (FIFO/LIFO)ArrayDequeStack, LinkedList
Unique elements, fast lookup, no orderHashSetList.contains in a loop
Unique, insertion orderLinkedHashSet
Unique, sorted, range / floor / ceilingTreeSet
Key-value, fastestHashMapHashtable
Key-value, insertion or access order (LRU cache)LinkedHashMap (accessOrder=true, removeEldestEntry)
Key-value, sorted keysTreeMap
Enum keysEnumMap / EnumSetHashMap of enums
Smallest / largest firstPriorityQueueSorting after every insert
Thread-safe mapConcurrentHashMapCollections.synchronizedMap, Hashtable
Thread-safe list, many reads, rare writesCopyOnWriteArrayList
Producer-consumer hand-offArrayBlockingQueue / LinkedBlockingQueuewait/notify by hand
Constant dataList.of, Set.of, Map.of, List.copyOfArrays.asList (fixed-size but writable)

Keywords

KeywordOn a variable / fieldOn a methodOn a class
staticOne copy shared by the classCalled on the class; no this; hidden, not overriddenNested class without outer instance
finalAssigned once (reference, not object, is fixed)Cannot be overriddenCannot be extended (String)
abstract—No body; subclass must implementCannot be instantiated; may have constructors and state
synchronized—Locks this (or the Class for static) — mutual exclusion + visibility—
volatileReads/writes go to main memory; happens-before; no reordering. Not atomic for count++——
transientSkipped by Java serialization——
sealed / non-sealed——Only permits-listed subclasses allowed (17)
default—Interface method with a body (8)—
native / strictfp—Implemented in C via JNI / strict FP (redundant since 17)—

Difference between…

PairKey difference
== vs equalsSame object (reference) vs same content (as defined by the class)
String vs StringBuilder vs StringBufferImmutable vs mutable (not thread-safe) vs mutable synchronized (legacy)
ArrayList vs LinkedListArray: O(1) get, cache-friendly vs nodes: O(n) get, O(1) insert at a known node
HashMap vs HashtableUnsynchronized, one null key allowed vs synchronized legacy, no nulls
HashMap vs ConcurrentHashMapNot thread-safe vs per-bin locking/CAS, no null keys or values
HashMap vs TreeMap vs LinkedHashMapNo order O(1) vs sorted O(log n) vs insertion/access order O(1)
HashSet vs TreeSetHash O(1), no order, uses equals/hashCode vs red-black tree O(log n), sorted, uses compareTo
Comparable vs ComparatorNatural order inside the class (compareTo) vs external, many orders (compare)
Iterator fail-fast vs fail-safeThrows ConcurrentModificationException vs iterates a snapshot / weakly consistent view
Abstract class vs interfaceState + constructors, single inheritance vs no instance state, multiple inheritance of type
Overloading vs overridingSame name, different params, compile-time vs same signature in subclass, runtime dispatch
final vs finally vs finalizeModifier vs always-run block vs old GC hook (deprecated for removal since 18)
Checked vs unchecked exceptionMust catch or declare (IOException) vs RuntimeException subclasses, optional
throw vs throwsThrows an exception now vs declares a method may throw
Error vs ExceptionJVM problems you shouldn't catch (OutOfMemoryError) vs recoverable conditions
Stack vs heapPer-thread frames, locals, auto-freed vs shared objects, garbage collected
JDK vs JRE vs JVMTools + runtime vs runtime (libs + JVM) vs the engine that runs bytecode
sleep vs waitKeeps locks, Thread static vs releases the monitor, needs synchronized, woken by notify
Runnable vs CallableNo result, no checked exception vs returns a value, can throw
start() vs run()New thread vs plain method call on the current thread
synchronized vs ReentrantLockImplicit, block-scoped vs explicit: tryLock, timeout, fairness, multiple conditions
volatile vs AtomicIntegerVisibility only vs visibility + atomic read-modify-write (CAS)
Future vs CompletableFutureBlocking get() only vs chaining, combining, non-blocking callbacks
Platform vs virtual thread1:1 OS thread, ~1 MB stack vs JVM-scheduled, cheap, for blocking I/O (21)
map vs flatMapOne-to-one vs one-to-many then flatten
Intermediate vs terminal opLazy, returns a stream vs triggers the pipeline, returns a result
Optional.of vs ofNullableThrows on null vs empty on null
orElse vs orElseGetArgument always evaluated vs supplier called only when empty
Shallow vs deep copyCopies references vs copies the objects they point to
Static vs dynamic bindingResolved at compile time (static, private, final, overloads) vs at runtime (overrides)
record vs classFinal, immutable fields, generated equals/hashCode/toString vs everything by hand
Serializable vs ExternalizableAutomatic vs you write writeExternal/readExternal, needs public no-arg constructor
Maven vs GradleXML + fixed lifecycle vs script + task graph, incremental builds

Common method complexities

OperationCostOperationCost
ArrayList.get / setO(1)ArrayList.add (end)O(1) amortised
ArrayList.add(i, x) / remove(i)O(n)ArrayList.contains / indexOfO(n)
LinkedList.get(i)O(n)LinkedList.addFirst / removeFirstO(1)
ArrayDeque.push / pop / offer / pollO(1) amortisedPriorityQueue.offer / pollO(log n)
PriorityQueue.peekO(1)PriorityQueue.remove(x) / containsO(n)
HashMap.get / put / removeO(1) avg, O(log n) worst (treeified bin)TreeMap.get / put / floorKeyO(log n)
HashSet.containsO(1) avgTreeSet.first / ceilingO(log n)
String.charAt / lengthO(1)String.substring / concat / +O(n) (copies)
String.equals / hashCode (first call)O(n)StringBuilder.appendO(1) amortised
Arrays.sort(int[])O(n log n) dual-pivot quicksortCollections.sort / List.sortO(n log n) TimSort, stable
Arrays.binarySearchO(log n)Collections.reverse / shuffleO(n)
new PriorityQueue<>(collection)O(n) heapifyConcurrentHashMap.sizeO(counter cells); only an estimate while writes happen
Last-minute rules: override equals and hashCode together; program to interfaces (List, not ArrayList); prefer immutability; close resources with try-with-resources; never compare strings or boxed numbers with ==; use Integer.compare, not subtraction, in comparators.

Tricky Outputs — predict before you read

In plain words

Twelve short programs that interviewers love because the obvious answer is wrong. Cover the output, predict it, then check. Every output below was produced by running the code on Java 21.

Optical illusions for programmers: once you know why the lines only look bent, you never fall for it again.

1. String == vs equals

String s1 = "java";
String s2 = "java";
String s3 = new String("java");
String s4 = "ja" + "va";          // compile-time constant
String part = "ja";
String s5 = part + "va";          // built at runtime
System.out.println(s1 == s2);
System.out.println(s1 == s3);
System.out.println(s1 == s4);
System.out.println(s1 == s5);
System.out.println(s1 == s5.intern());
System.out.println(s1.equals(s3));
true
false
true
false
true
true

Why: literals and constant expressions share one pooled object; new and runtime concatenation make new objects; intern() returns the pooled one.

2. Integer cache

Integer a = 127, b = 127;
Integer c = 128, d = 128;
System.out.println(a == b);
System.out.println(c == d);
System.out.println(c.equals(d));
Long L = 127L;
System.out.println(L.equals(127));
true
false
true
false

Why: autoboxing uses Integer.valueOf, which caches −128..127; Long.equals(Integer) is false because the types differ.

3. char + int

System.out.println('a' + 'b');
System.out.println("" + 'a' + 'b');
System.out.println('a' + 1);
System.out.println((char) ('a' + 1));
System.out.println(1 + 2 + "3" + 4 + 5);
char ch = 'a';
ch += 1;
System.out.println(ch);
195
ab
98
b
3345
b

Why: chars are numbers (97 + 98) until a String appears on the left; + is evaluated left to right; += contains a hidden cast back to char.

4. finally overriding return

static int f() {
    try { return 1; }
    finally { return 2; }
}
static int g() {
    int x = 1;
    try { return x; }
    finally { x = 99; }
}
System.out.println(f());
System.out.println(g());
2
1

Why: a return in finally replaces the pending one (and swallows exceptions); but changing a local in finally doesn't affect a value already saved for return.

5. Static and instance initialisation order

class Parent {
    static { System.out.println("1 Parent static block"); }
    { System.out.println("3 Parent instance block"); }
    Parent() { System.out.println("4 Parent constructor"); }
}
class Child extends Parent {
    static { System.out.println("2 Child static block"); }
    { System.out.println("5 Child instance block"); }
    Child() { System.out.println("6 Child constructor"); }
}
new Child();
System.out.println("-- second object");
new Child();
1 Parent static block
2 Child static block
3 Parent instance block
4 Parent constructor
5 Child instance block
6 Child constructor
-- second object
3 Parent instance block
4 Parent constructor
5 Child instance block
6 Child constructor

Why: static blocks run once when each class is initialised (parent first); for every object, the parent is fully built (instance blocks, then constructor) before the child's.

6. Overload resolution with null

static void print(Object o) { System.out.println("Object"); }
static void print(String s) { System.out.println("String"); }
print(null);
Object o = "text";
print(o);
String
Object

Why: overloads are chosen at compile time by the most specific declared type — String is more specific than Object for null; the variable o is declared Object. (With print(Integer) added too, print(null) would not compile: ambiguous.)

7. i = i++

int i = 5;
i = i++;
System.out.println(i);
int j = 5;
j = j++ + ++j;
System.out.println(j);
5
12

Why: i++ yields the old value 5, increments i to 6, then the assignment writes 5 back; for j: 5 (j becomes 6) + 7 = 12.

8. Floating point

System.out.println(0.1 + 0.2);
System.out.println(0.1 + 0.2 == 0.3);
System.out.println(0.1f + 0.2f == 0.3f);
System.out.println(1.0 / 0);
System.out.println(0.0 / 0 == 0.0 / 0);
System.out.println(3 / 2);
System.out.println(new BigDecimal("0.1").add(new BigDecimal("0.2")));
0.30000000000000004
false
true
Infinity
false
1
0.3

Why: 0.1 has no exact binary form, and the float rounding happens to land on the same value while double doesn't; floating division by zero gives Infinity (integer division throws); NaN is never equal to anything, even itself; use BigDecimal from a String for money.

9. Integer overflow

System.out.println(Integer.MAX_VALUE + 1);
System.out.println(Math.abs(Integer.MIN_VALUE));
long wrong = 1000 * 1000 * 1000 * 10;
long right = 1000L * 1000 * 1000 * 10;
System.out.println(wrong + " " + right);
-2147483648
-2147483648
1410065408 10000000000

Why: int arithmetic silently wraps; |MIN_VALUE| doesn't fit in an int; the multiplication is done in int before widening to long — make the first operand long.

10. Ternary unboxing NullPointerException

Integer nothing = null;
boolean flag = false;
try {
    Integer r = flag ? 1 : nothing;
    System.out.println(r);
} catch (NullPointerException e) {
    System.out.println("NullPointerException");
}
Integer safe = flag ? Integer.valueOf(1) : nothing;
System.out.println(safe);
NullPointerException
null

Why: with an int and an Integer operand the ternary's type is int, so nothing is unboxed and throws; when both operands are Integer no unboxing happens.

11. HashSet with a mutable key

Set<List<Integer>> set = new HashSet<>();
List<Integer> key = new ArrayList<>(List.of(1, 2));
set.add(key);
key.add(3);                       // hashCode changes after insertion
System.out.println(set.contains(key));
System.out.println(set.size());
System.out.println(set.iterator().next().equals(key));
false
1
true

Why: the element is stored in the bucket for its old hash; contains looks in the bucket for the new hash and finds nothing — the element is "lost" but still there. Use immutable keys.

12. Method hiding (static) vs overriding

class A { static String who() { return "A"; } String me() { return "A"; } }
class B extends A { static String who() { return "B"; } @Override String me() { return "B"; } }
A x = new B();
System.out.println(x.me());
System.out.println(x.who());      // legal but misleading: static call through an instance
System.out.println(B.who());
B
A
B

Why: instance methods are dispatched on the runtime object (B); static methods are bound at compile time to the declared type (A) — they are hidden, not overridden.

Pattern behind all twelve: most traps come from three facts — boxing and caching (1, 2, 10), compile-time decisions (1, 6, 9, 12), and evaluation order (3, 4, 5, 7). When you see a trick question, ask: "what is decided at compile time here, and what is actually stored?"

Learning Path

In plain words

An ordered route from "I can write a loop" to "I can design, debug and tune a Java service in production". Each step lists what to learn (with the sections above), what to be able to explain in an interview, and a project that forces you to use it.

Like learning to drive: first the pedals (syntax), then the rules of the road (OOP, collections), then motorways (concurrency), then what is under the bonnet (JVM). Skipping steps works until the first breakdown.

Basics -> OOP -> Core APIs -> Functional -> Testing & Build -> Concurrency -> JVM -> Modern Java -> Spring & Production (1-7) (8-16) (17-23) (24-27) (44) (28-34) (35-38) (39-43)
StepLearnYou can explain…Project idea
1. Language basicsJDK/JVM, types, wrappers, strings, arrays, control flow, pass-by-value (s1–s7)Why Java is pass-by-value; String immutability; int vs IntegerCommand-line calculator and a word-frequency counter reading a text file
2. OOPClasses, encapsulation, inheritance, polymorphism, interfaces, equals/hashCode, enums, records (s8–s16)Abstract class vs interface; equals/hashCode contract; composition over inheritanceLibrary management system: books, members, loans, fines
3. Core APIsExceptions, generics, collections, HashMap internals, comparators, Optional (s17–s23)How HashMap works; fail-fast iterators; checked vs unchecked; type erasureBuild your own LRUCache and a generic MyHashMap<K,V> with resizing
4. Functional JavaLambdas, streams, collectors, java.time (s24–s27)Lazy evaluation; map vs flatMap; groupingByCSV sales-report generator: group, sum, top-N with streams
5. Testing and buildMaven/Gradle, JUnit 5, Mockito (s44), SOLID (s42)Unit vs integration test; mock vs stub; dependency scopesAdd 90% test coverage to steps 2–4 projects; set up a GitHub Actions build
6. ConcurrencyThreads, synchronized, volatile, locks, atomics, executors, CompletableFuture, concurrent collections, virtual threads (s28–s34)Happens-before; deadlock conditions; thread pool sizing; why count++ is not atomicMulti-threaded web crawler with a bounded pool, then rewrite with virtual threads and compare
7. JVM internalsMemory areas, GC, class loading, profiling (s35–s38)Heap generations; G1 vs ZGC; how to find a memory leakCreate a leak on purpose, capture a heap dump, find it in Eclipse MAT or VisualVM; tune GC flags with JFR
8. Modern and practical JavaJava 8–21 features, I/O and NIO, design patterns, reflection and annotations (s39–s43)Records + sealed + pattern matching; Singleton safety; how @Autowired worksMini dependency-injection container using your own @Inject annotation and reflection
9. Frameworks and productionSpring Boot (REST, Data JPA, Security, validation), SQL and transactions, Docker, logging, metricsBean lifecycle; @Transactional proxies; N+1 queries; connection poolsURL shortener or task tracker REST API with PostgreSQL, Testcontainers tests, Docker image
10. Senior levelSystem design, messaging (Kafka), caching (Redis), resilience (timeouts, retries, circuit breakers), observability, performance tuningTrade-offs, failure modes, capacity estimates; reading a flame graphSplit the step-9 app into two services talking over Kafka; load-test with Gatling and fix the bottleneck

How to study each step

  • Read, then run. Type every example yourself and change one thing to see what breaks — that is how the tricky-output questions stop being tricky.
  • Explain it out loud in two minutes, as in an interview: definition, how it works inside, one pitfall, when you'd use it.
  • Read JDK source for the classes you use most: ArrayList, HashMap, String, ConcurrentHashMap. Your IDE opens it with one click.
  • Practise DSA in Java alongside steps 3–4 (the DSA notes on this site), 2–3 problems a day.
  • Keep one "big" project that grows through steps 5–10 instead of many throw-away ones; it becomes your interview story.
LevelWhat interviewers expect
Junior (0–2 years)Steps 1–5 solid: OOP, collections, exceptions, streams, writing tests, clean code, DSA basics
Mid (2–5 years)Plus steps 6 and 9: concurrency correctness, Spring internals, SQL and transactions, design patterns applied to real code
Senior (5+ years)Plus steps 7, 8 and 10: JVM tuning and debugging production issues, modern Java idioms, system design, mentoring and trade-off reasoning
Final tip: for every topic, be ready with the three answers interviewers always want — what it is in one sentence, how it works underneath, and when you would (or would not) use it, ideally with a story from your own project.
